15.2      Distance based LP Relaxation
We describe an LP relaxation based on distance/length variables that generalizes
the relaxation for 𝑠-𝑡 cut that we saw previously. For each edge 𝑒 there is a
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                                200

length variable 𝑥 𝑒 indicating whether 𝑒 is cut or not. We require that the length
of any path 𝑝 connecting 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑠 𝑗 , 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗, should be at least 1.

                                          𝑐𝑒 𝑥𝑒
                                      Í
                           min
                                    𝑒∈𝐸
                           s.t.
                                        𝑥 𝑒 ≥ 1 𝑝 ∈ 𝒫𝑠 𝑖 ,𝑠 𝑗 , 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗
                                  Í
                                  𝑒∈𝑝
                                        𝑥𝑒 ≥ 0                𝑒∈𝐸
    The preceding LP can be solved in polynomial time via the Ellipsoid method
since the separation oracle is the shortest path problem. Alternatively, one can
write a compact LP. We focus on rounding the LP and establishing its integrality
gap.

  BallCut(𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸), 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉 )

  1. Solve distance based LP relaxation to obtain solution 𝑥

  2. Let 𝑑 𝑥 be the metric induced on 𝑉 by edge lengths 𝑥

  3. Pick 𝜃 uniformly at random from (0, 1/2)
             𝑘
  4. Output ∪𝑖=1 𝛿(𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃)) where 𝐵(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃) is the ball of radius 𝜃 around 𝑠 𝑖



Lemma 15.2. The algorithm outputs a feasible solution.

Proof. Consider a terminal 𝑠 𝑖 . 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃) does not contain any other terminal
𝑠 𝑗 , 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖 since 𝜃 < 1/2 and 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑗 ) ≥ 1. Thus removing the edges 𝛿(𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃))
disconnects 𝑠 𝑖 from every other terminal. Since this holds for each 𝑠 𝑖 taking
the union of the edges ∪𝑖 𝛿(𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃)) disconnects each terminal from all other
terminals.                                                                             
    Consider the open balls of radius 1/2 around the terminals, that is, 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 1/2)
for 𝑖 ∈ [𝑘]. We observe that they are disjoint for if 𝑣 ∈ 𝐵(𝑠 𝑖 , 1/2) ∩ 𝐵(𝑠 𝑗 , 1/2)
with 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗 then 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑗 ) < 1 which would violate the LP constraint. Thus the
algorithm is essentially running an 𝑠-𝑡 cut type algorithm in the disjoint balls but
since we only have half the radius we lose a factor of 2. In fact, as we will see in
a remark later, the analysis holds even if each 𝑠 𝑖 chose its own 𝜃𝑖 independently
— the correlated choice is not exploited here — but a correlated choice is quite
useful when considering other cut problems including Directed Multiway Cut
later in the chapter.

Lemma 15.3. Let 𝑒 = (𝑢, 𝑣). P[𝑒 is cut] ≤ 2𝑥 𝑒 .
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                                 201

Proof. There are several cases to consider but the main one is the one where both
𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 1/2] for some 𝑠 𝑖 . Suppose this is the case. Then only 𝑠 𝑖 can cut the
edge (𝑢, 𝑣). It is easy to see that P[𝑒 is cut] = 2|𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑢) − 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑣)| since we pick
𝜃 uniformly at random from (0, 1/2). But |𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑢) − 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑣)| ≤ 𝑥 𝑒 by triangle
inequality and hence we obtain the desired claim.
    Now we consider the case when 𝑢 ∈ 𝐵(𝑠 𝑖 , 1/2) and 𝑣 ∈ 𝐵(𝑠 𝑗 , 1/2) where 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗.
Let 𝛼 = 1/2 − 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑢) and let 𝛽 = 1/2 − 𝑑(𝑠 𝑗 , 𝑣). We observe that 𝛼 + 𝛽 ≤ 𝑥 𝑒 for
otherwise 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑢) + 𝑥 𝑒 + 𝑑(𝑠 𝑗 , 𝑣) < 1. We see that 𝑒 is cut iff 𝜃 lies in the interval
[𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑢), 1/2) or it lies in the interval [𝑑(𝑠 𝑗 , 𝑣), 1/2). Thus 𝑒 is cut with probability
2 max(𝛼, 𝛽) ≤ 2(𝛼 + 𝛽) ≤ 2𝑥 𝑒 .
    There are two other cases. One is when both 𝑢, 𝑣 are outside every half-ball.
In this case the edge 𝑒 is not cut. The other is when 𝑢 ∈ 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 1/2) for some 𝑖
and 𝑣 is not inside any ball. The analysis here is similar to the second case and
we leave it as an exercise.                                                                 
   Thus the expected cost of the cut is at most 2 𝑒 𝑐 𝑒 𝑥 𝑒 ≤ 2 OPT𝐿𝑃 ≤ 2 OPT.
                                                           Í
One can improve and obtain a 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation by saving on one
terminal as we did in the preceding section.

Exercise 15.2. Modify the algorithm to obtain a 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation with
respect to the LP relaxation.

Exercise 15.3. Consider a variant of the algorithm where each 𝑠 𝑖 picks an
independent 𝜃𝑖 ∈ (0, 1/2); we output the cut ∪𝑖 𝛿(𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃𝑖 )). Prove that this also
yields a 2-approximation (can be improved to 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation).

Integrality gap: The analysis is tight as shown by the following integrality gap
example. Consider a star with center 𝑟 and 𝑘 leaves 𝑠 1 , 𝑠2 , . . . , 𝑠 𝑘 which are the
terminals. All edges have cost 1. Then it is easy to see that a feasible integral
solution consists of removing 𝑘 − 1 edges from the star. Hence OPT = 𝑘 − 1. On
the other hand, setting 𝑥 𝑒 = 1/2 for each edge is a feasible fractional solution of
cost 𝑘/2. Hence the integrality gap is 2(1 − 1/𝑘).


15.3     A Partitioning View and Geometric Relaxation
In this section we describe improved approximation algorithms for Multiway
Cut problem via a strengthened LP relaxation. It is useful to take another
perspective on the LP that we saw in the previous section and write it with
distance variables. For this purpose we can assume without loss of generality
that 𝐺 is a complete graph (we can add dummy edges of zero cost to achieve
this). For each unordered pair 𝑢𝑣 of distinct nodes we have a variable 𝑑𝑢𝑣 ; the
variable denotes the distance between 𝑢 and 𝑣 which is supposed to be 1 if 𝑢
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                              202

and 𝑣 are separated and 0 otherwise. The relaxation requires 𝑑 to be a metric
subject to the constraint that 𝑑 𝑠 𝑖 𝑠 𝑗 = 1 for each pair of terminals 𝑠 𝑖 𝑠 𝑗 . Triangle
inequality constraints ensure that 𝑑 is a metric.

                                Õ
                          min        𝑐 𝑢𝑣 𝑑𝑢𝑣
                                𝑢𝑣
                                     𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 𝑠 𝑗 ) = 1    𝑖≠𝑗
                                𝑑𝑢𝑣 + 𝑑𝑣𝑤       ≥ 𝑑𝑢𝑤    𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑤
                                         𝑑𝑢𝑣    ≥ 0    𝑢𝑣

    It is not hard to convince oneself that the two relaxations are equivalent in
terms of the optimum cost; we note that the two relaxations are very different
in the polyhedral sense. Syntactically it is sometimes more convenient to use
variable 𝑑𝑢,𝑣 for the ordered pair (𝑢, 𝑣) and impose symmetry explicitly in the
constraints 𝑑𝑢,𝑣 = 𝑑𝑣,𝑢 and then let 𝑑𝑢𝑣 stand for the common variable.
    We saw that the worst case integrality gap of the preceding relaxations
is 2(1 − 1/𝑘). Calinescu, Karloff and Rabani [53] were the first to obtain an
approximation ratio better than 2 for MultiwayCut, and they achieved this via
novel LP relaxation that has since been extensively studied. Until very recently
the current best upper bound on the integrality gap of this relaxation (henceforth
called the CKR relaxation) was 1.2965 [233]. A very recent result obtains a
1.2787-approximation [45]. The best known lower bound on the relaxation is
1.20016 − 𝜖 when 𝑘 → ∞ [40]. Moreover, it is known, that under the unique
games conjecture (UGC) [199], for every fixed 𝑘, the integrality gap of the CKR
relaxation is the worst-case hardness of the problem! We do not know the precise
worst-case integrality gap of the CKR relaxation for 𝑘 ≥ 4. For 𝑘 = 3 it is known
to be 12/11 [83, 168].
    In this notes we describe the CKR relaxation and a rounding scheme that
achieves a 1.5 approximation which was the bound initially shown in [53] (they
show (1.5 − 1/𝑘)). This analysis is given in the approximation text books. The
main purpose of this lecture notes is to highlight a different analysis which
is based on the work of Chekuri and Ene [69] on the Submodular Multiway
Partition (SubMP) problem which generalizes MultiwayCut; they proved that
the bound of 1.5 can be obtained for all symmetric functions. We believe that it
gives a different perspective and also eliminates some of the intricacies in the
standard analysis, at least as far as obtaining an approximation ratio of 1.5 is
concerned. Improving this bound requires more intricate and messy analysis
that takes the graph aspect into account.
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                         203

15.3.1 CKR Relaxation
The CKR relaxation is typically described as a geometric relaxation and this
is well-justified. However, there are two other “natural” ways to derive it and
we will discuss them later. The basic observation that leads to the relaxation
is the following. It is useful to view MultiwayCut in undirected graphs as a
partition problem rather than as an edge-removal problem. We can assume
without loss of generality that 𝐺 is connected. We observe that if 𝐸0 ⊆ 𝐸 is a
minimal feasible solution to disconnect the terminals, then 𝐺 − 𝐸0 has exactly 𝑘
connected components with exactly one terminal in each. Moreover, each edge
in 𝐸0 connects two of these components. Thus, we can reformulate MultiwayCut
in the following equivalent way. Find a partition 𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉𝑘 of 𝑉 such that
                                                        Í𝑘
𝑠 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉𝑖 for 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑘, where the objective is to min 21 𝑖=1 𝑐(𝛿(𝑉𝑖 )). Once we have
this partition based formulation, it is natural to use variables 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) for each
vertex 𝑢 and index 𝑖 ∈ [𝑘]; the variable indicates whether 𝑢 belongs to 𝑉𝑖 . We
then write the following constraints that model the requirement that each 𝑢
belongs exactly to one piece of the partition and that 𝑠 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉𝑖 .

                         𝑘
                         Õ
                               𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) = 1           𝑢∈𝑉
                         𝑖=1
                               𝑥(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑖) = 1        𝑖 ∈ [𝑘]
                               𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) ≥ 0           𝑢 ∈ 𝑉 , 𝑖 ∈ [𝑘]

    How do we model the objective? Consider an edge 𝑢𝑣 and assume all the
allocation variables are binary and satisfy the constraints. 𝑢𝑣 is cut iff there
exists an 𝑖 such that 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) ≠ 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖). But if there is such an 𝑖 then there will
be an 𝑖 0 ≠ 𝑖 such that 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖 0) ≠ 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖 0). Thus, one can see that 𝑢𝑣 is cut iff
Í𝑘
  𝑖=1 |𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) − 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖)| = 2. Hence the objective becomes

Geometric interpretation: Perhaps the most useful interpretation, as far as
MultiwayCut is considered, is the geometric interpretation. Consider the 𝑘
unit basis vectors e1 , e2 , . . . , ek in 𝑅 𝑘 (here ei is the vector with 1 in the 𝑖’th
coordinate and 0 in all other coordinates). The convex hull of these 𝑘 vectors
creates the 𝑘-simplex and it is easy to see that for each 𝑢 ∈ 𝑉, the vector
𝑥 𝑢 = (𝑥(𝑢, 1), 𝑥(𝑢, 2), . . . , 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑘)) lies in this convex hull. Note that the simplex
lies on a hyperplane of 𝑅 𝑘 , and hence is not full-dimensional. The relaxation
is embedding the terminals at the corners of the 𝑘-simplex, and mapping each
node 𝑢 to a point within the simplex. The distance between 𝑢 and 𝑣 is half the ℓ1
distances between the vectors 𝑥 𝑢 and 𝑥 𝑣 . The advanced rounding algorithms
for MultiwayCut via the CKR relaxation make very strong use of the geometric
view point, as well as the special structure enjoyed by the objective function for
graphs, which decomposes in a nice fashion.
Interpretation as strengthening the distance relaxation: Another nice interpre-
tation of the CKR relaxation (also from [53]) is as follows. We already discussed
the basic distance LP and showed that it has a worst case integrality gap of
2(1 − 1/𝑘). It is natural to consider strengthening the LP by adding inequalities
that are valid for the integer program. Consider a node 𝑢. If we take the partition
view then we note that 𝑢 must belong to one of the components, which implies
that it must have a distance 0 to the terminal whose component it belongs to,
and distance 1 to the other 𝑘 − 1 terminals. Thus the following inequalites are
valid for the integer program:
                                         𝑘
                                         Õ
                                               𝑑(𝑢𝑠 𝑖 ) = 𝑘 − 1   𝑢∈𝑉
                                         𝑖=1
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                                   205

   We can also add another set of valid inequalities as follows. Consider distinct
nodes 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉. Let 𝑇 ⊂ [𝑘] correspond to a subset of terminals. Then we claim
that                              Õ
                         𝑑(𝑢𝑣) ≥      [𝑑(𝑢𝑠 𝑖 ) − 𝑑(𝑣𝑠 𝑖 )]
                                           𝑖∈𝑇

is a valid constraint. We leave it as an exercise to check this inequality’s validity
when the distances are induced by partitions. Note that there are an exponential
number of inequalities of the second type since we have to write it for each
edge 𝑢𝑣 and each 𝑇 ⊂ [𝑘]. It is possible to separate over these inequalities
in polynomial time (see [53] or think about it). Interestingly, if we add the
preceding two sets of valid inequalities to the basic distance LP we get the CKR
relaxation!
Interpretation via Lovász-extension of submodular functions: Recall that the
objective function that came out naturally from the allocation variables 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖)
was the following.

                                                  𝑘
                                 Õ           1Õ
                           min          𝑐 𝑢𝑣    |𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) − 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖)|
                                             2
                                 𝑢𝑣∈𝐸            𝑖=1

We had to add additional variables to model this objective as a linear function.
                                                                  Í𝑘
However, one can observe that the function 𝑢𝑣∈𝐸 𝑐 𝑢𝑣 21 𝑖=1            |𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) − 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖)| is
                                                      Í
convex in the variables (in fact it is a piece-wise linear convex function). Thus, we
can model our problem as a convex optimization problem with linear constraints.
Why is the objective convex? Is there a more general phenomenon going on
here? Indeed, that is the case. We can cast MultiwayCut as a special case of the
following more general problem. Let 𝑓 : 2𝑉 → ℝ+ be a submodular function and
let {𝑠 1 , 𝑠2 , . . . , 𝑠 𝑘 } ⊆ 𝑉. In SubMP the goal is to partition 𝑉 into 𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉𝑘
such that 𝑠 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉𝑖 for 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑘, and to minimize 𝑖 𝑓 (𝑉𝑖 ). Note that MultiwayCut
                                                        Í
is the special case of SubMP where 𝑓 is the cut function of a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸).
In fact the cut function is not only submodular but also symmetric, and hence
MultiwayCut is a special case of SymSubMP (Symmetric Submodular Multiway
Partition) which is obtained by restricting 𝑓 to be a symmetric submodular
function. It is natural to write a relaxation for SubMP via the same allocation
variables 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) as we did for MultiwayCut. The main difficulty is with the
objective function 𝑓 . How do we deal with a generic submodular function that is
only available as a value oracle? It turns out that there are useful ways to extend a
submodular set function 𝑓 over a discrete ground set 𝑉 to a continuous function
𝑔 over the entire real cube [0, 1]|𝑉 | . Lovász described a particular extension 𝑓ˆ,
named after him, that turns out to be convex and useful in the sense that it can
be evaluated at any point in the real cube efficiently given 𝑓 as a value oracle.
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                                  206

One can thus write a convex relaxation for SubMP (and hence also SymSubMP)
via the Lovász-extension. When specialized to MultiwayCut this relaxation
turns out to be the same as the CKR relaxation! We refer the reader to [69] for
more details.

15.3.2    Rounding Algorithm and Analysis
In this section we show a rounding algorithm that gives a 1.5 approximation for
MultiwayCut. A slightly more careful analysis will yield a bound of (1.5 − 1/𝑘).
As we already mentioned better ratios are known. The algorithm and analysis
here is an adaptation of the algorithm and analysis from [69] for the SymSubMP
problem. The original algorithm and analysis from [53] can be be found in the
approximation text books. We will briefly discuss the original algorithm and its
connection to our algorithm later.
    The informal description is as follows. It picks a random 𝜃 ∈ [0, 1) and for
each terminal 𝑠 𝑖 we consider 𝐴 𝑖 to be ball of radius (1 − 𝜃) around 𝑠 𝑖 . We observe
that 𝐴 𝑖 = {𝑣 | 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) ≥ 𝜃} because
                                    𝑘
                                 1Õ
                    𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 𝑣) =      |𝑥(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑗) − 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑗)| = 1 − 𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖).
                                 2
                                   𝑗=1

Note that the sets 𝐴1 , 𝐴2 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘 can overlap. We say that 𝑣 is unallocated if
𝑣 ∈ 𝑉 \ (∪𝑖 𝐴 𝑖 ). Since we are seeking a partition we need to fix the overalapping
sets. We do this via an uncrossing operation. If 𝐴 𝑖 ∩ 𝐴 𝑗 ≠ ∅ we replace them with
either 𝐴 𝑖 − 𝐴 𝑗 , 𝐴 𝑗 or 𝐴 𝑖 , 𝐴 𝑗 − 𝐴 𝑖 . We will argue that this does not increase the cost.
This way of understanding the algorithm is based on [69]. The algorithm from
[53] avoids the overlap by picking a random permutation of the terminals, and
𝐴 𝑖 is defined to set of all vertices in the ball of radius (1 − 𝜃) around 𝑠 𝑖 that have
not already been allocated to a terminal that came earlier in the permutation.
The random permutation is implicity using the fact that sets can be uncrossed
without increasing the cost.
     The analysis hinges on understanding the expected cost of 𝛿(𝐴 𝑖 ), 𝑖 ∈ [𝑘] and
the expected cost of 𝛿(𝑈). The algorithm is formally described below.
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                          207



  RoundCKR(G, S)

  1. Let 𝑥¯ be an optimum solution to the CKR relaxation on 𝐺 with terminals 𝑆

  2. Pick 𝜃 uniformly at random from [0, 1)

  3. For 𝑖 = 1 to 𝑘 let 𝐴 𝑖 = {𝑣 | 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖) ≥ 𝜃}
                   𝑘
  4. Let 𝑈 = 𝑉 \ (∪𝑖=1 𝐴 𝑖 ) be the unallocated nodes

  // Uncross 𝐴1 , 𝐴2 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘 so that they are disjoint

  5. While there exist 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗 such that 𝐴 𝑖 ∩ 𝐴 𝑗 ≠ ∅ do

     A. If 𝑐(𝛿(𝐴 𝑖 )) + 𝑐(𝛿(𝐴 𝑗 − 𝐴 𝑖 )) ≤ 𝑐(𝛿(𝐴 𝑖 )) + 𝑐(𝛿(𝐴 𝑗 )) then
         1. 𝐴 𝑗 ← 𝐴 𝑗 − 𝐴 𝑖
     B. Else
         1. 𝐴 𝑖 ← 𝐴 𝑖 − 𝐴 𝑗

  6. Output the partition 𝐴1 ∪ 𝑈 , 𝐴2 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘


     Let OPT be the cost of the CKR relaxation. For the sake of the analysis we let
𝐴1 , 𝐴2 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘 denote the random sets obtained in the first randomized step of
the algorithm, and let 𝐴01 , . . . , 𝐴0𝑘 denote the sets after the uncrossing step. We
make some basic observations that are easy to verify. They show that the final
partition is indeed a valid solution to the problem.

   • For each 𝑖, 𝑠 𝑖 ∈ 𝐴 𝑖 , and 𝑠 𝑖 ∈ 𝐴0𝑖 .

   • ∪𝑖 𝐴 𝑖 = ∪𝑖 𝐴0𝑖 .

   • The uncrossing step terminates in 𝑂(𝑘 2 ) iterations. Why?

    The cost analysis is based on the following facts that we will prove.
                                     Í𝑘

    We now prove the desired properties. The analysis will be on an edge by

linearity of expectation for the overall bounds. Recall that 𝑧 𝑢𝑣,𝑖 = |𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖)− 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖)|
and let 𝑧 𝑢𝑣 = 𝑖 𝑧 𝑢𝑣,𝑖 be the ℓ1 distance between 𝑥 𝑢 and 𝑥 𝑣 .
               Í

Lemma 15.4. P[𝑢𝑣 ∈ 𝛿(𝐴 𝑖 )] = 𝑧 𝑢𝑣,𝑖 .
Proof. It is easy to see that 𝑢𝑣 ∈ 𝛿(𝐴 𝑖 ) iff 𝜃 is in the interval

                    [min{𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖), 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖)}, max{𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖), 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖)}]

and this happens with probability |𝑥(𝑢, 𝑖) − 𝑥(𝑣, 𝑖)|.                                          

  𝑢𝑣 𝑐 𝑢𝑣 𝑧 𝑢𝑣,𝑖 and establishes the first property.
   We now show that uncrossing can be done without increasing the cost. Here
we rely on submodularity and symmetry of the cut function.
Lemma 15.5. Let 𝑓 : 2𝑉 → ℝ be a symmetric submodular function. For any two sets

                    𝑓 (𝐴) + 𝑓 (𝐵) ≥ 𝑓 (𝐴 − 𝐵) + 𝑓 (𝐵 − 𝐴).

15.3.3    The CKR Rounding
In this section we describe the rounding algorithm from [53]. The first step
is to pick a 𝜃 randomly from [0, 1) to take the balls of radius 𝜃 from each 𝑠 𝑖 .
However we need to uncross them since they can overlap. We did it explicitly in
the preceding algorithm. Instead the original algorithm does it implicitly via the
use of a random permutation 𝜎 to uncross the balls.

  CKR-Rounding(G, S)

  1. Let 𝑥¯ be an optimum solution to the CKR relaxation on 𝐺 with terminals 𝑆

  2. Pick 𝜃 uniformly at random from [0, 1)

  3. Pick a random permutation 𝜎 of {1, 2, . . . , 𝑘}

  4. For 𝑖 = 1 to 𝑘 let 𝐴 𝜎(𝑖) = 𝐵(𝑠 𝜎(𝑖) , 𝜃)\(∪ 𝑗<𝑖 𝐴 𝜎(𝑗) )
                   𝑘
  5. Let 𝑈 = 𝑉 \ (∪𝑖=1 𝐴 𝑖 ) be the unallocated nodes

  6. Output the partition 𝐴1 ∪ 𝑈 , 𝐴2 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                        210

    We leave the following as an exercise so that the reader can see that the
random permutation is one simple and very efficient way to uncross to preserve
the expected cost of uncrossing.

15.4    Node-weighted Multiway Cut
In Node-weighted Multiway Cut the graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) is undirected with non-
negative node weights 𝑤 : 𝑉 → ℝ+ and the goal is to find a minimum-weight
set of non-terminal nodes whose removal separates all terminals from each other.
It is easy to see that Node-weighted Multiway Cut generalizes Multiway Cut.
Vertex Cover reduces to Node-weighted Multiway Cut in an approximation-
preserving fashion [118], and hence, under UGC we do not expect any ratio
better than 2. This also shows that Node-weighted Multiway Cut is strictly
harder than Multiway Cut in terms of approximation ratio. Garg, Vazirani,
and Yannakakis [118] obtained a 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation for Node-weighted
Multiway Cut via the natural LP relaxation. To do this they showed that there
always exists a half-integral optimum solution to the natural LP relaxation and
used that property to round. In [77] it was shown that there is a much simpler
and natural rounding strategy which is similar to that for Multiway Cut that we
saw via the distance LP. We outline the ideas below and leave the details to the
reader to work out.
LP relaxation. Assign a length 𝑥 𝑣 ≥ 0 to each non-terminal node (terminals
are fixed with 𝑥 𝑠 𝑖 = 0). For any path 𝑝 between terminals 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑠 𝑗 , require
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                           211


    𝑣∈𝑝 𝑥 𝑣 ≥ 1:
Í
                                         Õ
                                min              𝑤𝑣 𝑥𝑣
                                         𝑣∈𝑉\𝑆
                                          Õ
                                s.t.           𝑥 𝑣 ≥ 1 𝑝 ∈ 𝒫𝑖𝑗 , 𝑖 < 𝑗
                                         𝑣∈𝑝
                                               𝑥𝑣 = 0           𝑣∈𝑆
                                               𝑥𝑣 ≥ 0           𝑣∈𝑉
Define 𝑑 𝑥 (𝑢, 𝑣) as the shortest-path distance counting node weights at both
endpoints, so 𝑑 𝑥 (𝑢, 𝑣) ≥ 𝑥 𝑢 + 𝑥 𝑣 . For a node 𝑢 and radius 𝑟 let 𝐵(𝑢, 𝑟) = {𝑣 :
𝑑 𝑥 (𝑢, 𝑣) ≤ 𝑟} and define the boundary 𝐵+ (𝑢, 𝑟) = {𝑣 ∉ 𝐵(𝑢, 𝑟) : 𝑣 has a neighbor
in 𝐵(𝑢, 𝑟)}.

Lemma 15.9. 𝑣 ∈ 𝐵+ (𝑢, 𝑟) if and only if 𝑟 < 𝑑 𝑥 (𝑢, 𝑣) ≤ 𝑟 + 𝑥 𝑣 . In particular,
𝑣 ∈ 𝐵+ (𝑢, 𝑟) for 𝑟 < 1 implies 𝑥 𝑣 > 0.

Rounding algorithm. The algorithm omits one terminal chosen uniformly
at random and cuts the boundaries of half-radius balls around all remaining
terminals.

    RoundNodeMC(G, S, x)

    1. Pick ℓ ∈ {1, 2, . . . , 𝑘} uniformly at random

    2. Pick 𝜃 ∈ (0, 1/2) uniformly at random
                    Ø
    3. Output 𝐶 =         𝐵+ (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃)
                    𝑖≠ℓ




Theorem 15.2 ([77]). RoundNodeMC always returns a feasible node multiway cut
and its expected cost is at most 2(1 − 1/𝑘) 𝑣 𝑤 𝑣 𝑥 𝑣 .
                                           Í

    This is essentially the same as the BallCut algorithm for Multiway Cut
generalized to the node-weighted setting. We omit the formal proof and leave it
for the reader to figure it out the details. It is easier to prove a bound of 2 via the
simpler BallCut algorithm.


15.5       Directed Multiway Cut
In Directed Multiway Cut the graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) is directed with non-negative
edge weights and the goal is to find a minimum-weight set of edges whose
removal ensures there is no directed path from 𝑠 𝑖 to 𝑠 𝑗 for any 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗. Note that
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                                  212

Directed Multiway Cut with 𝑘 = 2 requires simultaneously eliminating all paths
from 𝑠 1 to 𝑠 2 and from 𝑠 2 to 𝑠 1 . This is also called the 𝑠-𝑡 Bicut problem, and
surprisingly it is already NP-Hard [117].
   There are approximation-preserving reductions which establish the follow-
ing:

  Multiway Cut ≤ Node-weighted Multiway Cut ≤ Directed Multiway Cut.

Moreover, Vertex Cover reduces to both Node-weighted Multiway Cut and
Directed Multiway Cut in an approximation-preserving fashion [118], so both
problems are provably harder to approximate than Multiway Cut. Under the
Unique Games Conjecture [176], this implies that 2-approximation is the best
one can achieve. Naor and Zosin [212] obtained a 2-approximation for Directed
Multiway Cut which was also the first constant factor approximation. Although
their algorithms and analysis were short, they were somewhat mysterious. It is
only in 2016 that a very simple rounding algorithm achieving the same bound
was achieved in [77] and the analysis is also extremely simple. This algorithm
also had the advantage that it can be applied to any feasible solution to the
natural LP relaxation unlike the one in [212]. We describe it next.
LP relaxation. Let 𝒫𝑖𝑗 denote the set of all directed paths from 𝑠 𝑖 to 𝑠 𝑗 in 𝐺.
The natural LP relaxation assigns a length 𝑥 𝑒 ≥ 0 to each edge and requires
every directed 𝑠 𝑖 -𝑠 𝑗 path to have length at least 1:
                                     Õ
                            min            𝑤𝑒 𝑥𝑒
                                   Õ𝑒∈𝐸
                            s.t.          𝑥 𝑒 ≥ 1 𝑝 ∈ 𝒫𝑖𝑗 , 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗
                                    𝑒∈𝑝
                                          𝑥𝑒 ≥ 0           𝑒∈𝐸

For any feasible 𝑥 let 𝑑 𝑥 (𝑢, 𝑣) denote the shortest directed-path distance from
𝑢 to 𝑣 under edge lengths 𝑥, and let 𝐵(𝑣, 𝑟) = {𝑢 : 𝑑 𝑥 (𝑣, 𝑢) ≤ 𝑟} be the ball of
radius 𝑟 around 𝑣.
Rounding algorithm. The key insight is to reduce Directed Multiway Cut
to a directed multicut instance via dummy terminals. For each terminal 𝑠 𝑖
introduce a new dummy node 𝑡 𝑖 and add zero-weight edges (𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑗 ) for all 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖;
see Figure 15.1. Setting 𝑥(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑗 ) = 0 extends the fractional solution feasibly:
𝑑(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑗 ) = 0 for 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖, while 𝑑(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑖 ) ≥ 1 since any directed 𝑠 𝑗 -to-𝑠 𝑖 path in the
augmented graph still has length at least 1. The resulting instance requires
separating each pair (𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑖 ), which the following ball-cutting algorithm handles.

Lemma 15.10. For any 𝜃 ∈ (0, 1), 𝐶 is a feasible multiway cut.

Proof. Fix 𝑖 and 𝜃 ∈ (0, 1). Since 𝑑(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑗 ) = 0 for all 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖, every such 𝑠 𝑗 lies in
𝐵(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝜃). Since 𝑑(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑖 ) ≥ 1 > 𝜃, we have 𝑠 𝑖 ∉ 𝐵(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝜃). Removing 𝛿+ (𝐵(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝜃))
therefore eliminates all directed paths from any 𝑠 𝑗 (𝑗 ≠ 𝑖) to 𝑠 𝑖 . Taking the union
over all 𝑖 gives feasibility.                                                            

Then 𝑑(𝑡1 , 𝑢) = 𝑑(𝑠 2 , 𝑢) and 𝑑(𝑡2 , 𝑢) = · · · = 𝑑(𝑡 𝑘 , 𝑢) = 𝑑(𝑠 1 , 𝑢). Edge 𝑒 enters
𝛿+ (𝐵(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝜃)) iff 𝜃 ∈ 𝐼 𝑖 := [𝑑(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑢), 𝑑(𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑢) + 𝑥 𝑒 ). Since 𝑡2 , . . . , 𝑡 𝑘 are equidistant
CHAPTER 15. MULTIWAY CUT                                                              214

from 𝑢, the intervals 𝐼2 = 𝐼3 = · · · = 𝐼 𝑘 coincide. Hence 𝑒 ∈ 𝐶 only if 𝜃 ∈ 𝐼1 ∪ 𝐼2 ,
and since each interval has length at most 𝑥 𝑒 ,

                      P[𝑒 ∈ 𝐶] ≤ P[𝜃 ∈ 𝐼1 ] + P[𝜃 ∈ 𝐼2 ] ≤ 2𝑥 𝑒 .

    By linearity of expectation Lemma 15.11 gives 𝔼[𝑤(𝐶)] ≤ 2

Remark 15.1. The dummy-terminal reduction is essential: the naive algorithm
that cuts 𝑖 𝛿+ (𝐵(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝜃)) directly does not achieve a constant approximation ratio
         Ð
in general.
Integrality gap and hardness: The integerality gap of the LP is 2. Surprisingly
this holds even for 𝑘 = 2 in planar directed graphs [77], in other words for the
𝑠-𝑡 Bicut problem. Interestingly, under UGC, the hardness of 2 holds even for
this case!
    A notable structural difference from Multiway Cut is that the LP relaxation
for Directed Multiway Cut need not have half-integral optimum solutions, even
for 𝑘 = 2. In contrast, the LP for Multiway Cut and Node-weighted Multiway
Cut always has a half-integral optimum [118].


Bibliographic Notes
There is extensive work on Multiway Cut problem after the CKR relaxation.
Finding the right integrality gap is a fascinating open problem. The case of
𝑘 = 4 is open. Although there has been substantial focus on the CKR relaxation
for graphs there has been less attention to the generalization of the problem to
the submodular setting. As we mentioned, Chekuri and Ene [69] obtained a
1.5-approximation for Symmetric Submodular Multiway Partition (SymSubMP)
problem. They also obtained a 2-approximation for SubMP problem. Ene,
Vondrák and Yu [97] improved these bounds to (1.5 − 1/𝑘) and 2(1 − 1/𝑘), and
also simplified the analysis. Further, they proved hardness results: 2(1 − 1/𝑘) for
SubMP and 1.26 for SymSubMP. These hardness results are of a different flavor
and show that obtaining better than these ratios would require an exponential
number of queries in the value oracle model. We refer the interested reader to
[97]. Another interesting paper is that of Ene and Nguyen [96] who considered
Hypergraph Partition which generalizes Multiway Cut and is a special case of
SymSubMP. Can one improve the 1.5-approximation for SymSubMP or its special
cases including Hypergraph Partition?
Chapter 16

𝒌-Cut and Steiner 𝒌-Cut

In the minimum 𝑘-cut problem (𝑘-Cut) we are given an undirected graph
𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) with edge weights 𝑤 : 𝐸 → ℝ+ and a positive integer 𝑘 ≥ 2. The
goal is to find a minimum weight set of edges 𝐹 ⊆ 𝐸 such that 𝐺 − 𝐹 has
at least 𝑘 connected components. When 𝑘 = 2 this is the global minimum cut
problem, which can be solved in polynomial time (and in near-linear time using
randomization). The minimum 𝑘-cut problem can be solved in 𝑂(𝑛 𝑂(𝑘) ) time,
and hence is polynomial-time solvable for any fixed 𝑘. However, the problem is
NP-hard when 𝑘 is part of the input. Note that unlike Multiway Cut problem
that we saw in the previous chapter there are no terminals specified in 𝑘-Cut.
    We now describe the Steiner 𝑘-cut problem which generalizes 𝑘-Cut and
Multiway Cut. The input is an edge-weighted graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸), integer 𝑘, and
a set of terminals 𝑇 = {𝑡1 , 𝑡2 , . . . , 𝑡 ℎ } ⊆ 𝑉. The goal is to find a minimum weight
set of edges 𝐹 such that 𝐺 − 𝐹 has at least 𝑘-connected components such that each
of those components contains a terminal from 𝑇. When ℎ = 𝑘 this is Multiway
Cut and when 𝑇 = 𝑉 this is 𝑘-Cut.
    We saw that Multiway Cut admits a simple 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation and a
1.2787-approximation via the sophisticated CKR LP relaxation. In contrast, it
is known that 𝑘-Cut does not admit a better than (2 − 𝜖)-approximation under
plausible hardness results [200]. In this chapter we describe two approaches,
both achieving a 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation for Steiner 𝑘-cut. We describe an
algorithm based on Gomory-Hu trees for 𝑘-Cut which can be generalized to
Steiner 𝑘-cut, and a greedy splitting algorithm for the Steiner 𝑘-cut problem.
These results can be seen as special cases of submodular partitioning problems
that we will discuss later in bibliographic notes.




                                          215
CHAPTER 16. 𝐾-CUT AND STEINER 𝐾-CUT                                                        216

16.1         Gomory-Hu Trees
Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) be an undirected graph with edge weights 𝑤 : 𝐸 → ℝ+ and
let 𝑛 = |𝑉 |. For a pair of vertices 𝑎, 𝑏 ∈ 𝑉, let mc(𝑎, 𝑏) denote the value of a
minimum     𝑎-𝑏 cut in 𝐺. There are 𝑛2 pairs of vertices and hence potentially
 𝑛
 2 different minimum cut values. Remarkably, only 𝑛 − 1 of these values are
   
distinct. Moreover, the entire pairwise minimum cut structure is captured by a
single weighted tree.

Theorem 16.1 (Gomory-Hu [126]). For any undirected graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) with edge
weights 𝑤 : 𝐸 → ℝ+ , there exists a weighted tree 𝒯 = (𝑉 , 𝐸𝒯 ) on the same vertex set
𝑉 with edge weights 𝑢 : 𝐸𝒯 → ℝ+ such that:

   1. For every pair 𝑎, 𝑏 ∈ 𝑉, the value mc(𝑎, 𝑏) in 𝐺 equals the minimum weight of
      any edge on the unique 𝑎-𝑏 path in 𝒯 .

   2. For every edge 𝑒 = (𝑝, 𝑞) ∈ 𝐸𝒯 , the two components of 𝒯 − 𝑒 define a bipartition
      (𝑆, 𝑉 \ 𝑆) of 𝑉 that is a minimum 𝑝-𝑞 cut in 𝐺, and 𝑢(𝑒) = mc(𝑝, 𝑞).

    The tree 𝒯 is called a Gomory-Hu tree (or cut tree) of 𝐺. Note that 𝒯 has
the same vertex set as 𝐺 but its edges need not correspond to edges of 𝐺; it
is a separate weighted tree encoding pairwise minimum cut information. A
Gomory-Hu tree can be constructed using 𝑛 − 1 maximum flow computations.

16.2         A Gomory-Hu Tree Based Algorithm for 𝒌-Cut
The Gomory-Hu tree gives a simple approximation algorithm for the 𝑘-Cut
problem: remove the 𝑘 − 1 minimum weight edges of the Gomory-Hu tree and
use the resulting partition.
CHAPTER 16. 𝐾-CUT AND STEINER 𝐾-CUT                                                           217



  GomoryHuKCut(𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸, 𝑤), 𝑘 )

  1. Compute the Gomory-Hu tree 𝒯 = (𝑉 , 𝐸𝒯 , 𝑢) of 𝐺

  2. Let 𝑒1 , 𝑒2 , . . . , 𝑒 𝑘−1 be the 𝑘 − 1 minimum weight edges of 𝒯 (breaking ties
     arbitrarily)

  3. Remove 𝑒1 , . . . , 𝑒 𝑘−1 from 𝒯 ; let 𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉𝑘 be the resulting connected
     components

  4. Output the set of edges of 𝐺 crossing the partition (𝑉1 , . . . , 𝑉𝑘 )


   The partition (𝑉1 , . . . , 𝑉𝑘 ) is clearly a valid 𝑘-cut since removing 𝑘 − 1 edges
from a tree on 𝑛 vertices yields exactly 𝑘 connected components. We now
analyze the approximation ratio.
Theorem 16.2. The GomoryHuKCut algorithm is a 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation for
the minimum 𝑘-cut problem.
Proof. Let OPT denote the value of an optimal 𝑘-cut and let (𝑉1∗ , 𝑉2∗ , . . . , 𝑉𝑘∗ ) be
an optimal partition.
Step 1: Bounding the algorithm’s cost. For each removed tree edge 𝑒 𝑗 = (𝑝 𝑗 , 𝑞 𝑗 ),
the two components of 𝒯 − 𝑒 𝑗 give a bipartition (𝑆 𝑗 , 𝑉 \ 𝑆 𝑗 ) that is a minimum
𝑝 𝑗 -𝑞 𝑗 cut in 𝐺 of weight 𝑢(𝑒 𝑗 ). An edge of 𝐺 is in the output 𝑘-cut if and only if
its endpoints lie in different components of 𝒯 − {𝑒1 , . . . , 𝑒 𝑘−1 }. Since any such
edge must cross at least one of the bipartitions (𝑆 𝑗 , 𝑉 \ 𝑆 𝑗 ), the set of output
edges is contained in the union ∪ 𝑘−1     𝛿 (𝑆 ), and therefore
                                       𝑗=1 𝐺 𝑗

16.3      Greedy Splitting for Steiner 𝒌-Cut
We now describe a greedy splitting algorithm for the Steiner 𝑘-cut problem.
The idea is to build up a partition by splitting one component at a time, always
choosing the cheapest available split, until each component contains at most one
terminal.
CHAPTER 16. 𝐾-CUT AND STEINER 𝐾-CUT                                                   219



  GreedySplit(𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸, 𝑤), 𝑇 = {𝑡1 , . . . , 𝑡 ℎ })

  1. Initialize partition 𝒫0 ← {𝑉 }

  2. For 𝑖 = 1, 2, . . . , 𝑘 − 1:

     A. For each component 𝐶 ∈ 𝒫𝑖−1 that contains at least two terminals, find
         the minimum weight cut of 𝐺[𝐶] that places at least one terminal on
         each side; let 𝑓 (𝐶) be its value
     B. Let 𝐶 ∗ be the component minimizing 𝑓 (𝐶 ∗ )
     C. Split 𝐶 ∗ into two parts along this cut to obtain 𝒫𝑖

  3. Output the edges of 𝐺 crossing the final partition 𝒫𝑘−1


    At the start all ℎ terminals are in a single component. Each split increases
the number of components by one, and each resulting part contains at least one
terminal. After 𝑘 − 1 splits there are 𝑘 components, each containing at least one
terminal, so the output is a feasible solution to the Steiner 𝑘-cut instance.
    We use 𝑤(𝒫𝑖 ) to denote the total weight of edges crossing the partition 𝒫𝑖 ,
so the algorithm’s output cost is 𝑤(𝒫𝑘−1 ).
    We say that an arbitrary partition 𝒫 = {𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉𝑗 } of 𝑉 is valid if each 𝑉𝑖
contains at least one terminal from 𝑇.

Theorem 16.3. The GreedySplit algorithm is a 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation for the
Steiner 𝑘-cut problem.

    The proof relies on the following inductive lemma.

Lemma 16.1. For every 𝑖 ∈ [𝑘 − 1] and every valid partition 𝒫 = {𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉𝑖 }
into 𝑖 parts, we have

                   𝑤(𝒫𝑖−1 ) ≤ 𝑤(𝛿(𝑉1 )) + 𝑤(𝛿(𝑉2 )) + · · · + 𝑤(𝛿(𝑉𝑖−1 )).

   Note that the right-hand side in the lemma sums over only 𝑖 − 1 of the 𝑖 parts
of 𝒫.
   Before proving the lemma, we show how it implies the theorem.

Proof of Theorem 16.3. Let {𝐴1 , 𝐴2 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘 } be an optimal Steiner 𝑘-cut with cost
OPT. Each edge in the optimal cut has endpoints in two different parts,
   Í𝑘
so 𝑖=1   𝑤(𝛿(𝐴 𝑖 )) = 2 OPT. Without loss of generality assume 𝑤(𝛿(𝐴1 )) ≤

CHAPTER 16. 𝐾-CUT AND STEINER 𝐾-CUT                                              220

   Applying Lemma 16.1 with 𝑖 = 𝑘 and the valid partition {𝐴1 , . . . , 𝐴 𝑘 }:

Bibliographic Notes
The polynomial-time solvability of 𝑘-Cut for fixed 𝑘 was initially due to a paper of
Goldscmidt and Hochbaum [125]. There have been many results improving the
run-time and generalizing to hypergraphs, submodular functions and so on; see
[146] and references therein. The greedy splitting analysis for 𝑘-Cut was initially
due to Saran and Vazirani [229] and the analysis was not so easy. The Gomory-Hu
tree based algorithm for 𝑘-Cut is due to independent work of Kapoor and R.
Ravi and is much simpler to analyze. The Steiner 𝑘-Cut problem was coined and
considered in [70]; the paper gave a Gomory-Hu tree based algorithm as well as
another algorithm based on an LP relaxation. The analysis of the greedy splitting
algorithm for Steiner 𝑘-Cut is adapted from the paper of Zhao, Nagamochi
and Ibaraki [261] who showed that it yields a 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation for any
symmetric submodular function. The inductive claim and proof are short and
elegant but maynot be that intuitive. One can also obtain the same ratio via a
Gomory-Hu tree based algorithm since every symmetric submodular function
admits such a tree. Yet another 2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation can be obtained via
CHAPTER 16. 𝐾-CUT AND STEINER 𝐾-CUT                                         222

the principal sequence of partitions of a submodular function [61]; the same
paper has other interesting results for monotone and posi-modular submodular
functions. Zhao et al also considered general non-negative submodular functions
and showed that one obtains a 𝑘-approximation. Chekuri and Li [75] showed that
Hypegraph 𝑘-Cut, which is a special case of the general submodular case, is hard
to approximate to polynomial factor via hardness for the Densest 𝑘-Subgraph
problem. Thus, the general submodular case is much harder. This is in contrast
the version with terminals considered in the previous chapter which admits a
2(1 − 1/𝑘)-approximation even for general non-negative submodular functions.
Chapter 17

Multicut

In the Multicut problem, we are given a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸), a capacity function
that assigns a capacity 𝑐 𝑒 to each edge 𝑒, and a set of pairs (𝑠 1 , 𝑡1 ), ..., (𝑠 𝑘 , 𝑡 𝑘 ). The
Multicut problem asks for a minimum capacity set of edges 𝐹 ⊆ 𝐸 such that
removing the edges in 𝐹 disconnects 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 , for all 𝑖. Note that the Multicut
problem generalizes the Multiway Cut problem that we saw previously. We
describe an 𝑂(log 𝑘) approximation algorithm for Multicut.
    We start by describing an LP formulation for the problem. For each edge
𝑒, we have a variable 𝑑 𝑒 . We interpret each variable 𝑑 𝑒 as a distance label for
the edge. Let 𝒫𝑠 𝑖 ,𝑡 𝑖 denote the set of all paths between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 . We have the
following LP for the problem:

                                          𝑐 𝑒 𝑑𝑒
                                      Í
                          min
                                    𝑒∈𝐸
                          s.t.
                                        𝑑 𝑒 ≥ 1 𝑝 ∈ 𝒫𝑠 𝑖 ,𝑡 𝑖 , 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑘
                                  Í
                                  𝑒∈𝑝
                                        𝑑𝑒 ≥ 0                    𝑒∈𝐸
   The LP assigns distance labels to edges so that, on each path 𝑝 between 𝑠 𝑖
and 𝑡 𝑖 , the distance labels of the edges on 𝑝 sum up to at least one. Note that,
even though the LP can have exponentially many constraints, we can solve the
LP in polynomial time using the ellipsoid method and the following separation
oracle. Given distance labels 𝑑 𝑒 , we set the length of each edge to 𝑑 𝑒 and, for
each pair (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ), we compute the length of the shortest path between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖
and check whether it is at least one. If the shortest path between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 has
length smaller than one, we have a violated constraint. Conversely, if all shortest
paths have length at least one, the distance labels define a feasible solution.
   We also consider the dual of the previous LP. For each path 𝑝 between any
pair (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ) we have a dual variable 𝑓𝑝 . We interpret each variable 𝑓𝑝 as the


                                                   223
CHAPTER 17. MULTICUT                                                             224

amount of flow between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 that is routed along the path 𝑝. We have the
following dual LP:
                            𝑘
                                              𝑓𝑝
                            Í       Í
                    max
                            𝑖=1 𝑝∈𝒫𝑠 𝑖 ,𝑡 𝑖
                    s.t.
                                    𝑓𝑝 ≤ 𝑐 𝑒                  𝑒 ∈ 𝐸(𝐺)
                            Í
                           𝑝: 𝑒∈𝑝


                                     𝑓𝑝 ≥ 0 𝑝 ∈ 𝒫𝑠1 ,𝑡1 ∪ ... ∪ 𝒫𝑠 𝑘 ,𝑡 𝑘
    The dual is an LP formulation for the Maximum Throughput Multicommod-
ity Flow problem. In the Maximum Throughput Multicommodity Flow problem,
we have 𝑘 different commodities. For each 𝑖, we want to route commodity 𝑖
from the source 𝑠 𝑖 to the destination 𝑡 𝑖 . Each commodity must satisfy flow
conservation at each vertex other than its source and its destination. Additionally,
the total flow routed on each edge must not exceed the capacity of the edge. The
goal is to maximize the sum of the commodities routed.
    The dual LP tries to assign an amount of flow 𝑓𝑝 to each path 𝑝 so that the
total flow on each edge is at most the capacity of the edge (the flow conservation
constraints are automatically satisfied). Note that the endpoints of the path 𝑝
determine which kind of commodity is routed along the path.

Exercise 17.1. Write the Multicut LP and its dual in a compact form with
polynomially many constraints.


17.1     Upper Bound on the Integrality Gap
In this section, we will show that the integrality gap of the LP is 𝑂(log 𝑘) using
a randomized rounding algorithm due to Calinescu, Karloff, and Rabani [51].
The first algorithm that achieved an 𝑂(log 𝑘)-approximation for Multicut is due
to Garg, Vazirani, and Yannakakis [117] (see [250] and [255]), and it is based
on the region growing technique introduced by Leighton and Rao [189]. The
reason that we choose to present the randomized rounding algorithm is due to
its future application for metric embeddings.
     Let 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑣, 𝑟) denote the ball of radius 𝑟 centered at the vertex 𝑣 in the metric
induced by the distance labels 𝑑 𝑒 .
CHAPTER 17. MULTICUT                                                                     225

                     CKR-RandomPartition:
                      Solve the LP to get the distance labels 𝑑 𝑒
                      Pick 𝜃 uniformly at random from [0, 1/2)
                      Pick a random permutation 𝜎 on {1, 2, ..., 𝑘}
                      for 𝑖 = 1 to 𝑘
                           𝑉𝜎(𝑖) = 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝜎(𝑖) , 𝜃)\ 𝑉𝜎(𝑗)

Lemma 17.1. CKR-RandomPartition correctly outputs a feasible multicut for the given
instance.

Proof. Let 𝐹 be the set of edges output by the algorithm. Suppose 𝐹 is not a
feasible multicut. Then there exists a pair of vertices (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ) such that there is a
path between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 in 𝐺 − 𝐹. Therefore there exists a 𝑗 such that 𝑉𝑗 contains
𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 . Since 𝑉𝑗 ⊆ 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑠 𝑗 , 𝜃), both 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 are contained in the ball of radius 𝜃
centered at 𝑠 𝑗 . Consequently, the distance between 𝑠 𝑗 and 𝑠 𝑖 is at most 𝜃 and the
distance between 𝑠 𝑗 and 𝑡 𝑖 is at most 𝜃. By the triangle inequality, the distance
between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 is at most 2𝜃. Since 𝜃 is smaller than 1/2, it follows that the
distance between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 is smaller than one. This contradicts the fact that the
distance labels 𝑑 𝑒 are a feasible solution for the LP. Therefore 𝐹 is a multicut, as
desired.                                                                                     
Lemma 17.2. The probability that an edge 𝑒 is cut is at most 2𝐻 𝑘 𝑑 𝑒 , where 𝐻 𝑘 is the
𝑘-th harmonic number and 𝑑 𝑒 is the distance label of the edge 𝑒.

Proof. Fix an edge 𝑒 = (𝑢, 𝑣). Let:

                               𝐿 𝑖 = min{𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑢), 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑣)}

                               𝑅 𝑖 = max{𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑢), 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑣)}
We may assume without loss of generality that 𝐿1 ≤ 𝐿2 ≤ ... ≤ 𝐿 𝑘 (by reindexing
the pairs as needed). See Fig 17.1.
Let 𝐴 𝑖 be the event that the edge 𝑒 is cut first by 𝑠 𝑖 . More precisely, 𝐴 𝑖 is the
event that |𝑉𝑖 ∩ {𝑢, 𝑣}| = 1 and |𝑉𝑗 ∩ {𝑢, 𝑣}| = 0 for all 𝑗 such that 𝜎(𝑗) < 𝜎(𝑖).
Note that |𝑉𝑖 ∩ {𝑢, 𝑣}| = 1 simply says that 𝑠 𝑖 cuts the edge 𝑒. If 𝑠 𝑖 is the first to
cut the edge 𝑒, for all 𝑗 that come before 𝑖 in the permutation, neither 𝑢 nor 𝑣
can be in 𝑉𝑗 (if only one of 𝑢 and 𝑣 is in 𝑉𝑗 , 𝑠 𝑗 cuts the edge 𝑒; if both 𝑢 and 𝑣 are
in 𝑉𝑗 , 𝑠 𝑖 cannot cut the edge 𝑒).
Note that the event that the edge 𝑒 is cut is the union of the disjoint events
𝐴1 , ..., 𝐴 𝑘 . Therefore we have:
CHAPTER 17. MULTICUT                                                                 226




Figure 17.1: For a fixed edge 𝑒 = (𝑢, 𝑣) we renumber the pairs such that
𝐿1 ≤ 𝐿2 ≤ ... ≤ 𝐿 𝑘 .


                                               Õ
                               P[𝑒 is cut] =         P[𝐴 𝑖 ].
                                               𝑖

Let us fix 𝑟 ∈ [0, 1/2) and consider P[𝐴 𝑖 | 𝜃 = 𝑟]. Note that 𝑠 𝑖 cuts the edge 𝑒
only if one of 𝑢, 𝑣 is inside the ball of radius 𝑟 centered at 𝑠 𝑖 and the other is
outside the ball. Differently said, 𝑠 𝑖 cuts the edge only if 𝑟 ∈ [𝐿 𝑖 , 𝑅 𝑖 ):

                          P[𝐴 𝑖 | 𝜃 = 𝑟] = 0       if 𝑟 ∉ [𝐿 𝑖 , 𝑅 𝑖 )




Now suppose that 𝑟 ∈ [𝐿 𝑖 , 𝑅 𝑖 ). Let us fix 𝑗 < 𝑖 and suppose 𝑗 comes before
𝑖 in the permutation (that is, 𝜎(𝑗) < 𝜎(𝑖)). Recall that, since 𝑗 < 𝑖, we have
𝐿 𝑗 ≤ 𝐿 𝑖 ≤ 𝑟. Therefore at least one of 𝑢, 𝑣 is inside the ball of radius 𝑟 centered at

to cut the edge 𝑒 only if 𝜎(𝑖) < 𝜎(𝑗) for all 𝑗 < 𝑖. See Fig 17.2. Since 𝜎 is a random
permutation, 𝑖 appears before 𝑗 for all 𝑗 < 𝑖 with probability 1/𝑖. Therefore we
have:

Corollary 17.1. The integrality gap of the Multicut LP is 𝑂(log 𝑘).

Proof. Let 𝐹 be the set of edges outputted by the Randomized Rounding algo-
rithm. For each edge 𝑒, let 𝜒𝑒 be an indicator random variable equal to 1 if and
only if the edge 𝑒 is in 𝐹. As we have already seen,

                              E 𝜒𝑒 = P[𝜒𝑒 = 1] ≤ 2𝐻 𝑘 𝑑 𝑒
Let 𝑐(𝐹) be a random variable equal to the total capacity of the edges in 𝐹. We
have:
CHAPTER 17. MULTICUT                                                                          228

                        𝑒                 𝑒                       𝑒

Consequently, there exists a set of edges 𝐹 such that the total capacity of the
edges in 𝐹 is at most 2𝐻 𝑘 OPTLP . Therefore OPT ≤ 2𝐻 𝑘 OPTLP , as desired. 

for the Multicut problem.

Remark 17.1. The expected cost analysis can be used to obtain an algorithm,
via repetition, a randomized algorithm that outputs an 𝑂(log 𝑘)-approximation
with high probability. The algorithm can also be derandomized but it is not
straight forward. As we remarked there is an alternative deterministic 𝑂(log 𝑘)-
approximation algorithm via region growing.
Flow-Cut Gap: Recall that when 𝑘 = 1 we have the well-known maxflow-
mincut theorem. The integrality gap of the standard LP for Multicut is the same
as the relative gap between flow and cut when 𝑘 is arbitrary. The upper bound
on the integrality gap gives an upper bound on the gap.

17.2     Lower Bound on the Integrality Gap
In this section, we will show that the integrality gap of the LP is Ω(log 𝑘). That
is, we will give a Multicut instance for which the LP gap is Ω(log 𝑘). Let’s start
by looking at expander graphs and their properties.

17.2.1   Expander Graphs
Definition 17.4. A graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) is an 𝛼-edge-expander if, for any subset 𝑆 of at
most |𝑉 |/2 vertices, the number of edges crossing the cut (𝑆, 𝑉\𝑆) is at least 𝛼|𝑆|.

Note that the complete graph 𝐾 𝑛 is a (|𝑉 |/2)-edge-expander. However, the more
interesting expander graphs are also sparse. Cycles and grids are examples of
graphs that are very poor expanders.




Figure 17.3: The top half of the cycle has |𝑉 |/2 vertices and only two edges
crossing
p         the cut. The left half of the grid has roughly |𝑉 |/2 vertices and only
  |𝑉 | edges crossing the cut.


Definition 17.5. A graph 𝐺 is 𝑑-regular if every vertex in 𝐺 has degree 𝑑.

Note that 2-regular graphs consist of a collection of edge disjoint cycles and
therefore they have poor expansion. However, for any 𝑑 ≥ 3, there exist 𝑑-regular
graphs that are very good expanders.
CHAPTER 17. MULTICUT                                                                              230

Theorem 17.6. For every 𝑑 ≥ 3 there exists an infinite family of 𝑑-regular 1-edge-
expanders.

We will only need the following special case of the previous theorem.

Theorem 17.7. There exists a universal constant 𝛼 > 0 and an integer 𝑛0 such that,
for all even integers 𝑛 ≥ 𝑛0 , there exists an 𝑛-vertex, 3-regular 𝛼-edge-expander.

Proof Idea. The easiest way to prove this theorem is using the probabilistic
method. The proof itself is beyond the scope of this lecture1. The proof idea is
the following.
    Let’s fix an even integer 𝑛. We will generate a 3-regular random graph 𝐺 by
selecting three random perfect matchings on the vertex set {1, 2, ..., 𝑛} (recall
that a perfect matching is a set of edges such that every vertex is incident to
exactly one of these edges). We select a random perfect matching as follows.
We maintain a list of vertices that have not been matched so far. While there is
at least one vertex that is not matched, we select a pair of distinct vertices 𝑢, 𝑣
uniformly at random from all possible pairs of unmatched vertices. We add the
edge (𝑢, 𝑣) to our matching and we remove 𝑢 and 𝑣 from the list. We repeat this
process three times (independently) to get three random matchings. The graph
𝐺 will consist of the edges in these three matchings. Note that 𝐺 is actually a
3-regular multigraph since it might have parallel edges (if the same edge is in at
least two of the matchings). There are two properties of interest: (1) 𝐺 is a simple
graph and (2) 𝐺 is an 𝛼-edge-expander for some constant 𝛼 > 0. If we can show
that 𝐺 has both properties with positive probability, it follows that there exists
a 3-regular 𝛼-edge-expander (if no graph is a 3-regular 𝛼-edge-expander, the
probability that our graph 𝐺 has both properties is equal to 0).
    It is not very hard to show that the probability that 𝐺 does not have property
(1) is small. To show that the probability that 𝐺 does not have property (2) is
small, for each set 𝑆 with at most 𝑛/2 vertices, we estimate the expected number
of edges that cross the cut (𝑆, 𝑉\𝑆) (e.g., we can easily show that |𝛿(𝑆)| ≥ |𝑆|/2).
Using tail inequalities (e.g., Chernoff bounds), we can show that the probability
that |𝛿(𝑆)| differs significantly from its expectation is extremely small (i.e., small
enough so that the sum – taken over all sets 𝑆 – of these probabilities is also
small) and we can use the union bound to get the desired result.                    
    Note that explicit constructions of 𝑑-regular expanders are also known.
Margulis [201] gave an infinite family of 8-regular expanders. There are many
explicit construction by now and it is a very important topic of study — we
refer the reader to the survey on expanders by Hoory, Linial and Wigderson
    1A more accurate statement is that the calculations are a bit involved and not terribly interesting
for us.
CHAPTER 17. MULTICUT                                                                          231

[151]. The vertex set of a graph 𝐺 𝑛 in Margulis’ construction is ℤ𝑛 × ℤ𝑛 , where
ℤ𝑛 is the set of all integers mod 𝑛. The neighbors of a vertex (𝑥, 𝑦) in 𝐺 𝑛 are
(𝑥 + 𝑦, 𝑦), (𝑥 − 𝑦, 𝑦), (𝑥, 𝑦 + 𝑥), (𝑥, 𝑦 − 𝑥), (𝑥 + 𝑦 + 1, 𝑦), (𝑥 − 𝑦 + 1, 𝑦), (𝑥, 𝑦 + 𝑥 + 1),
and (𝑥, 𝑦 − 𝑥 + 1) (all operations are mod 𝑛). Another example is the following
infinite family of 3-regular expanders. For each prime 𝑝, we have a 3-regular
graph 𝐺 𝑝 . The vertex set of 𝐺 𝑝 is ℤ𝑝 . The neighbors of a vertex 𝑥 in 𝐺 𝑝 are 𝑥 + 1,
𝑥 − 1, and 𝑥 −1 (as before, all operations are mod 𝑝; 𝑥 −1 is the inverse of 𝑥 mod 𝑝,
and we define the inverse of 0 to be 0)2.
    We conclude this section with the following observations (they will be very
useful in showing the Ω(log 𝑘) lower bound on the integrality gap of the LP).
Claim 17.2.1. Let 𝐺 be an 𝑛-vertex 𝑑-regular 𝛼-edge-expander, for some constants
𝑑 ≥ 3 and 𝛼 > 0. Then the diameter of 𝐺 is Θ(log 𝑛).
Proof. For any two vertices 𝑢 and 𝑣, let 𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑡(𝑢, 𝑣) denote the length of a shortest
path between 𝑢 and 𝑣 (the length of a path is the number of edges on the path).
Let’s fix a vertex 𝑠. Let 𝐿 𝑖 be the set of all vertices 𝑣 such that 𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑡(𝑠, 𝑣) is at most
𝑖. Now let’s show that (1 + 𝛼/𝑑)|𝐿 𝑖−1 | ≤ |𝐿 𝑖 | ≤ 𝑑|𝐿 𝑖−1 |. Clearly, |𝐿1 | = 𝑑 (since 𝑠
has degree 𝑑). Therefore we may assume that 𝑖 > 1. Every vertex in 𝐿 𝑖 is in 𝐿 𝑖−1
or it has a neighbor in 𝐿 𝑖−1 . Therefore it suffices to bound |𝐿 𝑖 \𝐿 𝑖−1 |.
      Note that any vertex in 𝐿 𝑖−1 has at least one neighbor in 𝐿 𝑖−1 . Therefore the
vertices in 𝐿 𝑖−1 have at most (𝑑 − 1)|𝐿 𝑖−1 | neighbors outside of 𝐿 𝑖−1 . Consequently,
|𝐿 𝑖 | ≤ 𝑑|𝐿 𝑖−1 |.
      Now one of 𝐿 𝑖−1 , 𝑉\𝐿 𝑖−1 has at most |𝑉 |/2 vertices. Let’s assume without loss
of generality that 𝐿 𝑖−1 has at most |𝑉 |/2 vertices (the other case is symmetric).
Let 𝐴 = 𝐿 𝑖−1 and let 𝐵 be the set of all vertices in 𝑉\𝐿 𝑖−1 that have a neighbor in
𝐿 𝑖−1 (note that |𝐿 𝑖 | = |𝐴| + |𝐵|). Let 𝐹 be the set of all edges that cross the cut
(𝐿 𝑖−1 , 𝑉\𝐿 𝑖−1 ). Now let’s look at the bipartite graph 𝐻 = (𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐹). Since 𝐺 is an
𝛼-edge-expander, we have |𝐹| ≥ 𝛼|𝐴|. Moreover, |𝐹| = 𝑣∈𝐵 𝑑𝐻 (𝑣), where 𝑑𝐻 (𝑣)
                                                               Í
is the degree of 𝑣 in 𝐻. Since 𝑑𝐻 (𝑣) is at most 𝑑, we have 𝛼|𝐴| ≤ |𝐹| ≤ 𝑑|𝐵|.
Therefore we have:

                    𝐿 𝑖 = |𝐴| + |𝐵| ≥ (1 + 𝛼/𝑑)|𝐴| = (1 + 𝛼/𝑑)|𝐿 𝑖−1 |
    It follows by induction that 𝑑(1 + 𝛼/𝑑)𝑖−1 ≤ |𝐿 𝑖 | ≤ 𝑑 𝑖 . Therefore 𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑡(𝑠, 𝑣)
is 𝑂(log 𝑛) for all 𝑣 and there exists a vertex 𝑣 such that 𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑡(𝑠, 𝑣) is Ω(log 𝑛).
Since this is true for any 𝑠, it follows that the diameter of 𝐺 is Θ(log 𝑛).      
Claim 17.2.2. Let 𝐺 be an 𝑛-vertex 3-regular 𝛼-edge-expander and let 𝐵(𝑣, 𝑖) be the

17.2.2    The Multicut Instance
Let 𝑛0 , 𝛼 be as in Theorem 7. Let 𝑛 ≥ 𝑛0 and let 𝐺 be an 𝑛-vertex 3-regular
𝛼-edge-expander. For each edge 𝑒 in 𝐺, we set the capacity 𝑐 𝑒 to 1. Now let
𝑋 = {(𝑢, 𝑣)|𝑢 ∉ 𝐵(𝑣, log3 𝑛/2)}. The pairs in 𝑋 will be the pairs (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ) that we
want to disconnect. Let (𝐺, 𝑋) be the resulting Multicut instance.

Claim 17.2.3. There exists a feasible fractional solution for (𝐺, 𝑋) of capacity 𝑂(𝑛/log 𝑛).

Proof. Let 𝑑 𝑒 = 2/log3 𝑛, for all 𝑒. Note that, since 𝐺 is 3-regular, 𝐺 has 3𝑛/2
edges. Therefore the total capacity of the fractional solution is

the path 𝑝 has more than log3 𝑛/2 edges (recall that 𝐵(𝑣, 𝑖) is the set of all vertices
𝑢 such that there is a path between 𝑢 and 𝑣 with at most 𝑖 edges). Consequently,

Claim 17.2.4. Any integral solution for (𝐺, 𝑋) has capacity Ω(𝑛).

Proof. Let 𝐹 be an integral solution for (𝐺, 𝑋). Let 𝑉1 , ..., 𝑉ℎ be the connected
components of 𝐺 − 𝐹. Fix an 𝑖 and let 𝑣 be an arbitrary vertex in the connected
component 𝑉𝑖 . Note that, for any 𝑢 in 𝑉𝑖 , there is a path between 𝑣 and 𝑢
with at most log3 𝑛/2 edges (if not, (𝑢, 𝑣) is a pair in 𝑋 which contradicts the
fact that removing the edges in 𝐹 disconnects every pair in 𝑋). Therefore√    𝑉𝑖 is
contained in 𝐵(𝑣, log3 𝑛/2). It follows from Claim 17.2.2 that |𝑉𝑖 | ≤ 𝑛. Since
𝐺 is an 𝛼-edge-expander and |𝑉𝑖 | ≤ |𝑉 |/2, we have |𝛿(𝑉𝑖 )| ≥ 𝛼|𝑉𝑖 |, for all 𝑖.
Consequently,
                                      ℎ                ℎ
                                    1Õ            𝛼Õ         𝛼𝑛
                        |𝐹| =          |𝛿(𝑉𝑖 )| ≥    |𝑉𝑖 | =
                                    2             2           2
                                      𝑖=1             𝑖=1

Therefore 𝐹 has total capacity Ω(𝑛) (recall that every edge has unit capacity). 
CHAPTER 17. MULTICUT                                                         233

Theorem 17.8. The integrality gap of the Multicut LP is Ω(log 𝑘).

Proof. Note that 𝑘 = |𝑋 | = 𝑂(𝑛 2 ). It follows from claims 10 and 11 that the LP
has integrality gap Ω(log 𝑛) = Ω(log 𝑘), as desired.                            

Bibliographic Notes
Multicut is closely related to the Sparsest Cut problem. Initial algorithm
for Multicut were based on algorithms for Sparsest Cut. Garg, Vazirani and
Yannakakis [117] then used Leighton and Rao’s region growing argument (as
well as their integrality gap example on expanders for the uniform sparsest
cut problem) [189] to obtain a tight 𝑂(log 𝑘) bound on the integrality gap
for Multicut. The randomized proof that we described is from the work of
Calinescu, Karloff and Rabani [51] on the 0-extension problem; their algorithm
and analysis eventually led to an optimal bound for approximating an arbitrary
metric via random trees [101]. For planar graphs (and more generally any proper
minor closed family of graphs) the integrality gap is 𝑂(1), as shown by Klein,
Plotkin and Rao [177] — the constant depends on the family. There have been
several subsequent refinements of the precise dependence of the constant in the
integrality gap — see [2]. The 𝑂(log 𝑘) bound extends to node-weighted case
and the 𝑂(1) approximation for planar graphs also extends to the node-weighted
case. Multicut is APX-Hard even on trees and in general graphs. Assuming
UGC, the problem is known to be hard to approximate to a super-constant factor
[68]. For some special case of Multicut based on the structure of the demand
graph one can obtain improved approximation ratios [76].
    The directed graph version of Multicut turns out to be much more difficult.
The flow-cut gap is known to be Ω̃(𝑛 1/7 ) and the problem is also known to
be hard to approximate to almost polynomial factors; these negative results
are due to Chuzhoy and Khanna [87]. The best known approximation ratio is
         ˜ 11/23 )} [5]. Very recently Kawarabayashi and Sidiropoulos obtained
min{𝑘, 𝑂(𝑛
a poly-logarithmic approximation for Directed Multicut if 𝐺 is a planar directed
graph [172]. There is a notion of symmetric demands in directed graphs and
for that version of Multicut one can get a poly-logarithmic flow-cut gap and
approximation; see [72, 179]. This is closely connected to the Feedback Arc Set
problem in directed graphs [98, 231].

Low Diameter Decomposition
and Metric Tree Embeddings

18.1     Low Diameter Decompositions
Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) be a graph with non-negative edge lengths ℓ : 𝐸 → ℝ+ . The
shortest path distances in 𝐺 define a metric space (𝑉 , 𝑑) where 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) is the
shortest path distance between 𝑢 and 𝑣 in 𝐺. There are several different ways to
decompose a graph based on the application in question. In the context of metrics,
a useful notion is to decompose or partition the graph into subgraphs/clusters of
small radius/diameter. More precisely, given a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) we would like
to partition 𝑉 into clusters with vertex sets 𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉ℎ such that each 𝑉𝑖 has
diameter at most some given parameter 𝛿. This is of course trivial by choosing
the partition with each vertex in its own part. However, the goal in partitioning
is to ensure that two vertices 𝑢, 𝑣 that are close to each other are in the same
cluster. It is clear that this is impossible to do this deterministically since the
graph/metric space is connected. Consider the case of a line for instance. What
we can hope for is a randomized partition that probabilistically ensures that any
two points 𝑢, 𝑣 are separated only with probability proportional to 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)/𝛿.
The following is a formalization of this notion. We let 𝒫 denote the set of all
partitions of 𝑉. For a partition 𝑃 ∈ 𝒫 we let 𝐸𝑃 be the set of edges/pairs that are
separatd by 𝑃.

Definition 18.1. Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) be a graph with edge lengths ℓ : 𝐸 → ℝ+ inducing a
distance 𝑑 : 𝑉 × 𝑉 → ℝ+ and let Δ be the diameter of the metric space (𝑉 , 𝑑). For a
given 𝛿 ∈ [0, Δ], a low-diameter decomposition with cutting probability parameter 𝛼 is
a probability distribution 𝒟 over 𝒫 (partitions of 𝑉) such that

   • For any partition 𝑃 = (𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉ℎ ) in the support of the distribution, the

                                          234
CHAPTER 18. LOW DIAMETER DECOMPOSITION AND METRIC TREE EMBEDDINGS235

       diameter of each (𝑉𝑖 , 𝑑) is at most 𝛿.
   • For any given 𝑢, 𝑣, the probability that 𝑢 and 𝑣 are in different parts is at most
        𝑑(𝑢,𝑣)                             𝑑(𝑢,𝑣)
     𝛼 · 𝛿 . That is P[𝑢𝑣 ∈ 𝐸𝑃 ] ≤ 𝛼 · 𝛿
A low-diameter decomposition scheme with parameter 𝛼 is a family of algorithms that
given any 𝛿 ∈ [0, Δ) generates a low-diameter decomposition with cutting/separation
probability parameter at most 𝛼.
Strong vs weak diameter guarantee: A low-diameter decomposition is said
to have the strong diameter guarantee if the diameter of each cluster 𝑉𝑖 in the
induced graphs 𝐺[𝑉𝑖 ] is at most 𝛿. Note that the preceding definition does not
require that because it is based on the metric clousure (𝑉 , 𝑑) of the given graph.
The standard definition is called the weak diameter guarantee. Some applications
require the strong diameter guarantee. We will, by default, work with weak
diameter guarantee and mention strong diameter guarantee when needed.
Padded decomposition: We can require more of a low-diameter decomposition.
Given a point 𝑢 let 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑢, 𝑟) = {𝑣 ∈ 𝑉 | 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) ≤ 𝑟} be the ball of radius 𝑟 around
𝑢. In padded decomposition we require that for each 𝑢, and for each 𝑟 ≤ 𝛿, the
probability of 𝐵(𝑢, 𝑟) being contained in the same part is at least 𝑒 −𝛽𝑟 .
Sparse covers: A sparse cover consists of several clusters 𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉ℎ . Each
cluster should have weak/strong diameter at most 𝛿. For each 𝑢, 𝑣 with distance
at most 𝛿, there must be some cluster 𝑉𝑖 that contains both 𝑢 and 𝑣. And no
vertex 𝑢 must be in more than some number 𝑠 of clusters. The techniques
underlying sparse covers and low-diameter decompositions are related though
we will mostly work only with low-diameter decompositions.
Main results: The main questions regarding low-diameter decomposition is
the smallest 𝛼 that one can obtain. It turns out that for general metric spaces
𝛼 = 𝑂(log 𝑛) is a tight bound for both strong and weak diameter guarantee;
this was formally shown by Bartal [38]. For planar graph metrics 𝛼 = 𝑂(1) is
achievable; weak diameter guarantee was first shown by Klein, Plotkin and Rao
[178] and the strong diameter guarantee was more difficult and was shown later.
These are important results that have many applications. There is substantial
work on extending the planar graph results to graphs that exclude a fixed minor
- we will not dwell into these more advanced issues in this course. See [111] for
some recent work and pointers to literature on these topics.

a given parameter 𝛿. We will assume that 𝑉 = {𝑣 1 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 }.

    CKR-RandomPartition:
     Pick 𝜃 uniformly at random from [0, 𝛿/2)
     Pick a random permutation 𝜎 of {1, 2, ..., 𝑛}
     for 𝑖 = 1 to 𝑛
          𝑉𝜎(𝑖) = 𝐵 𝑑 (𝑣 𝜎(𝑖) , 𝜃)\ 𝑉𝜎(𝑗)

for low-stretch spanning trees. The only change in the algorithm is in choosing
the radius in the range [𝛿/4, 𝛿/2) rather than from [0, 𝛿/2).

18.3      Randomized region growing for a strong diameter
          guarantee
We now describe an alternate scheme that also gives a strong diameter guarantee.
This is by Bartal [38]. For this we need some properties of the geometric
distribution.
Geometric distribution: Recall that Geom(𝑝) for a parameter 𝑝 ∈ (0, 1] is the
number of independent Bernoulli trials of a coin with bias 𝑝 to see the first heads
(here 𝑝 is the probability of the coin coming up heads). If 𝑋 ≃ Geom(𝑝) then
CHAPTER 18. LOW DIAMETER DECOMPOSITION AND METRIC TREE EMBEDDINGS238

P[𝑋 = 𝑘] = (1 − 𝑝) 𝑘−1 𝑝. The geometric distribution is a memory less discrete
distribution (only one). That is, P[𝑋 > 𝑚 + 𝑛 | 𝑋 > 𝑛] = P[𝑋 > 𝑚]; this is
intuitive from the definition but can be verified formally. The corresponding
continuous time distribution is the exponential distribution. The expectation of
a random variable 𝑋 distributed according to Geom(𝑝) is 1/𝑝 and the variances
is (1 − 𝑝)/𝑝 2 .
    Given parameter 𝛿, we can shrink all edges with length at most 𝛿/𝑛 2 and
discretize edge lengths to integer values. This does not affect the analysis by
more than constant factors. Since we want strong diameter guarantee we will
work with the underlying graph and not the metric completion. However, we
will assume that for each edge 𝑒 = 𝑢𝑣 that ℓ (𝑒) is equal to the shortest path
distance between 𝑢 and 𝑣 in 𝐺.

         RandomCarving:(𝐺, 𝛿)
           Let 𝑝 = min{1, 4 ln𝛿 𝑛 }
           While 𝐺 ≠ ∅ do
                Let 𝑣 be an arbitrary vertex
                𝑅 𝑣 = Geom(𝑝).
                Let 𝐶𝑣 = 𝐵𝐺 (𝑣, 𝑅 𝑣 ) be the ball of radius 𝑅 𝑣 in current graph
                Add 𝐶𝑣 to clusters
                𝐺 ← 𝐺 − 𝐶𝑣
           EndWhile
           Output the clusters found during the algorithm

   Note that 𝐺 − 𝐶𝑣 is the graph obtained by removing all the vertices in 𝐶𝑣 .
This may increase the shortest path distances in the remaining graph. We also
remark that the choice of 𝑝 depends on 𝑛, the original number of vertices in the
graph and does not change while the graph is undergoing changes.

Lemma 18.5. With probability at least (1 − 1/𝑛), the strong diameter of each cluster
output by the algorithm is at most 𝛿.

Lemma 18.6. For any 𝑢 ≠ 𝑣, probability that 𝑢 and 𝑣 are in different clusters is
𝑂(log 𝑛)𝑑(𝑢𝑣)/𝛿.

   We refer the reader to the proofs in Gupta’s lecture notes.


18.4    Deterministic region growing and duality
The first 𝑂(log 𝑘)-approximation algorithm for Multicut was due to Garg,
Vazirani and Yannakakis [117] via a deterministic region growing argument.
The basic argument is simple and shows up in several places (the use of the
argument in bounding flow-cut gaps is credited to Leighton and Rao [189]). Our
CHAPTER 18. LOW DIAMETER DECOMPOSITION AND METRIC TREE EMBEDDINGS239

goal here is to make a connection to low-diameter network decomposition that
we have defined probabilistically. One of the consequence of the probabilistic
statement is the following. Suppose we are given a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸), edge
lengths ℓ : 𝐸 → ℝ+ and edge weights 𝑤 : 𝐸 → ℝ+ . Then 𝐺 can be partitioned
into clusters of diameter at most 𝛿 such that the total weight of edges between
the clusters is 𝑂(log 𝑛) 1𝛿 𝑒 𝑤 𝑒 ℓ 𝑒 . To see this we simply apply the probabilistic
                           Í
low diameter decomposition to the metric induced by the edge lengths and use
linearity of expectation. Recall that this is how we proved the approximation
bound for Multicut where we obtained a slightly refined bound of 𝑂(log 𝑘).
The deterministic region growing proof for Multicut can be found in the
standard approximation textbooks [250, 255]. The proof is typically explained
via a continuous time argument and can be somewhat mysterious at first
glance. Moreover the proof is given only for Multicut and not for low-diameter
decomposition though one can easily adapt it. For this reason we give a proof
here while also giving some pedagogical perspective that may be helpful for
some readers.
    We will define the notion of volume. For a set of edges 𝐴 ⊆ 𝑉 we let
vol(𝐴) = 𝑒∈𝐴 𝑤 𝑒 ℓ 𝑒 . For a set of vertices 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉 we overload notation and define
          Í
vol(𝑆) = vol(𝐸(𝑆) ∪ 𝛿(𝑆)) (that is, the volume counts all edges that have an end
point in 𝑆). Fix a vertex 𝑣 and let 𝑅 be the radius from 𝑣, that is 𝑅 = max𝑢 𝑑(𝑣, 𝑢)
where 𝑑 is based on the shortest path distances induced by ℓ . When considering
the 𝑠-𝑡 mincut rounding we argued that if we picked a random 𝑟 ∈ (0, 𝑅) then
the 𝐸[𝑤(𝛿(𝑆𝑟 )] ≤ vol(𝑉) where 𝑆𝑟 is the set of vertices in the ball of radius 𝑟
around 𝑣; technically we had 𝑅 = 1 but the same proof holds by scaling by 𝑅.
Note that we are bounding the weight of the cut around 𝑆𝑟 by the total volume of
the graph.
    Instead we want a lemma that bounds the weight of the cut only with respect
to the volume of 𝑆𝑟 . This local guarantee will allow the algorithm to be used
repeatedly to cut the graph into many clusters while still being able to charge to
the total volume but now with an approximation factor.

18.4.1   Using LP Duality to Obtain an LDD
We will now show how one can use Theorem 18.2 to obtain a probabilistic low
diameter decomposition as we initially defined it. For this we rely on a useful
trick that is used often but also not known as widely as it should be. Recall that
an LDD is a distributions over partitions 𝑃 such that clusters in 𝑃 have diameter
at most 𝛿. Given a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) with edge lengths ℓ : 𝐸 → ℝ+ we define
𝒫 to be the set of all partitions of 𝑉 which are valid — that is, 𝑃 ∈ 𝒫 if each
cluster of 𝑃 has diameter at most 𝛿. This is an exponentially large set and we are
just defining it implicitly. Let 𝐸𝑃 be the set of edges crossing the partition 𝑃 (in
other words cut by 𝑃). Any probability distribution 𝒟 of 𝒫 can be defined by
numbers 𝑦𝑃 , 𝑃 ∈ 𝒫 such that 𝑃 𝑦𝑃 = 1 and 𝑦¯ ≥ 0. The probability that an edge
                                Í
𝑒 = 𝑢𝑣 is cut by the distribution 𝑦 is 𝑃:𝑒∈𝐸𝑃 𝑦𝑃 . Let this be denoted by 𝛼 𝑦,𝑒 . We
                                       Í
want to find the distribution 𝑦 to minimize 𝛼 𝑦,𝑒 ℓ (𝑒)/𝛿 since we are interested in
the scaled probability. We can write a large implicit LP to find the best 𝑦.

    Thus we have obtained an existential proof that a good probabilistic LDD
exists. But this seems not very useful since we want an efficient algorithm to
sample from this distribution. It turns out that one can use an algorithmic
version of Theorem 18.2 to obtain an efficient algorithm for creating and sampling
the desired distribution. This is based on the multiplicative weight update
(MWU) method. We will not discuss the technical details of this but refer the
reader to old papers of the instructor with his co-authors which employed this
methodology to obtain deterministic algorithms based on tree embeddings [63,
64].


18.5    LDDs and Probabilistic Embedding of Metrics into
        Dominating Tree Metrics
Using tree representations of graphs is a powerful tool in algorithm design.
Here we will be interested in representing the distances in an undirected graph
CHAPTER 18. LOW DIAMETER DECOMPOSITION AND METRIC TREE EMBEDDINGS243

via distances in a tree. Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) be a graph with non-negative edge length
ℓ : 𝐸 → ℝ+ . This naturally defines a metric (𝑉 , 𝑑) via shortest path distances.
    Can we approximate distances in 𝐺 by a spanning tree 𝑇 = (𝑉 , 𝐸𝑇 ) of 𝐺?
Clearly 𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣) ≥ 𝑑𝐺 (𝑢, 𝑣) for every pair 𝑢, 𝑣 where 𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣) is the distance in
the tree and 𝑑𝐺 (𝑢, 𝑣) is the distance in the graph. An extreme example is the
cycle 𝐶 on 𝑛 vertices with unit edge lengths. A spanning tree corresponds to
removing one edge (𝑢, 𝑣) from 𝐶 and in the resulting tree 𝑇, 𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣) = 𝑛 − 1
while 𝑑𝐺 (𝑢, 𝑣) = 1.

Exercise 18.2. Prove that for every weighted graph 𝐺 there is a spanning tree 𝑇
such that 𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣) ≤ (𝑛 − 1)𝑑𝐺 (𝑢, 𝑣).

    Alon et al [13], motivated by applications of spanning tree based metric
approximations, noticed that if we are allowed to pick a probability distribution
over spanning trees then the expected distance for any pair of vertices can be
much better than the above worst-case example shows. For instance, in the cycle,
if we pick a spanning tree at random from the 𝑛 trees of the cycle then for any
given pair (𝑢, 𝑣) the expected distance is only 2! This kind of guarantee suffices
for many applications. [13] showed that for any weighted graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸)
there is a distribution 𝒟 over spanning trees of 𝐺 such that for any 𝑢, 𝑣,
                                     p
                  𝐸[𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣)] ≤ exp( log 𝑛 log log 𝑛) · 𝑑𝐺 (𝑢, 𝑣)

We think of this as probabilistic approximation of a graph metric by tree metrics.
This can be viewed also as a metric embedding result. Note that the distances
only increase so we are interested in the worst-case guarantee of how much the
expected distance for any pair increases? In keeping with metric embedding
terminology this quantity is often called distortion or stretch.
    Alon et al proved a lower bound of Ω(log 𝑛) on the distortion required for
probabilistic tree approximation and conjectured that this is the tight bound.
It turned out to be quite difficult to obtain even a poly-logarithmic bound. It
was finally shown by Elkin et al. [95], and currently the best known bound
is 𝑂(log 𝑛 log log 𝑛) [1]. Removing the log log 𝑛 factor is an interesting and
non-trivial open problem (or proving a stronger lower bound than Ω(log 𝑛)).
    Bartal [38] overcame the difficulty of spanning tree embeddings by consid-
ering metric embeddings. What does this mean? Instead of starting with the
topology of the graph 𝐺 we work with the metric completion (𝑉 , 𝑑) and view
it as a complete graph on 𝑉. Any spanning tree of the complete graph is now
allowed. Note that such a tree may not correspond to a spanning tree of the
original graph 𝐺; for instance consider the cycle 𝐶 and its metric completion.
As Bartal noted, it can have additional vertices.
CHAPTER 18. LOW DIAMETER DECOMPOSITION AND METRIC TREE EMBEDDINGS244

Definition 18.3. A tree (𝑉𝑇 , 𝐸𝑇 ) with edge lengths ℓ𝑇 : 𝐸 → ℝ+ is a dominating
tree metric for a finite metric (𝑉 , 𝑑) if 𝑉 ⊆ 𝑉𝑇 and for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉, 𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣) ≥ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)
where 𝑑𝑇 is the metric induced on 𝑉𝑇 by the edge lengths of 𝑇.
    Bartal considered approximating metrics probabilistically by dominating
tree metrics.
Definition 18.4. Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a metric space. A probabilistic approximation of (𝑉 , 𝑑)
by dominating tree metrics is a probability distribution 𝒟 over a collection of trees
𝑇1 , 𝑇2 , . . . , 𝑇ℎ if each 𝑇𝑖 is a dominating tree metric for (𝑉 , 𝑑). The distribution has
stretch/distortion 𝛼 if for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉,
                                𝐸𝒟 [𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣)] ≤ 𝛼 · 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣).
    Bartal developed LDDs and showed that one can use them to efficiently
sample from a distribution that has stretch 𝑂(log2 𝑛) [38]. He subsequently im-
proved the stretch bound to 𝑂(log 𝑛 log log 𝑛) [37] and this was finally improved
to the optimal 𝑂(log 𝑛) bound by Fakcharaenphol, Talwar and Rao [100] using
the CKR-RnadomPartition procedure. The construction of the trees from LDDs
is quite simple in retrospect and we do the basic construction and then show
how the subtle properties of the CKR procedure yields the optimal bound.

18.5.1     Low stretch trees via LDDs
Bartal’s construction is based a recursive use of LDDs. The high-level idea is
quite simple. Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a metric space with diameter Δ. We use a LDD
with parameter 𝛿 = Δ/2 to randomly partition 𝑉 into clusters 𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉ℎ of
diameter at most Δ/2. We then recursively find a tree for each of the 𝑉𝑖 , say with
root 𝑟 𝑖 . We create a new dummy root 𝑟 and connect each 𝑟 𝑖 to 𝑟 with an edge of
length Δ. The base case is when |𝑉 | = 1 when we simply return a tree with the
root as the lone vertex. That’s it! We can easily prove that this yields a stretch of
𝑂(log 𝑛 log Δ) (here we assume that the minimum distance is at least 1) which
depends on the aspect ratio but this can be easily altered to get stretch 𝑂(log2 𝑛).
To be more formal, especially for the next section, we use the notion of levels
and use 𝛿 = Δ/2𝑖 at level 𝑖 to make the analysis cleaner.
         TreeEmbed:((𝑉 , 𝑑), 𝐷)
           if |𝑉 | = 1 then
                 return tree with single vertex 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉 as root
           Create tree 𝑇 with root 𝑟
           Use LDD with on (𝑉 , 𝑑) with 𝛿 = 𝐷/2 to obtain clusters 𝑉1 , 𝑉2 , . . . , 𝑉ℎ
           For 𝑗 = 1 to ℎ
                 Let 𝑇𝑗 be output of TreeEmbed((𝑉1 , 𝑑), 𝐷/2) with root 𝑟 𝑗
                 Connect 𝑇𝑗 to 𝑇 by adding edge (𝑟, 𝑟 𝑗 ) of length 𝐷
           return 𝑇 with root 𝑟
