               Figure 18.1: Illustration of recursive tree construction

    We will assume that the minimum non-zero distance in the metric space is 1
by scaling. Let Δ be the diameter of the metric space with this assumption.

Theorem 18.5. Let Δ be the diameter of (𝑉 , 𝑑). The algorithm TreeEmbed((𝑉 , 𝑑), Δ))
outputs a random dominating tree metric 𝑇 = (𝑉𝑇 , 𝐸𝑇 , ℓ𝑇 ) such that for each 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉,
𝐸[𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣)] ≤ 𝑂(𝛼 log Δ)𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) where 𝛼 is the cutting probability of the LDD
procedure.

   The proof is by induction. The base case is easy since the tree has a singleton
and there is nothing to prove. It is easy to see that if we start with 𝐷 = Δ then at
depth 𝑖 of the recursion the parameter is Δ/2𝑖−1 and it is an upper bound on the
diameter of the metric space in that recursive call.
   We omit the proof of the following easy claim.

Claim 18.5.1. The length of the root to leaf path of a tree created at level 𝑖 of the recursion
is at most 𝑗≥𝑖 Δ/2 𝑗−1 ≤ 2Δ/2𝑖−1 .
          Í

    The following should be easy to see from the construction.
CHAPTER 18. LOW DIAMETER DECOMPOSITION AND METRIC TREE EMBEDDINGS246

Claim 18.5.2. The output of the algorithm is a dominating metric for (𝑉 , 𝑑).

   Fix a pair of vertices 𝑢, 𝑣 with distance 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣).

Claim 18.5.3. Suppose 𝑢 and 𝑣 are first separated at level 𝑖 of the recursion. Then
𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣) ≤ 4Δ/2𝑖−1 .

Proof. Follows from the previous claim.                                          
    The pair 𝑢, 𝑣 are separated in the first level of the recursion due to the LDD
with probability 𝛼𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)/(Δ/2) ≤ 2𝛼𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)/Δ in which case their distance in
the tree is at most 4Δ. Otherwise they are in the same cluster and we can apply
induction. Note that 𝑢 and 𝑣 are definitely separated by level 𝑡 where 𝑡 is the
smallest integer such that Δ/2𝑡+1 < 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣). The depth of the recursion is at most
1 + dlog Δe ≤ 2 log Δ.
    It is easy to unroll the induction and use the preceding claim to obtain:
                            𝑡+1
                            Õ           𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)      Δ
           𝐸[𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣)] ≤         2𝛼        𝑖−1
                                                · (4 𝑖−1 ) ≤ 𝑂(𝛼 log Δ)𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)
                            𝑖=0
                                       (Δ/2 )       2

since the depth of the recursion is 𝑂(log Δ).
Remark 18.3. The tree may require depth log Δ to provide a good approximation.
Note that in general log Δ can be as large as 𝑛 and moreover one cannot expect
the depth of the tree (in terms of number of edges) to be less than log Δ if one
wants reasonable distortion. Consider the metric induced by a path with 𝑛 edges
and edge lengths 2𝑖 , 𝑖 = 1 to 𝑛. In such cases the dependence of the stretch on
log Δ is undesirable. One can alter the algorithm slightly to make the stretch
bound 𝑂(log2 𝑛) as follows. In applying the LDD with parameter 𝛿, we ensure
that any pair 𝑢, 𝑣 such that 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) ≤ 𝛿/𝑛 2 is not cut during the procedure. We
can do this by contracting all such pairs without changing the diameter of the
resulting metric space too much. This will ensure that in the tree construction
process a pair 𝑢, 𝑣 participates in only 𝑂(log 𝑛) levels, and hence the expected
stretch can be bounded by 𝑂(𝛼 log 𝑛).
Hierarchically well-separated trees (HSTs): The reader may have noticed that
the trees constructed by the algorithm have an additional strong property: the
edge lengths at each level are the same and the length from the root to the leaf
go down by a factor of 2 at each level (we can adjust this factor which will of
course impact the stretch factor correspondingly). A tree metric with such a
property is called hierarchically well-separated tree metric and this additional
property can be exploited in algorithms and comes for free in the construction.
CHAPTER 18. LOW DIAMETER DECOMPOSITION AND METRIC TREE EMBEDDINGS247

18.5.2   An optimal construction
[100] showed that if one used the LDD based on the CKR-RandomPartition in
the TreeEmbed algorithm then the expected stretch is 𝑂(log 𝑛) which is optimal!
We recall the guarantee of the LDD which is refined one.

Lemma 18.8. The probability that 𝑢 and 𝑣 are in different clusters is at most
4𝑑(𝑢,𝑣)       |𝐵(𝑢,𝛿)|
   𝛿    log( |𝐵(𝑢,𝛿/8)| ).

   Thus, the LDD guarantee is no longer uniform but depends on the scale 𝛿.
We plug this refined bound into the previous expression for the stretch.

                     𝑡+1
                     Õ               |𝐵(𝑢, Δ/2𝑖 )| 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)         Δ
   𝐸[𝑑𝑇 (𝑢, 𝑣)] ≤          2 log(                  )          · (4 𝑖−1 )

                 ≤ 𝑂(log 𝑛)𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣).


Lower bounds: How can we prove that the 𝑂(log 𝑛) bound for LDDs and tree
embeddings are near optimal (modulo precise constant factors)? One can use
existence of low-girth graphs which are closely tied to expanders in a direct
fashion — see Gupta’s notes. Another way is via indirection. In the previous
lecture we saw that the integrality gap of the LP relaxation for Multicut is
Ω(log 𝑘). Note that we prove an upper bound of 𝑂(log 𝑘) essentially via an LDD.
In fact if 𝛼 is the factor for LDD then we get an 𝑂(𝛼) approximation for Mulitcut
via the LP. Thus, one sees that 𝛼 = Ω(log 𝑛) for general metrics (in the integrality
gap example for Multicut 𝑘 = Ω(𝑛 2 )). One can use similar approach to prove
lower bound for tree embeddings.
Fast Algorithms for LDDs and Tree Embeddings: We focused on the quality
of LDDs and tree embeddings but not so much on the running times. It is easy to
see that the algorithms themselves can be implemented in polynomial-time. The
main computation is about shortest paths and if one computes all-pairs-shortest
paths (APSP) then the algorithms are pretty simple. However APSP is slow;
takes 𝑂(𝑚𝑛) time. It is possible compute LDDs and metric tree embeddings in
close to linear time on a weighted graph with 𝑚 edges. This involves computing
approximate shortest paths and several tricks and sometimes we give up on the
quality of the approximation by logarithmic factors. See [42] for an 𝑂(𝑚 log 𝑛)
time algorithm for finding an 𝑂(log 𝑛) stretch (in expectation) tree.
Chapter 19

Sparsest Cut

Sparsest Cut is a fundamental problem in graph algorithms with many appli-
cations and connections. There are several variants that are considered in the
literature and they are closely related but it is useful to have proper terminology
and understand the similarities and differences.
Non-Uniform Sparsest Cut: We consider the general one first. The input is
a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) with non-negative edge capacities 𝑐 : 𝐸 → ℝ+ and a set of
pairs (𝑠1 , 𝑡1 ), ..., (𝑠 𝑘 , 𝑡 𝑘 ) along with non-negative demand values 𝐷1 , 𝐷2 , . . . , 𝐷 𝑘 .
When considering undirected graphs the demand pairs are unordered — by this
we mean that we do not distinguish (𝑠 1 , 𝑡1 ) from (𝑡1 , 𝑠1 ). One can also think of
the demand values as “weights” but the demand terminology makes more sense
when considering the dual flow problem. Given a set/cut 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉 the sparsity of
                                                𝑐(𝛿(𝑆))
the cut 𝑆 is defined as the ratio (Í                    𝐷𝑖 )
                                                             . The numerator is the capacity of
                                         𝑖:|𝑆∩{𝑠 𝑖 ,𝑡 𝑖 }|=1
the cut and the denominator is the total demand of the pairs separated by 𝑆. The
goal is to find the cut 𝑆 with minimum sparsity. In other words we are trying to
find the best “bang per buck” cut: how much capacity do we need to remove per
amount of demand separated? It is sometime convenient to consider 𝐺 as the
supply graph and the demands as forming a demand graph 𝐻 = (𝑉 , 𝐹) where 𝐹
represents the pairs and we associate 𝐷 : 𝐹 → ℝ+ to represent the demand value
(alternatively we can also consider multigraphs). With this representation of the
                                              𝑐(𝛿 (𝑆))
demand pairs, the sparsity of cut 𝑆 is simply 𝐷(𝛿𝐺𝐻 (𝑆)) note that 𝛿 𝐺 (𝑆) represents
the supply edges crossing 𝑆 and 𝛿 𝐻 (𝑆) represents the demand edges crossing
the cut 𝑆.
Remark 19.1. One can define a cut as removing a set of edges. This may lead to
more than two components. In the case of sparsest cut in undirected graphs it
suffices to restrict attention to cuts of the form 𝛿(𝑆) for some 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉. It is a useful
exercise to see why there is always a sparsest cut of that form for any given


                                                  248
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                           249

instance. This is not necessarily true for directed graphs or even in undirected
graphs with node-weights or in hypergraphs.
Uniform Sparsest Cut: Very often when people say Sparsest Cut they mean the
uniform version. This is the version in which 𝐷(𝑢, 𝑣) = 1 for each unordered pair
                                                  𝑐(𝛿 𝐺 (𝑆))
of vertices (𝑢, 𝑣). For these demands a cut 𝑆 is |𝑆||𝑉\𝑆|    . Alternatively the demand
graph 𝐻 is a complete graph with unit demand values on each edge. A slight
generalization of Uniform Sparsest Cut is obtained by considering demands
induced by weights on vertices (the dual flow instances are called Product
Multicommodity Flow instances). There is a weight function 𝜋 : 𝑉 → ℝ+ on
the vertices and demand 𝐷(𝑢, 𝑣) for pair (𝑢, 𝑣) is set to be 𝜋(𝑢)𝜋(𝑣). Note that
if 𝜋(𝑢) = 1 for all 𝑢 then we obtain Uniform Sparsest Cut. If 𝜋(𝑢) ∈ {0, 1}
for all 𝑢 then we are focusing our attention on sparsity with respect to the set
𝑉 0 = {𝑣 | 𝜋(𝑣) = 1} since the vertices with 𝜋(𝑢) = 0 play no role. This may seem
unnatural at first but it is closely connected to expansion and conductance as we
will see below.
Expansion: The expansion of a multi-graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) is defined as
               |𝛿(𝑆)|
min𝑆:|𝑆|≤|𝑉 |/2 |𝑆| . Recall that 𝐺 is an 𝛼-expander if the expansion of 𝐺 is at least
𝛼. A random 3-regular graph is 𝛼-expander with 𝛼 = Ω(1) with high probability.
Thus, to find an 𝛼-expander one can obtain an efficient randomized algorithm
by picking a random graph and then verifying its expansion. However, checking
expansion is coNP-Hard. Expansion is closely related to Uniform Sparsest Cut.
Note that when |𝑆| ≤ |𝑉 |/2 we have

                         1 |𝛿(𝑆)|     |𝛿(𝑆)|      2 |𝛿(𝑆)|
                                  ≤            ≤           .
                        |𝑉 | |𝑆|    |𝑆||𝑉 \ 𝑆|   |𝑉 | |𝑆|

Thus Expansion and Uniform Sparsest Cut are within a factor of 2 of each other.
Sometimes it is useful to consider expansion with vertex weights 𝑤 : 𝑉 →
ℝ+ . Here the expansion is defined as min𝑆:𝑤(𝑆)≤𝑤(𝑉)/2 |𝛿(𝑆)|
                                                           𝑤(𝑆)
                                                                . This corresponds
to product multicommodity flow instances where 𝜋(𝑣) = 𝑤(𝑣). The term
                                                 |𝛿(𝑆)|                 Í
Conductance is used to denote the quantity vol(𝑆) where vol(𝑆) = 𝑣∈𝑆 deg(𝑣)
(here vol is short for volume). When a graph is regular the definition of expansion
and conductance are the same but not in the general setting. Note that we
can capture conductance by setting weights on vertices where 𝑤(𝑣) = deg(𝑣).
Conductance arises naturally when considering random walks in graphs. The
stationary distribution of a random walk in an undirected graph (we will assume
the graph is connected and not bipartite) is the vector 𝑝¯ where the probability
of being in vertex 𝑣 is 𝑝(𝑣) = deg(𝑣)/(2𝑚). How fast does the random walk
converge to the stationary distribution? This is governed by the conductance.
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                         250

Some key applications: Uniform Sparsest Cut is fundamentally interesting
because it helps us directly and indirectly solve the Balanced Separator problem.
In the latter problem we want to partition 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) into two pieces 𝐺1 = (𝑉1 , 𝐸1 )
and 𝐺2 = (𝑉2 , 𝐸2 ) where |𝑉1 | and |𝑉2 | are roughly the same size so that we
minimize the number of edges between 𝑉1 and 𝑉2 . One can repeatedly use
a sparse cut routine to get a balanced separator. The other key application is
to certify expansion of a graph. Expander graphs and relatives arise in many
applications and knowing whether a graph is expanding or not is very useful.


19.1    LP Relaxation and Maximum Concurrent Flow
How do we write an LP relaxation for Sparsest Cut? This is less obvious than it
is for Multicut and other cut problems where we have explicit terminal pairs
that we wish to separate. We consider Non-Uniform Sparsest Cut. First we
develop an integer program. We have two sets of variables. For each pair (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 )
we have a variable 𝑦 𝑖 to indicate whether we want to separate the pair 𝑖. For
each edge we have a variable 𝑥 𝑒 to indicate whether 𝑒 is cut. If we decide to
separate pair 𝑖 then for every path between 𝑠 𝑖 and 𝑡 𝑖 we should cut at least one
edge on the path — this is similar to relaxations we have seen before. We let
𝒫𝑠 𝑖 ,𝑡 𝑖 be the set of all 𝑠 𝑖 -𝑡 𝑖 paths. The following captures the problem:


                                 𝑐𝑒 𝑥𝑒

                                𝑒∈𝑝

                                      𝑦𝑖   ∈   {0, 1}   𝑖 ∈ [𝑘]

    Note, however, that the objective is a ratio and not linear. It is a standard
trick to obtain an LP relaxation wherein we normalize the denominator in the
ratio to 1 and relax the variables to be real-valued. Thus we obtain the following
LP relaxation.

    The dual LP is a multicommodity flow. It solves the Maximum Concurrent
Multicommodity Flow problem for the given instance. It finds the largest value
of 𝜆 such that there is a feasible multicommodity flow for the given pairs in
which the flow routed for pair (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ) is at least 𝜆𝐷𝑖 . It is called concurrent
flow since we need to route all demand pairs to the same factor which is in
contrast to the dual of Multicut which corresponds to the maximum throughput
multicommodity flow (in which some pairs may have zero flow while others
have a lot of flow).

Flow-cut gap: The flow-cut gap in this context is the following equivalent way
of thinking about the problem. Consider a multicommodity flow instance on 𝐺
with demand pairs (𝑠 1 , 𝑡1 ), . . . , (𝑠 𝑘 , 𝑡 𝑘 ) and demand values 𝐷1 , . . . , 𝐷 𝑘 . Suppose
𝐺 satisfies the cut-condition, that is, for every 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉 the capacity 𝑐(𝛿(𝑆)) is at least
the demand separated by 𝑆. Can we route all the demand pairs? This is true
when 𝑘 = 1 but is not true in general even for 𝑘 = 3 in undirected graphs. The
question is the maximum value of 𝜆 such that we can route 𝜆𝐷𝑖 for every pair 𝑖?
The worst-case integrality gap of the preceding LP relaxation for Sparsest Cut is
precisely the flow-cut gap. One can ask about the flow-cut gap for all graphs, a
specific class of graphs, for a specific class of demand graphs, a specific class of
supply and demand graphs, and so on.
     In these notes we will establish that the flow-cut gap in general undirected
graphs is at most 𝑂(log 𝑘). And there are instances where the gap is Ω(log 𝑘)
which are uniform instances — in fact the same expander based example we
saw for Multicut shows that the gap is Ω(log 𝑛) even for Uniform Sparsest Cut.
                p that the gap is 𝑂(1) for planar graphs but the best upper bound
It is conjectured
we have is 𝑂( log 𝑛). Resolving the flow-cut gap in planar graphs is a major
open problem.
Exercise 19.3. Use the expander construction that we saw for Multicut to show
that the flow-cut gap for Uniform Sparsest Cut can be Ω(log 𝑛).
Remark 19.2. Approximating the Sparsest Cut problem is not the same as
establishing flow-cut gaps. One can obtain improved approximations for Sparsest
Cut via stronger relaxationspthan the natural LP. Indeed the best approximation
ratio for Sparsest Cut is 𝑂( log 𝑛) via an SDP relaxation.


19.2      Rounding the LP via Connection to Multicut
There are close connections between Sparsest Cut and Multicut. By repeatedly
using Sparsest Cut routine and Set Cover style analysis prove the following.
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                                      253

Exercise 19.4. Suppose there is an 𝛼(𝑘, 𝑛)-approximation for Non-Uniform
Sparsest Cut. Prove that this implies an 𝑂(𝛼(𝑘, 𝑛) ln 𝑘)-approximation for
Multicut.

    Can we prove some form a converse? That is, can we use an approximation
algorithm for Multicut to obtain an approximation algorithm for Sparsest Cut?
Note that if someone told us the pairs to separate in an optimum solution to
the Sparsest Cut instance then we can use an (approximation) algorithm for
Multicut to separate those pairs. Here we show that one can use information
from the LP solution to figure out which pairs to separate. We sketch the
argument and focus our attention on the simpler case when 𝐷𝑖 = 1 for all 𝑖 ∈ [𝑘].
We give this argument even though it does not lead to the optimum ratio, for
historical interest, as well as to illustrate a useful high-level technique that has
found applications in other settings.
Identifying the pairs to separate from LP solution: Suppose we solve the LP
and obtain a feasible solution (𝑥, 𝑦). 𝑦 𝑖 indicates the extent to which pair 𝑖 is
separated. Suppose we have an ideal situation where 𝑦 𝑖 ∈ {0, 𝑝} for every 𝑖.
Let 𝐴 = {𝑖 | 𝑦 𝑖 = 𝑝}. We have |𝐴| = 1/𝑝 since 𝑖 𝑦 𝑖 = 1. Then it is intutively
                                                         Í
clear that the LP is separating the pairs in 𝐴. We can then solve the Multicut
problem for the pairs in 𝐴 and consider the ratio of the cost of the cut to |𝐴|.
How do we argue about this algorithm? We do the following. Consider a
fractional assignment 𝑥 0 : 𝐸 → ℝ+ where 𝑥 0𝑒 = min{1, 𝑥 𝑒 /𝑝}; in other words
we scale each 𝑥 𝑒 by 1/𝑝. Note that 𝑦 𝑖 = 𝑑 𝑥 (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ). Since we scaled up each 𝑥 𝑒
by 1/𝑝 it is not hard to see that 𝑑 𝑥0 (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ) ≥ 1; in other words 𝑥 0 is a feasible
solution to the Multicut instance on 𝐺 for the pairs in 𝐴. The fractional cost of
𝑥 0 is 𝑒 𝑐 𝑒 𝑥 0𝑒 ≤ 𝑒 𝑐 𝑒 𝑥 𝑒 /𝑝. Thus, by the algorithm for Multicut in the previous
      Í              Í
chapter, we can find a feasible Multicut 𝐸0 ⊆ 𝐸 that separates all pairs in 𝐴 and
𝑐(𝐸0) = 𝑂(log 𝑘) 𝑒 𝑐 𝑒 𝑥 𝑒 /𝑝. What is the sparsity of this cut? It is 𝑐(𝐸0)/|𝐴| which
                      Í
is 𝑂(log 𝑘) 𝑒 𝑥 𝑒 . Thus the sparsity of the cut is 𝑂(log 𝑘)𝜆 where 𝜆 is the value
              Í
of the LP relaxation.
     Now we consider the general setting. Recall that 𝑖 𝑦 𝑖 = 1. We partition

(1/2 𝑗+1 , 1/2 𝑗 ]. Thus all pairs in 𝐴 𝑗 have a 𝑦 𝑖 value that are within a factor of 2 of
each other.

Remark 19.3. When demands are not 1 (or identical) the preceding argument

19.3      Rounding via ℓ1 embeddings
Leighton and Rao [189], in their seminal work, obtained an 𝑂(log 𝑛) approxima-
tion and flow-cut gap for Uniform Sparsest Cut and showed many applications.
They also showed a lower bound of Ω(log 𝑛) on the flow-cut gap for Uniform
Sparsest Cut via expanders which was also an important connection. This led
to 𝑂(log2 𝑛)-approximation for Sparsest Cut and it was an open problem to
obtain a tight conjectured bound of 𝑂(log 𝑛). The optimal rounding of the LP
relaxation turns out to go via metric embedding theory and this connection was
discovered by Linial, London and Rabinovich [194] and Aumann and Rabani
[26]. We need some basics in metric embeddings to point out the connection
and rounding. Even though the metric embedding machinery is powerful, it
can seem like magic. The more basic ideas for Uniform Sparsest Cut based on
region growing is useful to know. One can find the details in [189] or in a more
accessible form in the book of Williamson and Shmoys [255].

19.3.1     A digression through trees
It is instructive to consider the simple setting when 𝐺 is a tree 𝑇 = (𝑉 , 𝐸). In this
case it is easy to find the sparsest cut. For each edge 𝑒 ∈ 𝑇 we can associate a
cut 𝑆 𝑒 which is one side of the two components in 𝑇 − 𝑒. The capacity of the cut
𝛿(𝑆 𝑒 ), by definition, is 𝑐 𝑒 . Let 𝐷(𝑒) = 𝑖:|𝑆𝑒 ∪{𝑠 𝑖 ,𝑡 𝑖 }|=1 𝐷𝑖 be the demand separated
                                           Í
by 𝑒. The sparsity of the cut 𝑆 𝑒 is simply 𝑐 𝑒 /𝐷𝑒 . Finding sparsest cut in a tree is
easy from the following exercise.

    What made the proof work for trees? Is there a more general phenomenon
than the fact that trees are pretty simple structures? It turns out that the key fact
is that shortest path distances induced by a tree are ℓ1 metrics or equivalently
cut metrics.

19.3.2    Cut metrics, line metrics, and ℓ1 metrics
Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a finite metric space. We will be interested in two special types of
metrics.
Definition 19.1. Let 𝑉 be a finite set and let 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉. The metric 𝑑𝑆 associated with the
cut 𝑆 is the following: 𝑑𝑆 (𝑢, 𝑣) = 1 if |𝑆 ∩ {𝑢, 𝑣}| = 1 and 𝑑𝑆 (𝑢, 𝑣) = 0 otherwise.
Definition 19.2. Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a finite metric space. The metric 𝑑 is a cut metric if
there is a set 𝑆 ⊂ 𝑉 such that 𝑑 = 𝑑𝑆 . 𝑑 is in the cut cone (or in the cone of cut metrics)
if there exist non-negative scalars 𝑦𝑆 , 𝑆 ⊂ 𝑉 such that 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) = 𝑆⊂𝑉 𝑦𝑆 𝑑𝑆 (𝑢, 𝑣) for
                                                                      Í
all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉.
Definition 19.3. Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a finite metric space. The metric 𝑑 is a line metric if
there is a mapping 𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ (the real line) such that 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) = | 𝑓 (𝑢) − 𝑓 (𝑣)| for all
𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉.
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                                    256

Definition 19.4. Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a finite metric space. The metric 𝑑 is an ℓ 1 metric1 if
there is some integer 𝑑 and a mapping 𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ 𝑑 (Euclidean space in 𝑑 dimensions)
such that 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) = | 𝑓 (𝑢) − 𝑓 (𝑣)| 1 (the ℓ 1 distance) for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉.

Claim 19.3.1. A metric (𝑉 , 𝑑) is an ℓ 1 metric iff it is a non-negative combination of
line metrics (in the cone of line metrics).

Proof Sketch. If 𝑑 is an ℓ1 metric then each dimension corresponds to a line metric
and since the ℓ 1 metric is separable over the dimensions it is a non-negative
combination of line metric. Conversely, any non-negative combination of line
metrics can be made into an ℓ1 metric where each line metric becomes a separate
dimension (scalar multiplication of a line metric is also a line metric).        
Lemma 19.2. 𝑑 is an ℓ1 metric iff 𝑑 is in the cut cone.

Proof. Consider the metric 𝑑𝑆 . It is easy to see that it is a simple line metric. Map
all vertices in 𝑆 to 0 and all vertices in 𝑉 − 𝑆 to 1. If 𝑑 is in the cut cone then it is
a non-negative combination of the cut metrics, and hence it is a non-negative
combination of line metrics, and hence an ℓ 1 metric.
     To prove the converse, it suffices to argue that any line metric is in the
cut cone. Let 𝑉 = {𝑣 1 , 𝑣2 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 } and let 𝑑 be a line metric on 𝑉. Without
loss of generality assume that the coordinates of the points corresponding to
the line metric 𝑑 are 𝑥 1 ≤ 𝑥 2 ≤ . . . ≤ 𝑥 𝑛 on the real line. For 1 ≤ 𝑖 < 𝑛 let
𝑆 𝑖 = {𝑣 1 , 𝑣2 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑖 }. It is not hard to verify that 𝑛−1
                                                                𝑖=1 |𝑥 𝑖+1 − 𝑥 𝑖 |𝑑𝑆 𝑖 = 𝑑.
                                                              Í
                                                                                            

19.3.3    Brief introduction to metric embeddings
Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a finite metric space. Note that any finite metric space can be
viewed as one that is derived from the shortest path metric induced on a graph
with some non-negative edge lengths. If 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) is a simple graph and
ℓ : 𝐸 → ℝ+ are some edge-lengths, the metric induced on 𝑉 depends both on the
“topology” of 𝐺 as well as the lengths. Finite metrics can encode graph structure
and hence can be diverse. When trying to round we may want to work with
simpler metric spaces. One way to do this is to embed a given metric space (𝑉 , 𝑑)
into a simpler host metric space (𝑉 0 , 𝑑0). An embedding is simply a mapping
of 𝑉 to 𝑉 0. Even though we may be interested in finite metric spaces, the host
metric space can be continuous/infinite such as the Euclidean space in some
dimension 𝑑. Embedding typically distorts the distances and thus one wants
to find embeddings with small distortion. We will focus on relative notion of
    1We define ℓ1 metric with respect to finite dimensional embeddings. Technically we can
allow infinite dimensional embeddings but they are not needed for finite metrics. Moreover, it is
algorithmically more useful to confine attention to finite dimensional embeddings.
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                                257

distortion; additive notions are also explored in the literature although they are
very restrictive due to lack of scale invariance.
Definition 19.5. An embedding of a finite metric space (𝑉 , 𝑑) to a host metric space
(𝑉 0 , 𝑑0) is a mapping 𝑓 : 𝑉 → 𝑉 0. The embedding is an isometric embedding
if 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) = 𝑑0( 𝑓 (𝑢), 𝑓 (𝑣)) for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉. An embedding is a contraction if
𝑑0( 𝑓 (𝑢), 𝑓 (𝑣)) ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉. An embedding is non-contracting if
𝑑0( 𝑓 (𝑢), 𝑓 (𝑣)) ≥ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉.
Definition 19.6. Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) and (𝑉 0 , 𝑑0) be two metric spaces and let 𝑓 : 𝑉 → 𝑉 0 be
                                                               𝑑(𝑢,𝑣)      𝑑0 ( 𝑓 (𝑢), 𝑓 (𝑣))
an embedding. The distortion of 𝑓 is max𝑢,𝑣∈𝑉 ,𝑢≠𝑣 max{ 𝑑0( 𝑓 (𝑢), 𝑓 (𝑣)) , 𝑑(𝑢,𝑣) }.

    Of particular importance are embeddings of finite metric spaces into Eu-
clidean space ℝ 𝑑 where the distance in the host space is measured under a norm.
Examples include ℓ 1 , ℓ2 , ℓ∞ . An embedding of a finite metric space (𝑉 , 𝑑) into ℝ 𝑑
means that we map each 𝑣 to a point (𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑑 ) and the distance between
say 𝑥, 𝑦 is measured as 𝑥 − 𝑦 for some norm of interest.
    The dimension 𝑑 is also important in various applications but in some settings
like with Sparsest Cut the dimension is not important.
Theorem 19.7 (Bourgain). Any 𝑛-point finite metric space can be embedded into ℓ1
with distortion 𝑂(log 𝑛). Moreover the embedding can be chosen to be a contraction,
can be constructed in randomized polynomial time, and maps points into ℝ 𝑑 where
𝑑 = 𝑂(log2 𝑛). There is also an ℓ2 version of the theorem with the same asymptotic
distortion.
    In fact one can obtain a refined theorem that is useful for Sparsest Cut.
Theorem 19.8 (Bourgain). Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be 𝑛-point finite metric and let 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉 with
|𝑆| = 𝑘. Then there is a randomized polynomial time algorithm to compute an
                               2
embedding 𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ 𝑂(log 𝑛) such that (i) the embedding is a contraction (that is,
  𝑓 (𝑢) − 𝑓 (𝑣) 1 ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉 and (ii) for every 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑆, 𝑓 (𝑢) − 𝑓 (𝑣) 1 ≥
  𝑐
log 𝑘 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) for some universal constant 𝑐.

Remark 19.4. Theorem 19.8 is stronger than what is needed for the sparsest-cut
application. To obtain an 𝑂(log 𝑘)-approximation it would suffice to have a single
line embedding 𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ that preserves, up to an 𝑂(log 𝑘) factor, the weighted
                                      Í𝑘
sum of the 𝑘 terminal-pair distances 𝑖=1  𝐷𝑖 𝑑(𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ). We return to this point in
Exercise 19.10.

19.3.4    Utilizing the ℓ1 embedding
We saw that the integrality gap of the LP is 1 on trees since the shortest path
metric on trees is in the cut cone (equivalently ℓ 1 -embeddable). More generally
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                              258

one can prove that if the shortest path metric on a graph 𝐺 embeds into ℓ 1 with
distortion 𝛼 then the integrality gap of the LP for Sparsest Cut is at most 𝛼. This
will imply an 𝑂(log 𝑛)-integrality gap via Bourgain’s theorem since any 𝑛 point
finite metric embeds in to ℓ1 with distortion 𝑂(log 𝑛).
Theorem 19.9. Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) be a graph. Suppose any finite metric induced by edge
lengths on 𝐸 can be embedded into ℓ 1 with distortion 𝛼. Then the integrality gap of the
LP for Sparsest Cut is at most 𝛼 for any instance on 𝐺.

Thus there is a cut whose sparsity is at most 𝛼 · 𝜆.                                   
Polynomial-time algorithm: How do we fine a sparse cut? The preceding
proof used the embedding of 𝑑 into the cut-cone. The proof shows that one of the
cuts with 𝑧 𝑆 > 0 has sparsity at most 𝛼 · 𝜆. Recall the proof that a metric is in the
cut-cone iff it is ℓ1 -embeddable. That argument shows the following. Suppose
we have an ℓ1 embedding into 𝑑-dimensions. Each dimension corresponds
to a line-embedding. Each line embedding is in the cut-cone with only 𝑛 − 1
cuts used to express it. Thus, given an ℓ 1 embedding into 𝑑 dimensions with
distortion 𝛼 we only need to try 𝑑(𝑛 − 1) cuts and one of them will be guaranteed
to have sparsity at most 𝛼 · 𝜆.
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                              259

    Via Theorem 19.8 we can obtain an 𝑂(log 𝑘) randomized approximation and
the algorithm is described below.

  SparseCutviaEmbedding

  1. Solve LP relaxation to obtain (𝑥, 𝑦) and metric 𝑑 𝑥 on 𝑉

  2. Use Theorem 19.8 obtain map 𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ 𝑑 where 𝑑 = 𝑂(log2 𝑛)

  3. For 𝑖 = 1 to 𝑑 do

     A. Let 𝑣 𝑗1 , 𝑣 𝑗2 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑗𝑛 be the sorting of 𝑉 according to dimension 𝑖
     B. For ℎ = 1 to 𝑛 − 1 let 𝑆 𝑖,ℎ = {𝑣 𝑗1 , 𝑣 𝑗2 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑗 ℎ }

  4. Among all cuts 𝑆 𝑖,ℎ with 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑑 and 1 ≤ ℎ ≤ 𝑛 − 1 output the one with
     the smallest sparsity.



Exercise 19.6. Show that the described algorithm is a randomized 𝑂(log 𝑘)-
approximation algorithm for Sparsest Cut. You may either use the refined
guarantee in Theorem 19.8, or alternatively use the weighted line-embedding
statement from Exercise 19.10 together with the proof outline in Theorem 19.9.

19.3.5    Line embeddings, ℓ1 embeddings and tree embeddings
Bourgain’s theorem shows that any finite metric space on 𝑛 points embeds
into ℓ 1 with distortion 𝑂(log 𝑛). This can also be derived via probabilistic tree
embeddings because every tree metric embeds into ℓ 1 isometrically (that is,
without any distortion or with distortion 1).

Exercise 19.7. Prove formally that if a finite metric (𝑉 , 𝑑) can be probabilistically
approximated via dominating tree metrics with distortion 𝛼 then it can be
embedded into ℓ 1 with distortion 𝛼. Is the resulting map an expansion or a
contraction?

Remark 19.5. One can also obtain the 𝑂(log 𝑘) bound for Sparsest Cut via tree
embeddings, not only via Bourgain’s ℓ1 embedding. Gupta, Nagarajan, and
Ravi [133] prove a strengthened FRT-style tree embedding theorem tailored to
a designated terminal set 𝑊 ⊆ 𝑉: the sampled tree metric is non-contracting
on pairs in 𝑊, while the expected stretch on all pairs is 𝑂(log |𝑊 |). If we take
𝑊 to be the set of endpoints that appear in the demand pairs, then |𝑊 | ≤ 2𝑘,
and combining this with the fact that the flow-cut gap on trees is 1 yields an
alternative 𝑂(log 𝑘) bound for Sparsest Cut.
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                       260

    For general metrics, tree embeddings provide a more constrained space while
yielding the same worst-case distortion. However, one can ask if ℓ1 embeddings
provide better distortion for concrete graph classes. Indeed this is the case.
For instance consider a ring network (a cycle with capacities). One can prove
that tree embeddings require a distortion 2. However the ring metric can be
isometrically embedded into ℓ1 . Thus, the flow-cut gap on ring networks is 1
which is not obvious.

Exercise 19.8. Prove that the metric on a ring network embeds into ℓ 1 isometri-
cally.

     Planar graphs are an important class of graphs for a number of reasons,
both from a theoretical and applications point of view. They also pave the
way to the class of proper minor closed families of graphs. There is a famous
conjecture that the flow-cut gap in planar graphs is 𝑂(1) [134]. Interestingly,
for tree embeddings there is a lower bound of Ω(log 𝑛) even on the special case
of planar graphs called series parallel graphs. Thus, tree embeddings are not
powerful
      p enough to prove the conjecture. Rao proved that the flow-cut gap
is 𝑂( log 𝑛) via ℓ 1 embeddings, thus separating the general graph case from
planar graph case. For series-parallel graphs we know that the flow-cut gap is a
tight bound of 2 and establishing this tight bound took a fair amount of work.
For Uniform Sparsest Cut, [178] showed that the flow-cut gap in planar graphs
is 𝑂(1). One can show a tight connection between embedebbility into ℓ 1 and
flow-cut gap [134].
     We saw that ℓ 1 embeddings are a non-negative combination of line embed-
dings. A particular type of line-embedding is called a Frechet embedding. Let
(𝑉 , 𝑑) be a metric space and let 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉. Then we can define a mapping 𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ
to the real line where 𝑓 (𝑣) = 𝑑(𝑆, 𝑣), that is the distance of 𝑣 from 𝑆. Note that
all vertices in 𝑆 get mapped to 0. And the mapping is a contraction. Many
results in embeddings into ℓ 𝑝 spaces are based on using Frechet embeddings
in various clever and often highly non-trivial ways. Bourgain’s embedding is,
in particular, based on picking many random sets and combining the resulting
Frechet embeddings. Line embeddings are simple and have substantial power
on their own. Rabinovich [219] defined the following notion of average distortion
for line embeddings.

Definition 19.10. Let (𝑉 , 𝑑) be a metric space and let 𝑤 : 𝑉 × 𝑉 → ℝ+ be a
non-negative symmetric weight/demand function on the vertices. We say that a line
embedding 𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ is has average weighted distortion 𝛼 for some 𝛼 ≥ 1 if
                  Õ                               1Õ
                        𝑤(𝑢𝑣)| 𝑓 (𝑢) − 𝑓 (𝑣)| ≥        𝑤(𝑢𝑣)𝑑(𝑢𝑣).
                   𝑢𝑣
                                                  𝛼 𝑢𝑣
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                        261

   Note that a contraction implies 𝑢𝑣 𝑤(𝑢𝑣)| 𝑓 (𝑢) − 𝑓 (𝑣)| ≤ 𝑢𝑣 𝑤(𝑢𝑣)𝑑(𝑢𝑣) so
                                      Í                                 Í
we are mainly interested in lower bounding the average distances.
   Bourgain’s result in fact implies the following.
Theorem 19.11. For any 𝑛 point metric space (𝑉 , 𝑑) and any weight function 𝑤 :
𝑉 × 𝑉 → ℝ+ there is a line embedding with average weighted distortion 𝑂(log 𝑛).
Remark 19.6. In the sparsest-cut application the weight function is supported
only on the demand pairs. Thus if there are only 𝑘 demand pairs, one expects
a bound depending on 𝑘 rather than on 𝑛. Indeed, by adapting Bourgain’s
proof one can obtain a line embedding whose weighted distortion for these 𝑘
prescribed pairs is 𝑂(log 𝑘); see Exercise 19.10.
   One can use line embeddings with average distortion to prove flow-cut gap.
You should think of the weight 𝑤(𝑢𝑣) as the demand 𝐷(𝑢𝑣) for pair 𝑢𝑣.
Exercise 19.9. Prove that the flow-cut gap for Sparsest Cut is 𝑂(log 𝑛) using
Theorem 19.11.
    For product multicommodity flow the demand 𝐷(𝑢𝑣) is of the form 𝜋(𝑢)𝜋(𝑣).
Rabinovich [219] showed that existing results such as [178] can be inter-
preted as providing line embeddings with low average distortion for these
demands/weights. Line embeddings turn out to be very relevant when consid-
ering node capacities and directed graphs (among other generalizations) where
ℓ1 embedding do not quite work. They are also relevant in spectral methods and
SDP methods. We refer the reader to [104] for the relevance of line embeddings
in node-capacitated settings and their utility beyond node-capacities [71].


19.4    A Proof Sketch of Bourgain’s Theorem
We now sketch Bourgain’s 𝑂(log 𝑛)-distortion embedding theorem in the ℓ1 form
that is most useful for Sparsest Cut. The proof is based on Fréchet embeddings: if
𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉, define
                           𝑓𝑆 (𝑣) = 𝑑(𝑣, 𝑆) := min 𝑑(𝑣, 𝑥).
                                                  𝑥∈𝑆
This is a line embedding 𝑓𝑆 : 𝑉 → ℝ.
Lemma 19.3. For every 𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉, the map 𝑓𝑆 is a contraction:
                   | 𝑓𝑆 (𝑢) − 𝑓𝑆 (𝑣)| ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)         for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉.
Proof. Let 𝑥 ∈ 𝑆 be such that 𝑓𝑆 (𝑣) = 𝑑(𝑣, 𝑥). Then
               𝑓𝑆 (𝑢) ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑥) ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) + 𝑑(𝑣, 𝑥) = 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) + 𝑓𝑆 (𝑣).
Reversing the roles of 𝑢 and 𝑣 gives 𝑓𝑆 (𝑣) ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) + 𝑓𝑆 (𝑢), and hence | 𝑓𝑆 (𝑢) −
𝑓𝑆 (𝑣)| ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣).                                                                 
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                            262

    The proof of Bourgain’s theorem constructs many such contractions at
different scales. Let ℎ = dlog2 𝑛e. For each 𝑡 = 1, . . . , ℎ + 1 we choose a random
subset 𝑆𝑡 ⊆ 𝑉 by including each vertex independently with probability 2−𝑡 .
Then we define
                            1
                   𝐹(𝑣) =     𝑓𝑆1 (𝑣), 𝑓𝑆2 (𝑣), . . . , 𝑓𝑆 ℎ+1 (𝑣) ∈ ℝ ℎ+1 .
                                                                  
                            ℎ
Since each coordinate is a contraction, 𝐹 is also a contraction in ℓ 1 :

                              k𝐹(𝑢) − 𝐹(𝑣)k 1 ≤ 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣).

    The nontrivial part is to show that every pair 𝑢, 𝑣 is separated by a constant
fraction of its distance in expectation.
Choosing the relevant scales. Fix a pair 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉, and let 𝐿 = 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣). For
𝑡 ≥ 0, define 𝜌𝑡 to be the smallest radius 𝑟 such that both balls 𝐵(𝑢, 𝑟) and 𝐵(𝑣, 𝑟)
contain at least 2𝑡 points; let 𝜌0 = 0. Let ℓ be the largest index with 𝜌ℓ < 𝐿/2.
Thus the interval [0, 𝐿/2] is partitioned into the increments

                    𝜌1 − 𝜌0 , 𝜌2 − 𝜌1 , . . . , 𝜌ℓ − 𝜌ℓ −1 , 𝐿/2 − 𝜌ℓ .

How a single scale contributes. The random set 𝑆𝑡 is tailored to detect the 𝑡-th
increment. For 1 ≤ 𝑡 ≤ ℓ , one of the two open balls 𝐵◦ (𝑢, 𝜌𝑡 ) and 𝐵◦ (𝑣, 𝜌𝑡 ) must
contain fewer than 2𝑡 points; assume without loss of generality that 𝐴 = 𝐵◦ (𝑢, 𝜌𝑡 )
does. Let 𝐵 = 𝐵(𝑣, 𝜌𝑡−1 ). By the choice of ℓ , the sets 𝐴 and 𝐵 are disjoint, |𝐴| < 2𝑡 ,
and |𝐵| ≥ 2𝑡−1 . If 𝑆𝑡 ∩ 𝐴 = ∅ but 𝑆𝑡 ∩ 𝐵 ≠ ∅, then every point of 𝑆𝑡 is at distance
at least 𝜌𝑡 from 𝑢, while some point of 𝑆𝑡 is within distance 𝜌𝑡−1 of 𝑣. Hence

                              𝑓𝑆𝑡 (𝑢) − 𝑓𝑆𝑡 (𝑣) ≥ 𝜌𝑡 − 𝜌𝑡−1 .

19.5      SDP Relaxation
Can we obtain a better approximation than 𝑂(log 𝑘) for sparsest cut? Using semi-
definite
      p programming based relaxation, Arora, Rao and Vazirani [21] obtained
an 𝑂( log 𝑛)-approximation for Uniform Sparsest Cut (and more generally
product
      p instances). Building on this work, Arora, Lee and Naor [18, 19] obtained
an 𝑂( log 𝑛 log log 𝑛)-approximation for Sparsest Cut. Currently these are
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                         265

the best approximation algorithms known for these problems. There was a
conjecture that the SDP based relaxation would yield an 𝑂(1)-approximation
but it was shown that the integrality gap is essentially close to Ω( log 𝑛); this
                                                                    p

required substantial mathematical work.


19.6     Spectral Relaxation for Conductance
As we said expander graphs are very important. However, certifying expansion
is co-NP hard. In several applications it is important to obtain constant-degree
expanders with constant expansion. The approximation algorithms we saw are
not useful in this regime. It turns out that there is a very different method based
on spectral graph theory that helps in this regime. For an undirected graph on
𝑛 vertices one can associate a symmetric matrix called the Laplacian ℒ 𝐺 . The
matrix has the following entries. The diagonal entry for 𝑖 is 𝑑𝑒 𝑔(𝑣 𝑖 ). We have
ℒ 𝐺 [𝑖, 𝑗] = ℒ 𝐺 [𝑖, 𝑗] = −1. Since this matrix is symmetric all its eigen values are
real. More over, this matrix is also positive semidefinite (PSD) and hence all
its eigen values are non-negative. Let 0 = 𝜆1 ≤ 𝜆2 ≤ . . . 𝜆𝑛 be its eigen values
(it is not hard to verify that 0 is an eigen value with eigen vector being the all
1’s vector). A well-known and famous result in spectral graph theory is the
following Cheeger’s inequality on the conductance of 𝐺.

                                𝜆2           p
                                    ≤ 𝜙(𝐺) ≤ 2𝜆2
                                 2
    Thus 𝜆2 provides a constant factor approximation for the conductance
when the conductance is a constant! Note that expansion and conductance
are related by the maximum degree and hence when the degree is small and
constant one can use 𝜆2 to certify expansion. Due to its importance for certifying
expansion/conductance, some use 𝜆2 as the definition of expansion since it is
computable and also helps in construction of expanders.


19.7     Node capacities and Directed Graphs
We typically discuss 𝑠-𝑡 cuts and other problems such as Sparsest Cut cut with
edge capacities. One can define cut problems with node capacities; for instance
Menger’s theorem on node disjoint paths is an example. In the 𝑠-𝑡 case it
is typical to reduce the node-capacitated problems to edge capacitated case
when the graph is directed. However this reduction does not apply when the
graph is undirected. When dealing with more complicated problems involving
multicommodity flows and cuts, the directed graph problems often become
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                                    266

harder than their undirected graph counterparts. And thus one has to consider
node-capacitated undirected graph problems separately.
      In the context of Uniform Sparsest Cut, node capacitated cuts are quite
important since they are closely related to the notion of treewidth of a graph. It is
a graph parameter and tree decompositions associated with treewidth play a
fundamental role in graph structure theory and many algorithmic applications.
Treewidth is NP-hard to compute but an 𝛼 approximation for Uniform Sparsest
Cut implies an 𝑂(𝛼)-approximation for treewidth. We think of node separator
as a set of vertices 𝑆 such that 𝐺 − 𝑆 has (at least) two non-trivial components.
Sometimes one uses the terminology (𝐴, 𝑆, 𝐵) for disjoint sets 𝐴, 𝐵, 𝑆 with 𝑆 as
the separation if there is no edge between 𝐴 and 𝐵 (all paths between 𝐴 and
𝐵 goe via 𝑆). The cost/weight of the cut/separation is now the cost/weight
of the separator set 𝑆. When considering sparse cuts and the like there is a bit
of an added complication because 𝑆 itself is a set of vertices which may have
demands associated with them. Handling this requires some care and proper
definitions. The existing algorithms for Uniform Sparsest Cut can be generalized
with additional technical work (one needs to work with line embeddings             p rather
than ℓ 1 embeddings) to handle node capacities. This leads to an 𝑂( log 𝑛)-
approximation for Uniform Sparsest Cut and a corresponding algorithm for
treewidth. See [104].
      Sparsest Cut in directed graphs is more difficult. The natural definition,
motivated by obstructions to routing, is the following. We are given a di-
rected edge-capcitated graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) and a set of 𝑘 ordered demand pairs
(𝑠 1 , 𝑡1 ), . . . , (𝑠 𝑘 , 𝑡 𝑘 ) each with a non-negative demand 𝐷𝑖 . We think of a cut as
a set of edges 𝐸0 ⊆ 𝐸. Removing 𝐸0 disconnects some pairs and the sparsity
is simply 𝑐(𝐸0)/dem(𝐸0) where dem(𝐸0) is the demand separated by 𝐸0. Unlike
undirected graphs minimal 𝐸0 cuts do no induce bipartititions of the vertex set.
It turns out that the flow-cut gap (for Multicut also) can be as large as 𝑘 [228],
and also Ω(𝑛 11/27 [87]. More over, [87] prove hardness of approximation close to
polynomial factors. However, there is an important special case of symmetric
demands that is quite relevant in several problems. Here the demands are
unordered, that is, each demand pair is 𝑠 𝑖 𝑡 𝑖 and we say that a pair is separated by
an edge set 𝐸0 if they are not in the same strongly connected component. Note
that this means that we are asking that at least one of the pairs (𝑠 𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑖 ) or (𝑡 𝑖 , 𝑠 𝑖 ) to
be separated. It turns out that Multicut and Sparsest Cut and Uniform Sparsest
Cut in this formulation have poly-logarithmic approximations and flow-cut gaps.
These problems have also connected to the problem of removing edges/nodes
to delete all cycles (called Feedback problems). We refer the reader to [99] on
flow-cut gaps and to [6, 66] for SDP based algorithms.
      Finally, since it is connected to the work of the instructor, there is a notion of
polymatroid networks which connect network flows to submodular functions.
CHAPTER 19. SPARSEST CUT                                                   267

This comes from classical work in combinatorial optimziation and one can gener-
alize the notions of multicommodity flows and cuts. Several ideas from regular
flows and cuts can be ported over with useful applications and consequences.
See [71] and some recent work on submodular hypergraphs [82].

Bibliographic Notes
The highly influential paper of Leighton and Rao [189] obtained an 𝑂(log 𝑛)-
approximation and flow-cut gap for Uniform Sparsest Cut and introduced the
region growing argument as well as the lower bound via expanders (an important
influence is the paper of Sharokhi and Matula [232]). [189] demonstrated many
applications of the divide and conquer approach. There is a large literature
on Sparsest Cut and related problems and we only touched upon a small part.
An outstanding open problem is whether the flow-cut gap for Non-Uniform
Sparsest Cut in planar graphs is 𝑂(1) (this is called the GNRS conjecture [134]
in the more general context of minor-free graphs); Rao, building on ideas from
[177], showed that the gap is 𝑂( log 𝑛) [224]. No super-constant lower bound
                                p

is known for planar graphs. The theory of metric embeddings has been a
fruitful bridge between TCS and mathematics and there are several surveys and
connections from both perspectives. The argument via Multicut is attributed to
Nabil Kahale — see the chapter by Shmoys on approximation algorithms for cut
problems [235].
Chapter 20

Semidefinite Programming and
Approximation

Semidefinite programming (SDP) is a powerful tool in the design of approxi-
mation algorithms. Many of the approximation algorithms we have seen were
based on LP relaxations. LP is a special case of convex programming. Since
convex programs can also be solved efficiently (modulo some technicalities with
precision), it is natural to wonder whether one can use convex programs in
approximation algorithms. It is not straightforward to use convexity generically
since we also need some clean way to model discrete optimization problems
via an appropriate convex program and then be able to round the resulting
fractional solution. SDP is a special case of convex programming and they turn
out to have a good sweet spot. Nevertheless, the first breakthrough via SDP
in approximation was only in 1994 via the celebrated 0.878-approximation for
Max-Cut by Goemans and Williamson [122]. This was subsequently generalized
to yield an optimum approximation for all Max-CSP problems [222]. Other
well-known approximation results based on SDP include algorithms for graph
coloring duep to Karger, Motwani and Sudan [167] (see [33] for a recent paper)
and the 𝑂( log 𝑛)-approximation for uniform sparsest cut due to Arora, Rao
and Vazirani [22]. The power of SDP in classical combinatorial optimization was
pointed out by Lovász in his famous work on the weak perfect graph theorem
[195] where he introduced the Lovász theta function of a graph.
    This chapter describes some necessary background for SDP-based approx-
imation. We begin with background on convex programming and positive
semidefinite matrices, establish the equivalence between SDPs and vector pro-
grams, and then present several applications: the GW Max-Cut algorithm,
Max-CSPs, graph coloring, metric embeddings, balanced graph partitioning,
and finally the connection between SDPs and sum-of-squares certificates for


                                      268
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION269

polynomial optimization.


20.1     Convex Programming and Semidefinite Matrices
Convex Programs: A convex program has the form
                                  min 𝑓 (𝑥),            𝑥 ∈ 𝑆 ⊆ ℝ𝑛 ,
where 𝑓 : ℝ 𝑛 → ℝ is a convex function and 𝑆 is a convex set. Two ingredients
suffice for efficient optimization:
   • a first-order oracle for 𝑓 : given 𝑥, output 𝑓 (𝑥) and a subgradient 𝑔 ∈ 𝜕 𝑓 (𝑥);
     and
   • a separation oracle for 𝑆: given 𝑥, either certify 𝑥 ∈ 𝑆 or output a hyperplane
     separating 𝑥 from 𝑆.
When both oracles run in polynomial time, the ellipsoid method solves the
convex program to within additive error 𝜀 in time polynomial in the input
size and log(1/𝜀). Linear programs are the canonical example. Beyond LPs,
the most important special case for combinatorial optimization is semidefinite
programming.
Quadratic objectives and matrices:                 A quadratic function on 𝑛 variables is of
the following form:

symmetric matrices naturally arise in considering quadratic functions. Which
quadratic functions are convex? Note that the convexity depends only on the
term 𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 and hence it is the same as asking the following: when is the function
𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 convex for a symmetric matrix 𝐴? It turns out that the answer is when 𝐴
is positive semidefinite (PSD) matrices. We will define them formally and then
revisit convexity aspects.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION270

Eigenvalues and spectral theorem for symmetric matrices: Recall that for a
𝑛 × 𝑛 real-valued matrix 𝐴, a vector 𝑥 is an eigenvector if 𝐴𝑥 = 𝜆𝑥 for some
real number 𝜆. We say that 𝜆 is an eigenvalue. Recall that the eigenvalues of
a matrix are the roots of the univariate polynomial det(𝐴 − 𝜆𝐼) = 0. A matrix
𝐴 may not have (real) eigenvalues. However, symmetric matrices have all real
eigenvalues. The spectral theorem is a very important result in linear algebra
with many applications in applied mathematics.

Theorem 20.1 (Spectral Theorem for Real Symmetric Matrices). Let 𝐴 be a 𝑛 × 𝑛
real symmetric matrix. Then

   • All eigenvalues of 𝐴 are real.

   • There is an orthonormal basis of ℝ 𝑛 consisting of eigenvectors of 𝐴

   • 𝐴 = 𝑉 𝐷𝑉 𝑇 where 𝐷 is a diagonal matrix with eigenvalues of 𝐴 and 𝑉 is an
     orthogonal matrix consisting of eigenvectors of 𝐴.

Positive Semidefinite (PSD) Matrices: A real symmetric 𝑛 × 𝑛 matrix 𝐴 is
positive semidefinite (psd), written 𝐴  0, if any of the following equivalent
conditions holds:

   1. 𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 ≥ 0 for all 𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 ;

   2. all eigenvalues of 𝐴 are real and non-negative;

   3. 𝐴 = 𝑊 𝑇 𝑊 for some real matrix 𝑊 (Cholesky decomposition).

If the inequality in (1) is strict for all 𝑥 ≠ 0, we say 𝐴 is positive definite and write
𝐴  0.

Theorem 20.2. For a real symmetric 𝑛 × 𝑛 matrix 𝐴, conditions (1), (2), and (3) are
equivalent.

Proof. We show (1) ⇒ (2) ⇒ (3) ⇒ (1).
(1) ⇒ (2): Since 𝐴 is real and symmetric, by the spectral theorem all eigenvalues
of 𝐴 are real. Let 𝜆 be any eigenvalue with eigenvector 𝑣 ≠ 0, so 𝐴𝑣 = 𝜆𝑣. Then
𝑣 𝑇 𝐴𝑣 = 𝜆k𝑣 k 2 . By (1), 𝑣 𝑇 𝐴𝑣 ≥ 0, and since k𝑣 k 2 > 0 we conclude 𝜆 ≥ 0.
(2) ⇒ (3): By the spectral theorem, 𝐴 = 𝑈 𝐷𝑈 𝑇 where 𝑈√is orthogonal        √     and
𝐷 = diag(𝜆1 , . . . , 𝜆𝑛 ) with each 𝜆 𝑖 ≥ 0. Let 𝐷 1/2 = diag( 𝜆1 , . . . , 𝜆𝑛 ). Set
𝑊 = 𝐷 1/2𝑈 𝑇 . Then

                       𝑊 𝑇 𝑊 = 𝑈 𝐷 1/2 𝐷 1/2𝑈 𝑇 = 𝑈 𝐷𝑈 𝑇 = 𝐴.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION271

(3) ⇒ (1): If 𝐴 = 𝑊 𝑇 𝑊 then for any 𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 ,

                         𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 = 𝑥 𝑇 𝑊 𝑇 𝑊 𝑥 = k𝑊 𝑥k 2 ≥ 0.

                                                                                   
PSD Matrices and Ellipsoids: We can interpret a 𝑛 × 𝑛 matrix 𝐴 as a linear operator
from ℝ 𝑛 to ℝ 𝑛 . If 𝐴 is psd then 𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 ≥ 0. Letting 𝐴𝑥 = 𝑢 we see that 𝑥 𝑇 𝑢 ≥ 0
is equivalent to the vectors 𝑢 and 𝑥 forming an acute angle; thus 𝐴 does not
rotate 𝑥 by more than 90 degrees. PSD matrices arise naturally when defining
ellipsoids in high dimensions. An ellipsoid is defined as a linear transformation
of the unit ball. Note that the unit ball is the set {𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 | k𝑥 k 2 ≤ 1}. Thus
an ellipsoid is {𝐵𝑥 + 𝑐 | k𝑥k 2 ≤ 1} where 𝑐 is the translation of center, and 𝐵
is a non-singular matrix — we ask for non-singularity so that the ellipsoid is
full-dimensional, otherwise we have to work with the smaller dimension. If we
ignore the translation aspect then an origin centered full-dimensional ellipsoid
is simply {𝐵𝑥 | k𝑥 k 2 ≤ 1} for some non-singular matrix. We note that this set is
the same as {𝑦 | 𝑦 𝑇 𝐴𝑦 ≤ 1} where 𝐴 = (𝐵−1 )𝑇 𝐵−1 ; hence 𝐴 is psd. Note that 𝐵
may not be psd. Conversely the set {𝑦 | 𝑦 𝑇 𝐴𝑦 ≤ 1} for a positive definite matrix
𝐴 defines an ellipsoid (do you see why?). Thus an ellipsoid centered at 𝑐 can
be defined alternatively as {𝑦 | (𝑦 − 𝑐)𝑇 𝑄 −1 (𝑦 − 𝑐) ≤ 1} where 𝑄 is a positive
definite matrix. The reason to use the inverse is because 𝑄 defines the shape of
the ellipsoid: the eigenvectors of 𝑄 are the principal axes of the ellipsoid and
the scaling in each dimension is given by the eigenvalues of 𝑄.
Convexity and PSD Matrices: We discuss two connections between psd ma-
trices and convexity. The first is about the set of all 𝑛 × 𝑛 psd matrices. We
                                                                                𝑛
interpret these matrices as vectors in ℝ 𝑛 . Technically we can work with ℝ( 2 )
                                          2


since we are interested in symmetric matrices but it is convenient to work in the
higher dimensions.

Lemma 20.1. The set 𝒮𝑛+ = {𝐴 ∈ ℝ 𝑛×𝑛 : 𝐴 symmetric, 𝐴  0} is a convex cone.

Proof. If 𝐴, 𝐵  0 and 𝛼, 𝛽 ≥ 0, then for any 𝑥, 𝑥 𝑇 (𝛼𝐴 + 𝛽𝐵)𝑥 = 𝛼𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 + 𝛽𝑥 𝑇 𝐵𝑥 ≥
0.                                                                                  
   The cone 𝒮𝑛+ is a convex subset of ℝ 𝑛 when we view matrices as vectors.
                                           2


   Another connection to convexity of functions is given by the following fact.

Lemma 20.2. Let 𝑓 : 𝐷 → ℝ be smooth convex function over a domain 𝐷 ⊆ ℝ 𝑛 .
Then 𝑓 is convex if and only if its Hessian ∇2 𝑓 (𝑥)  0 for all 𝑥 ∈ 𝐷.

Proof sketch. Convexity requires 𝑓 (𝑦) ≥ 𝑓 (𝑥) + (𝑦 − 𝑥)𝑇 ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥) for all 𝑥, 𝑦. It
suffices to check convexity for all points in their infinitesimal neighborhood
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION272

(one needs an argument for this but can be shown). Suppose 𝑦 is in a small
neighborhood of 𝑥. A second-order Taylor expansion gives 𝑓 (𝑦) ≈ 𝑓 (𝑥) + (𝑦 −
𝑥)𝑇 ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥) + 12 (𝑦 − 𝑥)𝑇 ∇2 𝑓 (𝑥)(𝑦 − 𝑥), so nonnegativity of the Hessian quadratic
form is necessary and sufficient to ensure that 𝑓 (𝑦) ≥ 𝑓 (𝑥) + (𝑦 − 𝑥)𝑇 ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥). 

Corollary 20.3. A quadratic function 𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 is convex over ℝ 𝑛 iff 𝐴 is psd.

Proof. If 𝑓 (𝑥) = 𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 and 𝐴 is symmetric, then ∇2 𝑓 (𝑥) = 2𝐴. By the preceding
lemma, 𝑓 is convex iff ∇2 𝑓 (𝑥) is psd for all 𝑥, which is equivalent to 𝐴  0. 

     The ellipsoid viewpoint is also suggestive. If 𝐴  0, then the sublevel sets
{𝑥 | 𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝜆} are ellipsoids (or degenerate ellipsoids) and hence convex. If 𝐴
has a negative eigenvalue with eigenvector 𝑣, then along the line 𝑥 = 𝑡𝑣 we have
𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥 = 𝑡 2 𝑣 𝑇 𝐴𝑣, which is a concave quadratic in 𝑡 since 𝑣 𝑇 𝐴𝑣 < 0. Thus 𝑥 𝑇 𝐴𝑥
cannot be convex.


20.2     Semidefinite Programming (SDP)
For matrices 𝐴, 𝐵 ∈ ℝ 𝑛×𝑛 , define the inner product 𝐴 · 𝐵 = 𝑖,𝑗 𝐴 𝑖𝑗 𝐵 𝑖𝑗 = tr(𝐴𝑇 𝐵);
                                                               Í

in other words we are treating 𝐴 and 𝐵 as vectors in 𝑛 2 dimensions. An SDP
in standard form maximizes a linear function of a psd matrix subject to linear
constraints:
                        max             𝐶 ·𝑌
                        s.t.     𝐷𝑖 · 𝑌 = 𝑑 𝑖 , 𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑘
                                      𝑌  0, 𝑌 ∈ ℝ 𝑛×𝑛 .
The objective and constraints are linear in the entries of 𝑌; the only nonlinearity
is the psd constraint 𝑌  0, which restricts 𝑌 to a convex set 𝒮𝑛+ . Hence an SDP
is a special case of convex programming.
    We can also allow a minimization objective and inequality constraints
𝐷𝑖 · 𝑌 ≥ 𝑑 𝑖 .
Solvability of SDP via a separation oracle. We can rewrite the SDP as a linear
program but with an infinite number of linear constraints.

                        max          𝐶 ·𝑌
                        s.t. 𝐷𝑖 · 𝑌 = 𝑑 𝑖 , 𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑘
                              𝑣 𝑇 𝑌𝑣 ≥ 0       𝑣 ∈ ℝ𝑛
                               𝑦 𝑖,𝑗 = 𝑦 𝑗,𝑖   1≤𝑖<𝑗≤𝑛
                                 𝑌 ∈ 𝑀𝑛
Here 𝑀𝑛 is the set of all 𝑛 × 𝑛 matrices, indicating that the space has dimension
𝑛 2 . This reformulation allows us to see that the original SDP has an efficient
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION273

separation oracle so that we can apply the ellipsoid method. Equivalently, we
wish to exhibit a separation oracle for the convex set 𝒮𝑛+ . Given a matrix 𝐴,
first check symmetry; if not symmetric, the violated constraint 𝑦 𝑖𝑗 = 𝑦 𝑗𝑖 is a
separating hyperplane. Then compute the eigenvalue decomposition of 𝐴: if all
eigenvalues are non-negative then 𝐴  0. Otherwise let 𝑣 be an eigenvector with
eigenvalue 𝜆 < 0; then 𝑣 𝑇 𝐴𝑣 = 𝜆 < 0 and the separating hyperplane is 𝑣 𝑇 𝑌𝑣 ≥ 0.
This oracle runs in polynomial time, so SDPs can be solved in polynomial time
to arbitrary precision.

20.2.1     SDP and Vector Programming
Why are SDPs useful in modeling discrete optimization problems? One im-
portant reason is because they can be recast as solving vector programming
relaxations of quadratic optimization problems. We formalize this below.
    An SDP solution 𝑌  0 can always be written as 𝑌 = 𝑊 𝑇 𝑊 via the Cholesky
decomposition. Let 𝑣 1 , 𝑣2 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 denote the columns of 𝑊; then 𝑌𝑖𝑗 = 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗
with the standard inner product.

Claim 20.2.1. The solution to any SDP with 𝑌 ∈ 𝑀𝑛 is a set of 𝑛 vectors 𝑣 1 , 𝑣2 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 ∈
ℝ 𝑛 such that 𝑌𝑖𝑗 = 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 .
      This observation motivates the vector programming viewpoint: instead of
optimizing over a psd matrix, we optimize directly over a collection of vectors
𝑣 1 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 with an objective and constraints that are linear in the inner
products 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 .
Example 20.1. The following is a valid vector program:

                              max (𝑣 1 · 𝑣 2 + 𝑣2 · 𝑣 3 − 5𝑣1 · 𝑣5 )                    (20.1)
                                  s.t.   𝑣1 · 𝑣2 − 𝑣2 · 𝑣3 = 5                          (20.2)
                                         𝑣1 , 𝑣2 , 𝑣3 ∈ ℝ   3
                                                                                        (20.3)

All constraints are linear in inner products, so this is an SDP.

Claim 20.2.2. Every vector program is an SDP.

Proof. We introduce a variable 𝑌𝑖𝑗 for each dot product 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 and then the
objective and constraints become linear in these variables. Then we add the
constraint that 𝑌  0.                                                       
    The vector programming perspective is useful because it is often more natural
to describe combinatorial problems as programs over vectors than as matrix
programs. We will use both formulations interchangeably.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION274

20.3     SDP for Max-Cut: The GW Algorithm
In the Max-Cut problem we are given an edge-weighted graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) with
weights 𝑤 : 𝐸 → ℝ≥0 . A cut is a partition of 𝑉 into two sets (𝑆, 𝑉 \ 𝑆). The weight
of the cut is 𝑤(𝛿(𝑆)) = 𝑖𝑗∈𝐸, 𝑖∈𝑆, 𝑗∉𝑆 𝑤 𝑖𝑗 . The goal is to find the maximum-weight
                       Í
cut. Another view of the problem is to find the largest weight bipartite subgraph
of a given graph.




Figure 20.1: An example of a max-cut: the red and blue vertices form the
two sides of the partition, and the bold edges crossing the cut are the edges
contributing to the cut weight.

    Max-Cut is NP-hard; it is one of Karp’s original 21 NP-complete problems.
In contrast, Min-Cut is solvable in polynomial time via max-flow. We have seen
that some simple algorithms achieve a 12 -approximation: pick 𝑆 by including
each vertex independently with probability 12 , or run a local search that moves
one vertex at a time into or out of 𝑆 to improve the cut. One can also develop
several LP-based 1/2-approximation algorithms. Despite the simplicity of the
problem it was unclear whether one can obtain a better than 1/2-approximation.
Goemans and Williamson, in a major breakthrough, showed in 1994 that an SDP
relaxation yields a substantially better approximation ratio of 𝛼 ≈ 0.878.
Quadratic Integer Program and Vector Relaxation: Assume 𝑉 = {1, 2, . . . , 𝑛}.
Assign each vertex 𝑖 a variable 𝑦 𝑖 ∈ {−1, +1}, where 𝑦 𝑖 = −1 means 𝑖 ∈ 𝑆 and
𝑦 𝑖 = +1 means 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉 \ 𝑆. An edge (𝑖, 𝑗) is cut if and only if 𝑦 𝑖 ≠ 𝑦 𝑗 , which
happens exactly when 𝑦 𝑖 𝑦 𝑗 = −1, so its contribution to the cut is 𝑤 𝑖𝑗 (1 − 𝑦 𝑖 𝑦 𝑗 )/2.
This gives the following quadratic integer program:
                                      Õ
                              max          𝑤 𝑖𝑗 (1 − 𝑦 𝑖 𝑦 𝑗 )/2
                                      𝑖𝑗∈𝐸
                              s.t.    𝑦 𝑖2 = 1, 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉.

This is NP-hard: solving it exactly is equivalent to Max-Cut.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION275

     To obtain a tractable relaxation, we interpret each scalar 𝑦 𝑖 as a one-
dimensional vector and then relax by allowing 𝑣 𝑖 to be a unit vector in ℝ 𝑛 .
The inner product 𝑦 𝑖 𝑦 𝑗 becomes 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 , and the constraint 𝑦 𝑖2 = 1 becomes
k𝑣 𝑖 k 2 = 1. This yields the vector program:
                                     Õ
                              max           𝑤 𝑖𝑗 (1 − 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 )/2
                                     𝑖𝑗∈𝐸
                              s.t.   𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑖 = 1, 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉
                                     𝑣 𝑖 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 , 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉.

Since this is a relaxation of the original IP (the ±1 vectors form a feasible subset),
the optimum value OPT𝑣 satisfies OPT𝑣 ≥ OPT. Being a vector program, it can
be solved as an SDP in polynomial time.

20.3.1    Rounding: The Random Hyperplane Algorithm
Let 𝑣 1∗ , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛∗ be an optimal solution to the vector program with value OPT𝑣 .
Each 𝑣 ∗𝑖 is a unit vector in ℝ 𝑛 . We produce a cut via the following randomized
rounding procedure.




Figure 20.2: Left: the unit vectors 𝑣 1∗ , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛∗ (one per vertex) on the unit sphere
after solving the SDP. Right: a random hyperplane through the origin divides
the vectors into two groups, defining the cut (𝑆, 𝑉 \ 𝑆).
       CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION276

                   GW-RandomHyperplane:

                    Set 𝑆 = {𝑖 ∈ 𝑉 : 𝑟 · 𝑣 ∗𝑖 ≥ 0}.
                    Output the cut (𝑆, 𝑉 \ 𝑆).

           The intuition is that a random hyperplane through the origin (with normal
       𝑟) partitions the unit sphere into two hemispheres; the cut assigns vertex 𝑖 to the
       hemisphere containing 𝑣 ∗𝑖 .

        𝜃      1 − cos 𝜃                                                2𝜃

    The minimum in Lemma 20.4 is achieved at 𝜃∗ ≈ 2.331 radians; it can be
verified numerically.

a 0.878-approximation for Max-Cut.

Generating a random unit vector: To implement the rounding algorithm one
needs to generate a random unit vector in 𝑛 dimensions. This is not obvious. Here
is one trick to accomplish this. Consider the standard 1-d Normal distribution
                                                                              2
𝑁(0, 1) with mean 0 and standard deviation 1, having density function √1 𝑒 −𝑥 /2 .
                                                                                               2𝜋

  1. Generate a vector 𝑧¯ = (𝑋1 , 𝑋2 , . . . , 𝑋𝑛 ), where each 𝑋𝑖 is an independent
     random variable drawn from 𝑁(0, 1).

  2. The joint density is:

                                                         1         2        2
                            𝑓𝑧¯ (𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑑 ) = √      𝑒 −(𝑥1 +···+𝑥 𝑛 )/2 .
                                                       ( 2𝜋)𝑑

  3. Notice the density is the same for all points (𝑥1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ) with the same
     length, i.e., it depends only on k 𝑧¯ k. Therefore the distribution is centrally
     symmetric, and the direction of 𝑧¯ is uniformly distributed.

  4. Output the unit vector 𝑟¯ = k 𝑧𝑧¯¯ k .
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION278

How does one generate a random number from the standard normal distribution?
This is not completely obvious either. There is a way to use two random
numbers uniformly distributed in (0, 1) to generate two random numbers
distributed according to the Normal distribution via the Box-Muller transform.
See https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Box%E2%80%93Muller_transform. There are
precision issues involved in these computations but the algorithm and analysis
are robust.
Integrality gap and hardness: What is the integrality gap of the SDP? Consider
the cycle 𝐶5 with unit edge weights. It is easy to see that the optimum value is
4. However the SDP can assign vectors to the five vertices such that its value is
4.5225. If 𝐶 = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 then consider the feasible SDP solution which maps
the vertices to the corners of a regular pentagon inscribed inside a unit circle in
the order 1, 4, 2, 5, 3. The angle between the vectors of adjacent vertices on the
cycle is 144 degrees. Thus shows a gap of 0.8845.
    It turns out that the integrality gap of the SDP is exactly 𝛼! This is a non-trivial
result [107] and requires a sophisticated construction. Moreover, under the
Unique Games Conjecture (UGC) of Khot [174], no polynomial-time algorithm
can approximate Max-Cut with ratio better than 𝛼; this is a surprising and deep
result [175] that connects worst-case integrality gaps of SDPs to hardness. In
other words, the GW algorithm is essentially optimal among efficient algorithms
under UGC.
Remark 20.1. The algorithm outputs a good cut in expectation. One can repeat
the rounding a polynomial number of times and keeps the best solution found,
obtaining a cut of expected quality ≥ 0.878 · OPT with high probability. A
deterministic algorithm achieving the same ratio is also known but technically
more involved [198].


20.4     SDP for Constraint Satisfaction Problems
The Max-Cut SDP is the first important example of a much more general
phenomenon: SDPs can capture the geometry of assignments to constraint
satisfaction problems. We illustrate this first for Max-2-SAT and then describe a
generic SDP for arbitrary Max-CSPs.

20.4.1   Warm-Up: Max-2-SAT
In Max-2-SAT we are given Boolean variables 𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 and weighted clauses,
each of which is a disjunction of at most two literals. The goal is to maximize
the total weight of satisfied clauses. If every clause has exactly two literals, then
a uniformly random assignment satisfies each clause with probability 3/4. The
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION279

standard definition also allows unit clauses; the SDP relaxation below extends
directly to that setting, with unit clauses contributing a simpler bilinear term
involving only one variable and the truth variable.
    It is convenient to first write the problem as a quadratic program using sign
variables. Introduce variables 𝑦0 , 𝑦1 , 𝑦2 , . . . , 𝑦𝑛 ∈ {−1, +1} where 𝑦0 is a special
variable representing “truth” and 𝑦 𝑖 represents the value of 𝑥 𝑖 . We interpret 𝑥 𝑖
as true if 𝑦 𝑖 = 𝑦0 and false if 𝑦 𝑖 = −𝑦0 . For a literal ℓ define
                                              (
                                                  𝑦𝑖     if ℓ = 𝑥 𝑖 ,
                                      𝑦˜ℓ =
                                                  −𝑦 𝑖   if ℓ = ¬𝑥 𝑖 .

Then ℓ is true iff 𝑦˜ℓ = 𝑦0 and false iff 𝑦˜ℓ = −𝑦0 . Hence the indicator that ℓ is false
is
                                        1 − 𝑦˜ℓ 𝑦0
                                                   .
                                              2
   For a unit clause 𝐶 = (ℓ ) of weight 𝑤 𝐶 , the satisfaction indicator is simply
                                                               1 + 𝑦˜ℓ 𝑦0
                                  1[𝐶 is satisfied] =                     .
                                                                  2
For a 2-clause 𝐶 = (ℓ1 ∨ ℓ 2 ) of weight 𝑤 𝐶 , the clause is unsatisfied iff both literals
are false. Therefore, for an integral assignment,
                                                     1 − 𝑦˜ℓ1 𝑦0 1 − 𝑦˜ℓ2 𝑦0
                 1[𝐶 is satisfied] = 1 −                         ·
                                                         2              2
                                                     1 − 𝑦˜ℓ1 𝑦0 − 𝑦˜ℓ2 𝑦0 + 𝑦˜ℓ1 𝑦˜ℓ2 𝑦02
                                         =1−
                                                                 4
                                           3 + 𝑦˜ℓ1 𝑦0 + 𝑦˜ℓ2 𝑦0 − 𝑦˜ℓ1 𝑦˜ℓ2
                                         =                                   ,
                                                          4
where the last step uses 𝑦02 = 1. Let 𝒞1 and 𝒞2 denote the unit clauses and the
2-clauses respectively. Then Max-2-SAT can be written as the quadratic integer
program
                Õ              1 + 𝑦˜ℓ 𝑦0             Õ             3 + 𝑦˜ℓ1 𝑦0 + 𝑦˜ℓ2 𝑦0 − 𝑦˜ℓ1 𝑦˜ℓ2
      max                 𝑤𝐶              +                    𝑤𝐶
                                  2                                                4
              𝐶=(ℓ )∈𝒞1                       𝐶=(ℓ1 ∨ℓ2 )∈𝒞2
      s.t.    𝑦 𝑖2 = 1         𝑖 = 0, 1, . . . , 𝑛.
    We now relax this quadratic program in the same way as for Max-Cut.
Replace each sign variable 𝑦 𝑖 by a unit vector 𝑣 𝑖 , and replace each product 𝑦 𝑖 𝑦 𝑗
by the inner product 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 . For a literal ℓ define
                                              (
                                                  𝑣𝑖     if ℓ = 𝑥 𝑖 ,
                                     𝑣˜ ℓ =
                                                  −𝑣 𝑖   if ℓ = ¬𝑥 𝑖 .
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION280

This yields the vector relaxation
                Õ           1 + 𝑣˜ ℓ · 𝑣 0         Õ                 3 + 𝑣˜ ℓ1 · 𝑣 0 + 𝑣˜ ℓ2 · 𝑣 0 − 𝑣˜ ℓ1 · 𝑣˜ ℓ2
   max                 𝑤𝐶                  +                    𝑤𝐶
                                 2                                                       4
             𝐶=(ℓ )∈𝒞1                         𝐶=(ℓ1 ∨ℓ2 )∈𝒞2
   s.t.      k𝑣 𝑖 k 2 = 1       𝑖 = 0, 1, . . . , 𝑛.

This is a vector program and hence an SDP. An integral assignment is recovered
by taking all vectors to lie on a common line and setting 𝑣 𝑖 = 𝑦 𝑖 𝑣 0 for each 𝑖;
therefore the SDP optimum upper bounds the optimum 2-SAT value.
    The role of the SDP is the same as for Max-Cut: it replaces a discrete sign
choice by a geometric configuration of unit vectors. The rounding is more
delicate than a plain random hyperplane because the objective depends not only
on whether two vectors are separated but also on how each literal correlates
with the truth vector 𝑣 0 . Standard roundings bias the hyperplane according to
these correlations and yield better guarantees than the naive random assignment
when clauses of size 2 are present; see [250, 255] for textbook treatments.

          Hyperplane-2SAT:
           Solve the SDP to obtain optimal unit vectors 𝑣0∗ , 𝑣1∗ , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛∗ .
           Choose a random unit vector 𝑟 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 uniformly from the unit sphere.
           For each variable 𝑖, set 𝑥 𝑖 = true iff 𝑟 · 𝑣 ∗𝑖 and 𝑟 · 𝑣 0∗ have the same sign.
           Output the resulting truth assignment.

Claim 20.4.1. Let 𝐶 be a clause of weight 𝑤 𝐶 and let the partition induced by
                                                          ¯
Hyperplane-2SAT on the relevant vectors be denoted by (𝑆, 𝑆).

   1. If 𝐶 = (ℓ ) is a unit clause, define a graph 𝐻𝐶 with one edge of weight 𝑤 𝐶 between
      the two vertices 𝑣 0∗ and 𝑢ℓ := −𝑣˜ ℓ∗ . Then

          (a) the SDP contribution of 𝐶 equals the SDP objective value of this edge in the
              Max-Cut relaxation, namely 𝑤 𝐶 (1 − 𝑢ℓ · 𝑣0∗ )/2;
                                                                    ¯
          (b) the clause 𝐶 is satisfied iff this edge is cut by (𝑆, 𝑆).

   2. If 𝐶 = (ℓ1 ∨ ℓ 2 ) is a 2-clause, define a triangle 𝐻𝐶 on the three vertices 𝑣 0∗ , 𝑢1 :=
      −𝑣˜ ℓ∗1 , 𝑢2 := −𝑣˜ ℓ∗2 , where each edge has weight 𝑤 𝐶 /2. Then

          (a) the SDP contribution of 𝐶 equals the SDP objective value of this triangle in
              the Max-Cut relaxation;
                                                                   ¯ has weight exactly 𝑤 𝐶
          (b) the clause 𝐶 is satisfied iff the cut induced by (𝑆, 𝑆)
              in 𝐻𝐶 .

Proof. For a unit clause 𝐶 = (ℓ ) we have
                                1 + 𝑣˜ ℓ∗ · 𝑣 0∗          1 − 𝑢ℓ · 𝑣 0∗
                           𝑤𝐶                      = 𝑤𝐶                   ,
                                       2                        2
which proves the SDP identity. Moreover, 𝐶 is satisfied iff 𝑣˜ ℓ∗ and 𝑣 0∗ lie on the
same side of the random hyperplane, which is equivalent to 𝑢ℓ and 𝑣 0∗ lying on
opposite sides. Hence the edge is cut iff the clause is satisfied.
   For a 2-clause 𝐶 = (ℓ 1 ∨ ℓ 2 ), the SDP value of the triangle 𝐻𝐶 is

                                            𝑤 𝐶 1 + 𝑣˜ ℓ1 · 𝑣 0 1 + 𝑣˜ ℓ2 · 𝑣 0 1 − 𝑣˜ ℓ1 · 𝑣˜ ℓ2
                                                       ∗      ∗        ∗      ∗        ∗       ∗
                                                                                                                         !

              ∗            ∗                        
                 +            +           =                     +               +
 2      2            2            2          2        2               2               2
                                                                3 + 𝑣˜ ℓ∗1 · 𝑣 0∗ + 𝑣˜ ℓ∗2 · 𝑣0∗ − 𝑣˜ ℓ∗1 · 𝑣˜ ℓ∗2
