which is exactly the SDP contribution of 𝐶.
       To prove the second part, note that 𝐶 is unsatisfied iff both literals are
false, that is, iff both 𝑣˜ ℓ∗1 , 𝑣˜ ℓ∗2 lie on the side of the hyperplane opposite from
𝑣 0∗ . Equivalently, 𝑢1 , 𝑢2 , 𝑣0∗ all lie on the same side of the hyperplane. Thus 𝐶
is satisfied iff the cut of the triangle 𝐻𝐶 is non-trivial. Any non-trivial cut of a
triangle cuts exactly two edges, and since each edge has weight 𝑤 𝐶 /2, the cut
weight is exactly 𝑤 𝐶 .                                                                
Theorem 20.5. Hyperplane-2SAT is an 𝛼-approximation for Max-2-SAT, where 𝛼 is
the Goemans–Williamson constant from Lemma 20.4.
Proof. Consider the gadget 𝐻𝐶 from Claim 20.4.1 for each clause 𝐶. The vectors
from the Max-2-SAT SDP give a feasible Max-Cut SDP solution for each gadget.
By the Goemans–Williamson analysis, the expected cut weight produced by the
random hyperplane rounding on 𝐻𝐶 is at least 𝛼 times the SDP value of 𝐻𝐶 .
By Claim 20.4.1, this implies that the expected weight contributed by clause 𝐶
under Hyperplane-2SAT is at least 𝛼 times the SDP contribution of 𝐶.
   Summing over all clauses and using linearity of expectation, the expected
weight of the assignment produced by Hyperplane-2SAT is at least 𝛼 times the
SDP optimum, and hence at least 𝛼 times the true optimum.                    
    The preceding theorem shows that the plain random hyperplane rounding
already matches the Goemans–Williamson guarantee for Max-Cut. One can
also prove the same 𝛼 bound by a direct geometric analysis of unit clauses and
2-clauses. The gadget proof above is not strictly necessary, but it makes a useful
conceptual connection between Max-2-SAT and Max-Cut.
    To do better, one uses a problem-specific rounding that first modifies the
vectors before the hyperplane step. Concretely, for each variable 𝑖 one replaces
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION282

𝑣 ∗𝑖 by a rotated vector 𝑣 0𝑖 lying in the plane spanned by 𝑣 ∗𝑖 and 𝑣 0∗ , where the new
angle with 𝑣 0∗ is chosen as a careful function of 𝑣 ∗𝑖 · 𝑣0∗ . One then applies the
random hyperplane rounding to the rotated vectors. With an appropriate choice
of the rotation function this yields an approximation ratio of 𝛽 ≈ 0.94016567
[192]. This constant is known to be optimal under the Unique Games Conjecture
[46].

20.4.2   A Generic SDP for Max-CSPs
It is helpful to first discuss Boolean CSPs. Let Γ = {𝑃1 , . . . , 𝑃𝑚 } be a finite
family of predicates, where each predicate 𝑃 has some arity 𝑘(𝑃) and maps
{0, 1} 𝑘(𝑃) → {0, 1}. An instance of Max-CSP(Γ) has Boolean variables 𝑥1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛
and weighted constraints 𝐶. Each constraint chooses a predicate 𝑃𝐶 ∈ Γ and a
tuple of variables 𝑆𝐶 = (𝑖1 , . . . , 𝑖 𝑡 ) of the appropriate length, and contributes

                                 𝑤 𝐶 · 𝑃𝐶 (𝑥 𝑖1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑖𝑡 )

to the objective. The goal is to maximize the total weight of satisfied constraints.
    The family Γ specifies which types of local constraints are allowed. Several
familiar problems fit this template.

   • Max-Cut: use the single binary predicate XOR(𝑥, 𝑦) (equivalently, NEQ(𝑥, 𝑦)).
     If 𝑥 𝑖 ∈ {0, 1} indicates on which side of the cut vertex 𝑖 lies, then an edge
     (𝑖, 𝑗) is cut iff 𝑥 𝑖 ⊕ 𝑥 𝑗 = 1.

   • Max-2-SAT: if we focus on 2-clauses, then Γ consists of the four binary
     predicates corresponding to (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦), (𝑥 ∨ ¬𝑦), (¬𝑥 ∨ 𝑦), and (¬𝑥 ∨ ¬𝑦). If
     one also allows unit clauses, then one adds the two unary predicates 𝑥 and
     ¬𝑥.

   • Max-3-SAT: here the arity is 3, and the family consists of the eight
     predicates obtained from (ℓ1 ∨ ℓ 2 ∨ ℓ 3 ) by independently negating each
     literal.

    For specific Boolean problems such as Max-Cut or Max-2-SAT, it is often
useful to write a quadratic program first and then relax the products 𝑦 𝑖 𝑦 𝑗 to
vector inner products, as we did earlier. However, that viewpoint is tied to the
special algebra of a particular predicate family. For a generic CSP, especially
when the family contains predicates of different arities, the cleaner starting point
is a local-distribution relaxation.
    There is nevertheless a direct algebraic formulation. For each variable 𝑥 𝑖 and
value 𝑎 ∈ {0, 1}, introduce an indicator variable 𝑥 𝑖,𝑎 ∈ {0, 1} with 𝑥 𝑖,0 + 𝑥 𝑖,1 = 1.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION283

Then a constraint 𝐶 on scope 𝑆𝐶 = (𝑖1 , . . . , 𝑖 𝑡 ) contributes

                                 Õ                     𝑡
                                                       Ö
                                              𝑃𝐶 (𝛼)         𝑥 𝑖 𝑟 ,𝛼(𝑖 𝑟 )
                               𝛼∈{0,1}   𝑆𝐶            𝑟=1


to the objective. Thus a generic Boolean CSP can be viewed as a polynomial
optimization problem whose degree is the arity of the constraints. The local-
distribution LP can be interpreted as a linearization of this polynomial program:
the variables 𝜇𝑖,𝑎 stand for the degree-1 indicators 𝑥 𝑖,𝑎 , while the variables 𝜆𝐶,𝛼
stand for the monomials 𝑡𝑟=1 𝑥 𝑖 𝑟 ,𝛼(𝑖 𝑟 ) . The consistency constraints are exactly
                               Î
the identities these monomials satisfy under an integral assignment. The SDP
then uses the same local variables, but replaces nonnegative scalars by vectors
and enforces the same local consistency through inner-product constraints; in
this sense it is a lifted, geometric strengthening of the LP relaxation.
    For Max-Cut, this polynomial viewpoint is very concrete. For each vertex
𝑖 we have indicator variables 𝑥 𝑖,0 , 𝑥 𝑖,1 with 𝑥 𝑖,0 + 𝑥 𝑖,1 = 1. An edge 𝑖𝑗 is cut iff
either 𝑥 𝑖,0 = 1, 𝑥 𝑗,1 = 1 or 𝑥 𝑖,1 = 1, 𝑥 𝑗,0 = 1. Hence the cut value is
                              Õ
                                     𝑤 𝑖𝑗 𝑥 𝑖,0 𝑥 𝑗,1 + 𝑥 𝑖,1 𝑥 𝑗,0 .
                                                                        
                              𝑖𝑗∈𝐸

This is a quadratic program because the predicate XOR has arity 2. For a ternary
predicate, such as in Max-3-SAT, the analogous expression would involve degree-
3 monomials. Thus Max-Cut and Max-2-SAT are special cases where the generic
polynomial program happens to be quadratic.
    The key point is that each constraint depends on only a constant number of
variables. Thus, rather than trying to optimize over a full distribution on all 2𝑛
assignments, we keep track only of local distributions. This gives a natural LP
relaxation.
Local-Distribution LP: For each variable 𝑥 𝑖 and value 𝑎 ∈ {0, 1} introduce a
variable 𝜇𝑖,𝑎 , intended to represent the marginal probability that 𝑥 𝑖 = 𝑎. For
each constraint 𝐶 and each local assignment 𝛼 ∈ {0, 1} 𝑆𝐶 introduce a variable
𝜆𝐶,𝛼 , intended to represent the local probability that the variables in 𝑆𝐶 take the
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION284

values prescribed by 𝛼. The LP is:
                  Õ                Õ
        max             𝑤𝐶                   𝑃𝐶 (𝛼) 𝜆𝐶,𝛼
                   𝐶          𝛼∈{0,1}   𝑆𝐶

        s.t.      𝜇𝑖,0 + 𝜇𝑖,1 = 1               𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑛,
                       Õ
                                 𝜆𝐶,𝛼 = 1            for every constraint 𝐶,
                          𝑆𝐶
                  𝛼∈{0,1}
                        Õ
                                           𝜆𝐶,𝛼 = 𝜇𝑖,𝑎          for every 𝐶, 𝑖 ∈ 𝑆𝐶 , 𝑎 ∈ {0, 1},
                            𝑆𝐶
                  𝛼∈{0,1}        :𝛼(𝑖)=𝑎
                  𝜇𝑖,𝑎 ≥ 0            𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑛, 𝑎 ∈ {0, 1},
                  𝜆𝐶,𝛼 ≥ 0                 for every 𝐶, 𝛼 ∈ {0, 1} 𝑆𝐶 .
    The constraints say exactly what they should:
    • 𝜇𝑖,0 , 𝜇𝑖,1 form a distribution on the value of 𝑥 𝑖 ;
    • for each constraint 𝐶, the variables {𝜆𝐶,𝛼 } 𝛼 form a distribution on assign-
      ments to its scope;
    • these local distributions are consistent: if 𝑥 𝑖 is in the scope of 𝐶, then the
      marginal of the local distribution for 𝐶 on coordinate 𝑖 must equal the
      one-variable marginal 𝜇𝑖,𝑎 .
    Why is this an LP relaxation? If 𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 is an integral assignment, we set
       (                                             (
       1       if 𝑥 𝑖 = 𝑎,                               1   if 𝛼 is the restriction of the assignment to 𝑆𝐶 ,
𝜇𝑖,𝑎 =                                      𝜆𝐶,𝛼 =
       0       otherwise,                                0   otherwise.
Then the LP objective is exactly the total weight of satisfied constraints. Thus
the LP optimum upper bounds the true optimum.
Example: Max-Cut via local distributions. Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) be an edge-
weighted graph. For each vertex 𝑖 we have variables 𝜇𝑖,0 , 𝜇𝑖,1 , and for each
edge 𝑒 = 𝑖𝑗 we have four local variables 𝜆 𝑖𝑗,00 , 𝜆 𝑖𝑗,01 , 𝜆 𝑖𝑗,10 , 𝜆 𝑖𝑗,11 . Since the predi-
cate is XOR, the edge contributes weight when its endpoints receive different
values. The LP becomes

The objective counts the probability that an edge is cut, and the constraints say
that the edge distribution must agree with the one-vertex marginals at both
endpoints. This is exactly the same local consistency principle as in the general
LP, specialized to arity 2 and the XOR predicate.
SDP Lift: The SDP uses the same local-distribution viewpoint but encodes
the probabilities by orthogonal vectors. In particular, orthogonality expresses
mutual exclusion of different values or different local assignments. Introduce:
   • a root vector 𝑢 with k𝑢k 2 = 1;
   • for every variable 𝑖 and value 𝑎 ∈ {0, 1}, a vector 𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 ;
   • for every constraint 𝐶 and local assignment 𝛼 ∈ {0, 1} 𝑆𝐶 , a vector 𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 .
The intended meaning is:
   • k𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 k 2 is the marginal probability that 𝑥 𝑖 = 𝑎;
   • k𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 k 2 is the local probability that the variables in 𝑆𝐶 take the joint values
     prescribed by 𝛼.
To make this a genuine vector program, we write all constraints in terms of inner
products.
   The resulting SDP is:
          Õ                Õ
   max         𝑤𝐶                    𝑃𝐶 (𝛼) k𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 k 2
           𝐶   𝛼∈{0,1} 𝑆𝐶
   s.t.      2
          k𝑢k = 1,
          𝑣 𝑖,0 · 𝑣 𝑖,1 = 0            𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑛,
          𝑢 · 𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 = k𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 k 2            𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑛, 𝑎 ∈ {0, 1},
          k𝑣 𝑖,0 k 2 + k𝑣 𝑖,1 k 2 = 1            𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑛,
          𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 · 𝑧 𝐶,𝛽 = 0              for every 𝐶, 𝛼 ≠ 𝛽,

               Õ
                         k𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 k 2 = 1          for every constraint 𝐶,
                  𝑆𝐶
          𝛼∈{0,1}
                Õ

          𝛼∈{0,1}        :𝛼(𝑖)=𝑎

          𝑣 𝑖,𝛼(𝑖) · 𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 = k𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 k 2            if 𝑖 ∈ 𝑆𝐶 .
    All constraints are linear in the Gram matrix entries, so this is an SDP. The
interpretation is parallel to the LP:
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION286

    • {𝑣 𝑖,0 , 𝑣 𝑖,1 } acts like a distribution on the value of 𝑥 𝑖 ;

    • {𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 } 𝛼 acts like a local distribution on assignments to the scope of 𝐶;

    • the local distributions are consistent with the one-variable marginals.

The consistency constraints have two parts. First,
                                     Õ
                                              k𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 k 2 = k𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 k 2
                                   𝛼:𝛼(𝑖)=𝑎

says that the marginal of the local distribution for 𝐶 on variable 𝑖 agrees with
the one-variable marginal. Second, if 𝛼(𝑖) = 𝑎, then the vector 𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 is allowed to
overlap only with 𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 and is forced to be orthogonal to the wrong value 𝑣 𝑖,1−𝑎 .
    The constraints involving 𝑢 and the norm sums play the role of normalization.
Intuitively they say that the vectors {𝑣 𝑖,0 , 𝑣 𝑖,1 } partition the unit vector 𝑢, and
similarly the vectors {𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 } 𝛼 partition 𝑢 for each constraint 𝐶. This is the
vector-program analogue of saying that the underlying probabilities sum to 1.
    This should be compared with the preceding Max-2-SAT relaxation. There
we exploited the special structure of the Boolean domain and encoded the two
values of each variable by a single sign vector. For a generic Boolean CSP, the
more symmetric and more useful view is to keep one object for each possible
value and one object for each local assignment to a constraint scope.
    Why is this an SDP relaxation? Given an integral assignment 𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ∈
{0, 1}, fix any unit vector 𝑢 and set
       (                                      (
        𝑢     if 𝑥 𝑖 = 𝑎,                         𝑢   if 𝛼 is the restriction of the assignment to 𝑆𝐶 ,
𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 =                           𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 =
        0     otherwise,                          0   otherwise.

Then all constraints are satisfied, and the objective value is exactly the total
weight of satisfied constraints. Thus the SDP optimum is at least the true
optimum.
Example: Max-Cut via the SDP lift. For Max-Cut, in addition to the vectors
𝑣 𝑖,0 , 𝑣 𝑖,1 for each vertex 𝑖, we have four local vectors 𝑧 𝑖𝑗,00 , 𝑧 𝑖𝑗,01 , 𝑧 𝑖𝑗,10 , 𝑧 𝑖𝑗,11 for
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION287

each edge 𝑖𝑗. The SDP specializes to
                      Õ
                             𝑤 𝑖𝑗 k𝑧 𝑖𝑗,01 k 2 + k𝑧 𝑖𝑗,10 k 2
                                                                

                      𝑖𝑗∈𝐸

                      𝑣 𝑗,𝑏 · 𝑧 𝑖𝑗,𝑎𝑏 = k𝑧 𝑖𝑗,𝑎𝑏 k 2        𝑖𝑗 ∈ 𝐸, 𝑎, 𝑏 ∈ {0, 1}.

Here the objective counts the probability mass on the two edge assignments
(0, 1) and (1, 0) that correspond to being cut. This is the SDP analogue of the
preceding LP. It is more cumbersome than the Goemans–Williamson vector
program because it keeps explicit local assignment vectors for every edge, but it
illustrates the generic CSP paradigm very clearly.
Finite-Domain Generalization: The same template extends immediately to
any finite domain [𝑞] = {1, 2, . . . , 𝑞}: one introduces vectors 𝑣 𝑖,𝑎 for each 𝑎 ∈ [𝑞]
and vectors 𝑧 𝐶,𝛼 for each local assignment 𝛼 ∈ [𝑞]𝑆𝐶 . The only change is
notational.
    Let us write the finite-domain version explicitly. Let Λ be a finite constraint
language over domain [𝑞]. An instance of Max-CSP(Λ) has variables 𝑥1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛
taking values in [𝑞], and weighted constraints 𝐶, where each constraint 𝐶 has
a scope 𝑆𝐶 = (𝑖1 , . . . , 𝑖 𝑡 ) and a predicate 𝑃𝐶 : [𝑞]𝑡 → {0, 1}. The objective is to

    This formulation is deliberately generic. Different problems admit additional
valid constraints, stronger hierarchies, or more specialized geometric interpre-
tations, but the important point is that the same local-distribution template
applies to every finite-domain Max-CSP. One then seeks a rounding scheme
that converts these consistent local distributions into a single global assignment
while preserving as much of the objective value as possible.

20.4.3     Raghavendra’s Theorem
The SDP above is meant to illustrate the local-distribution viewpoint. Raghaven-
dra’s theorem is stated for a stronger canonical SDP relaxation (often called the
basic SDP) built from the same local-consistency idea. The surprise is not merely
that one can write a generic SDP, but that this canonical relaxation captures
the optimal approximation threshold for every Max-CSP, assuming the Unique
Games Conjecture.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION289

Theorem 20.6 (Raghavendra [222]). Fix any finite Max-CSP(Λ) over a finite domain,
and let 𝛼Λ denote the best approximation ratio achievable by rounding the canonical
basic SDP relaxation for Λ. Then for every 𝜖 > 0 there is a polynomial-time algorithm
that achieves approximation ratio at least 𝛼Λ − 𝜖. Moreover, assuming the Unique
Games Conjecture, no polynomial-time algorithm can achieve approximation ratio better
than 𝛼Λ + 𝜖.

    In other words, under UGC the optimum approximation ratio for every
finite-domain Max-CSP is exactly the integrality-gap threshold of a canonical
SDP relaxation. The Goemans–Williamson theorem for Max-Cut is the most
famous special case, while Max-2-SAT, Max-DiCut, Max-𝑘-Cut, and many other
problems fall under the same umbrella.


20.5     Graph Coloring via SDP
A proper 𝑘-coloring of a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) assigns each vertex a color from
{1, . . . , 𝑘} so that no two adjacent vertices share a color. The chromatic number
𝜒(𝐺) is the minimum 𝑘 for which a proper 𝑘-coloring exists. Deciding whether
𝜒(𝐺) ≤ 3 is NP-complete [116]. General graph coloring is extremely hard to
approximate: it is NP-hard to color a graph using fewer than 𝑛 1−𝜀 colors for any
fixed 𝜀 > 0 [262].
    This hardness does not preclude interesting algorithms for graphs with small
chromatic number. We focus on the case 𝜒(𝐺) = 3 and ask: how few colors
does an efficient algorithm need to properly color a 3-colorable graph? This is
a promise problem since we cannot efficiently verify 3-colorability. SDP-based
approximation algorithms for this setting were initiated by Karger, Motwani,
and Sudan [167].
                    √
20.5.1   A Simple 𝑶( 𝒏)-Coloring
A key observation is that if 𝜒(𝐺) = 3, then for any vertex 𝑢 the subgraph 𝐺[𝑁(𝑢)]
induced by the neighbors of 𝑢 is 2-colorable (bipartite), since the neighbors of 𝑢
receive only two of the three colors. We can identify a bipartite decomposition
of 𝐺[𝑁(𝑢)] in polynomial time.
    This leads to a simple algorithm parameterized by a threshold 𝛿; it is
essentially Wigderson’s neighborhood-coloring idea [254].
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION290

          SimpleColoring:
            𝐺0 ← 𝐺
            while (Δ(𝐺0) > 𝛿) do
                let 𝑣 be a vertex of degree Δ(𝐺0) in 𝐺0
                2-color 𝐺0[𝑁(𝑣)] using 2 fresh colors; assign 𝑣 a fresh color
                remove 𝑣 and 𝑁(𝑣) from 𝐺0
            endwhile
            color 𝐺0 with 𝛿 + 1 fresh colors (greedy)

    In each iteration of the while loop at least 𝛿 + 1 vertices are removed,
so the loop runs at most 𝑡 ≤ 𝑛/(𝛿 + 1) times. Each iteration uses at most
3 fresh colors (2 for 𝑁(𝑣), 1 for 𝑣), so the total number
                                                    √      of colors is at most
                                                                            √
3𝑡 + (𝛿 + 1) ≤ 3𝑛/(𝛿 + 1) + (𝛿 + 1). Choosing 𝛿 = Θ( 𝑛) minimizes this to 𝑂( 𝑛).
Theorem 20.7. √There is a polynomial-time algorithm that properly colors any 3-colorable
graph using 𝑂( 𝑛) colors [254].

20.5.2   Vector Chromatic Number
                     √
To do better than 𝑂( 𝑛) we use an SDP relaxation of coloring called the vector
chromatic number [167]. It is closely related to the Łovász theta function and
other classical SDPs for graph coloring [130]. It is convenient to first optimize
the edge-correlation parameter
                                
                                
                                     𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 ≤ −𝑧         (𝑖, 𝑗) ∈ 𝐸, 
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                
                                                                       
                                                                        
                 𝑧 ∗ (𝐺) = max 𝑧 : 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑖 = 1         𝑖 ∈ 𝑉,              .
                                      𝑣 𝑖 ∈ ℝ𝑛
                                                                       
                                                       𝑖∈𝑉
                                
                                                                       
                                                                        
                                                                       
Since all constraints are linear in the inner products, this is an SDP. The vector
chromatic number is then defined by
                                                     1
                                 𝜒𝑣 (𝐺) := 1 +           .
                                                 𝑧 ∗ (𝐺)
Thus larger feasible values of 𝑧 correspond to better colorings; if adjacent vertices
can be forced to make angle at least 120◦ , then 𝑧 ≥ 1/2 and hence 𝜒𝑣 (𝐺) ≤ 3.
Lemma 20.5. If 𝐺 is 𝑘-colorable, then 𝑧 ∗ (𝐺) ≥ 1/(𝑘 − 1), and hence 𝜒𝑣 (𝐺) ≤ 𝑘. In
particular, every 3-colorable graph has 𝑧 ∗ (𝐺) ≥ 1/2 and therefore 𝜒𝑣 (𝐺) ≤ 3.
Proof. Given a proper 𝑘-coloring with color classes 𝑉1 , . . . , 𝑉𝑘 , we assign to the
𝑘 color classes the 𝑘 vertices of a regular (𝑘 − 1)-simplex centered at the origin.
Concretely, assign the same 𝑘-dimensional vector to every vertex in color class
𝑉ℓ :
                 𝑣 𝑖 = (𝛽, . . . , 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝛽, . . . , 𝛽) with 𝛼 in position ℓ ,
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION291
              p                            p
where 𝛼 = − (𝑘 − 1)/𝑘 and 𝛽 = 1/ 𝑘(𝑘 − 1). These are unit vectors with the
same pairwise inner product −1/(𝑘 − 1), which is exactly the defining geometry
of a regular simplex. Thus 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑖 = 1 for all 𝑖 and 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 = −1/(𝑘 − 1) whenever
vertices 𝑖, 𝑗 receive different colors, in particular for all edges (𝑖, 𝑗). Hence
𝑧 = 1/(𝑘 − 1) is feasible, so 𝑧 ∗ (𝐺) ≥ 1/(𝑘 − 1) and 𝜒𝑣 (𝐺) ≤ 1 + (𝑘 − 1) = 𝑘.     
    Equivalently, among 𝑘 unit vectors the largest possible common pairwise
angle is achieved by a regular (𝑘 − 1)-simplex, whose vertices have inner product
−1/(𝑘 − 1). For 𝑘 = 3, this is just the familiar configuration of three unit
vectors in the plane spaced 120◦ apart; adjacent vertices then have inner product
cos(120◦ ) = −1/2.

20.5.3   SDP-Based Coloring: 𝑶(𝚫𝒂 log 𝒏) Colors
Let 𝑣 1 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 be a feasible vector coloring for a 3-colorable graph 𝐺 with
𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 ≤ −1/2 on every edge (𝑖, 𝑗). Such a solution exists by Lemma 20.5. Then
the angle 𝜃𝑖𝑗 between the vectors of any edge (𝑖, 𝑗) satisfies 𝜃𝑖𝑗 ≥ 2𝜋/3 (i.e., at
least 120◦ ).

            VecColoring: (parameter 𝑡 = 2 + dlog3 Δe)
              compute vector coloring 𝑣1 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 via SDP
              while 𝑉 ≠ ∅ do
                  choose 𝑡 random unit vectors 𝑟1 , . . . , 𝑟𝑡 ∈ ℝ 𝑛
                  assign each vertex 𝑖 color (sgn(𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑟1 ), . . . , sgn(𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑟𝑡 ))
                  let 𝐵 = vertices incident to a monochromatic edge
                  permanently assign colors to 𝑉 \ 𝐵; set 𝑉 ← 𝐵
              endwhile

    We analyze the probability that a given edge (𝑖, 𝑗) is monochromatic (assigned
the same color string).

Lemma 20.6. For each edge (𝑖, 𝑗), P[color(𝑖) = color(𝑗)] ≤ (1/3)𝑡 .

Proof. Vertex 𝑖 and 𝑗 receive the same color in one coordinate if and only if the
random hyperplane 𝑟ℓ does not separate 𝑣 𝑖 and 𝑣 𝑗 . Since 𝜃𝑖𝑗 ≥ 2𝜋/3,

                                                           𝜃𝑖𝑗          2 1
                  P[sgn(𝑟 · 𝑣 𝑖 ) = sgn(𝑟 · 𝑣 𝑗 )] = 1 −         ≤ 1−    = .
                                                           𝜋            3 3
The 𝑡 random vectors are independent, so the edge is monochromatic with
probability at most (1/3)𝑡 .                                          
  Setting 𝑡 = 2 + log3 Δ gives (1/3)𝑡 ≤ 1/(9Δ). The expected number of
monochromatic edges is at most 𝑚/(9Δ) ≤ 𝑛/18. Each monochromatic edge
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION292

causes at most 2 vertices to be added to 𝐵, so 𝔼[|𝐵|] ≤ 𝑛/9. By Markov’s
inequality, with probability at least 12 , |𝐵| ≤ 2𝑛/9. Each iteration thus colors at
least a constant fraction of the remaining vertices.
    In each iteration we use 2𝑡 ≤ 4 · Δ𝑎 fresh colors, where 𝑎 = log 2/log 3 ≈ 0.631.
After 𝑂(log 𝑛) iterations all vertices are colored.
Theorem 20.8 (Karger–Motwani–Sudan, 1998). There is a polynomial-time ran-
domized algorithm that colors any 3-colorable graph using 𝑂(Δ𝑎 log 𝑛) colors, where
𝑎 = log3 2 ≈ 0.631 [167].
   Plugging this into the SimpleColoring algorithm: when Δ(𝐺0) > 𝛿 we
use the SDP-based coloring on 𝑁(𝑣), using 𝑂(𝛿 𝑎 log 𝑛) colors per iteration.
Optimizing 𝛿 gives an 𝑂(𝑛 0.387 )-coloring. A further refinement of the SDP
rounding (using a smarter projection step) improves the per-iteration cost to
𝑂(Δ1/3 (log Δ)1/2 log 𝑛) colors, yielding:
Theorem 20.9 (Karger–Motwani–Sudan, 1998). There is a polynomial-time ran-
domized algorithm that colors any 3-colorable graph using 𝑂(𝑛 1/4 log1/2 𝑛) colors
[167].
    The approach extends to 𝑘-colorable graphs: one can color them with
𝑂(Δ1−2/𝑘 (log Δ)1/2 log 𝑛) colors, or 𝑂(𝑛 1−3/(𝑘+1) log1/2 𝑛) colors overall.
    It is worth noting that the vector chromatic number itself can have a large
integrality gap. Feige, Langberg, and Schechtman showed that for every
positive integer 𝑘 there are graphs with 𝜒𝑣 (𝐺) ≤ 𝑘 but with no independent set
significantly larger than 𝑛/Δ1−2/𝑘 , and hence with chromatic number at least on
the order of Δ1−2/𝑘 [105]. In particular, the SDP is not a good approximation to
the true chromatic number in general; they also show that the vector chromatic
number does not approximate the chromatic number within a factor better than
𝑛/polylog 𝑛 [105].

20.5.4   The Łovász Theta Function
Another important SDP relaxation for graph coloring is the Łovász theta function.
It is usually defined for a graph 𝐻 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) via an orthonormal representation [130,
196]: this is an assignment of unit vectors 𝑢𝑖 to the vertices such that
                       𝑢𝑖 · 𝑢 𝑗 = 0    whenever 𝑖𝑗 ∉ 𝐸(𝐻).
Thus nonadjacent vertices must receive orthogonal vectors. Given in addition a
unit vector 𝑐, called a handle, define

                                            k𝑐 k 2 = 1,            

                                  1

             𝜗(𝐻) := min max              : k𝑢 𝑖 k                   .
                         𝑖∈𝑉 (𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 )
                                       2                          

                        
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION293

This is an SDP: the objective can be written by introducing a variable        √ 𝑡 and
minimizing 𝑡 subject to (𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 ) ≥ 1/𝑡 for all 𝑖, or equivalently 𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 ≥ 1/ 𝑡 after
                                 2

choosing the signs of the 𝑢𝑖 so that 𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 ≥ 0.
     It is useful to view 𝜗(𝐻) as a relaxation of coloring the complement graph
𝐻. A proper 𝑘-coloring of 𝐻 assigns each vertex one of 𝑘 colors so that adjacent
vertices get different colors. We can encode such a coloring by assigning to each
color ℓ the basis vector 𝑒ℓ ∈ ℝ 𝑘 , and assigning each vertex the basis vector of
its color. Then adjacent vertices of 𝐻 receive orthogonal vectors, which means
exactly that nonadjacent vertices of 𝐻 receive orthogonal vectors. Thus a genuine
coloring gives a very special orthonormal representation in which all vectors
are chosen from a finite set of basis directions. The theta function relaxes this
discrete condition by allowing each vertex to choose any unit vector, subject only
to the orthogonality constraints. The handle vector 𝑐 measures how concentrated
these vectors are around√    a common direction: in an integral 𝑘-coloring one can
take 𝑐 = (1, . . . , 1)/ 𝑘, in which case (𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 )2 = 1/𝑘 for every vertex, and the
objective value is exactly 𝑘. Thus 𝜗(𝐻) can be seen as a continuous, SDP-based
analogue of the coloring number 𝜒(𝐻).
     The theta function gives a remarkable bridge between independence and
coloring.
Theorem 20.10 (Łovász sandwich theorem). For every graph 𝐻,

                                𝛼(𝐻) ≤ 𝜗(𝐻) ≤ 𝜒(𝐻).

Proof. Let 𝑆 be any independent set in 𝐻. By the definition of an orthonor-
mal representation, the vectors {𝑢𝑖 : 𝑖 ∈ 𝑆} are pairwise orthogonal, hence
orthonormal. If 𝑡 = max𝑖 1/(𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 )2 , then (𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 )2 ≥ 1/𝑡 for every 𝑖, so by Bessel’s
inequality,
                                         Õ               |𝑆|
                         1 = k𝑐 k 2 ≥       (𝑐 · 𝑢𝑖 )2 ≥     .
                                                          𝑡
                                         𝑖∈𝑆
Hence 𝑡 ≥ |𝑆|. Since this holds for every feasible solution and every independent
set, 𝜗(𝐻) ≥ 𝛼(𝐻).
     For the upper bound, suppose 𝐻 has a proper 𝑘-coloring with color classes
𝑉1 , . . . , 𝑉𝑘 . Assign                                                𝑘
                      √ to each vertex in 𝑉ℓ the basis vector 𝑒ℓ ∈ ℝ , and let
𝑐 = (1, 1, . . . , 1)/ 𝑘. If 𝑖𝑗 ∉ 𝐸(𝐻) then 𝑖𝑗 is an edge of 𝐻, so 𝑖 and 𝑗 receive
different colors and therefore orthogonal basis vectors. Thus this is a valid
orthonormal representation of 𝐻. Moreover, for every vertex 𝑖,
                                                    1

                                                    𝑘
so the objective value is 𝑘. Therefore 𝜗(𝐻) ≤ 𝑘 = 𝜒(𝐻).                               

   Applying the theorem to 𝐻 = 𝐺 yields

                              𝜔(𝐺) ≤ 𝜗(𝐺) ≤ 𝜒(𝐺).
Thus 𝜗(𝐺) is another efficiently computable lower bound on the chromatic
number. It is closely related to the vector chromatic number, but the formulations
are conceptually different: vector coloring constrains the angles on edges of
𝐺, while 𝜗(𝐺) uses orthogonality on edges of 𝐺 together with a global handle
vector.
    The connection to perfect graphs is especially striking. Recall that a graph 𝐺
is perfect if every induced subgraph 𝐻 satisfies 𝜒(𝐻) = 𝜔(𝐻). Since the sandwich
theorem gives 𝜔(𝐻) ≤ 𝜗(𝐻) ≤ 𝜒(𝐻) for every induced subgraph 𝐻, it follows
that if 𝐺 is perfect then

           𝜗(𝐻) = 𝜔(𝐻) = 𝜒(𝐻)         for every induced subgraph 𝐻.
Thus on perfect graphs the theta function is exact: the SDP relaxation has no
gap. This exactness was one of the key reasons the theta function became central
in perfect graph theory, and it played a major role in Lovász’s proof of the weak
perfect graph theorem that 𝐺 is perfect if and only if 𝐺 is perfect [195, 234].
    For the approximation setting, however, this bound is less directly useful. If
𝐺 is 3-colorable then certainly 𝜗(𝐺) ≤ 𝜒(𝐺) ≤ 3, but this only gives a necessary
condition for 3-colorability. It does not by itself yield a rounding procedure that
converts a theta solution into a good coloring. By contrast, the vector chromatic
number relaxation is tailored to the rounding algorithms above: the constraint
that adjacent vertices make large angles is exactly what allows repeated random
hyperplane cuts to produce partial colorings.
Example: the five-cycle. The graph 𝐶5 is self-complementary, so the sandwich
theorem gives
                       2 = 𝛼(𝐶5 ) ≤ 𝜗(𝐶5 ) ≤ 𝜒(𝐶5 ) = 3.
Łovász showed that in fact                    √
                                   𝜗(𝐶5 ) =       5.
                                                                       √
This is already nontrivial: the SDP relaxation returns the exact value 5, which
lies strictly between the combinatorial quantities 2 and 3. This example was one
of the first indications that SDP can capture delicate graph structure not visible
to simpler LP-based relaxations [130, 196].


20.6    Metric Embeddings via SDP
A metric space is a pair (𝑉 , 𝑑) where 𝑑 : 𝑉 × 𝑉 → ℝ≥0 is symmetric, satisfies the
triangle inequality, and has 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) = 0 iff 𝑢 = 𝑣. A semi-metric allows distinct
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION295

points to have distance 0.
    Given a finite metric (𝑉 , 𝑑), we often wish to embed it into a simpler, more
structured target metric (𝑊 , 𝜌) via a map 𝑓 : 𝑉 → 𝑊 that approximately
preserves distances. The distortion of 𝑓 is the smallest 𝑐 ≥ 1 such that for some
scale 𝛼 > 0:

             𝛼 · 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) ≤ 𝜌( 𝑓 (𝑢), 𝑓 (𝑣)) ≤ 𝛼 · 𝑐 · 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣)          ∀ 𝑢 ≠ 𝑣.

Distortion 1 means 𝑓 is isometric (perfectly preserving distances).
   Two natural target metrics arise in the context of vector programs:
   • Euclidean (ℓ2 ): 𝜌(𝑖, 𝑗) = k𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗 k 2 , for vectors 𝑣 𝑖 ∈ ℝ 𝑘 .

   • Squared Euclidean (negative-type): 𝜌(𝑖, 𝑗) = k𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗 k 22 .
Minimizing the distortion into either of these targets can be formulated as an
SDP.

20.6.1    Finding the Optimal ℓ2 Embedding
Suppose we are given a finite metric (𝑉 , 𝑑) with |𝑉 | = 𝑛. We seek to find the
least distortion embedding of this metric into ℓ 2 . It suffices to consider ℓ2 space
with 𝑛 dimensions. We can find the optimum distortion via SDP as follows. Let
𝑉 = {1, 2, . . . , 𝑛}. The problem requires us to find 𝑛 vectors 𝑣 1 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 ∈ ℝ 𝑛
such that the Euclidean distances between these vectors minimize the distortion.
Since we can scale the vectors we can assume without loss of generality that we
are seeking an embedding that does not contract any pair of points in (𝑉 , 𝑑).
Thus, we want k𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗 k 2 ≥ 𝑑(𝑖, 𝑗) for all 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗). Subject to this constraint we want
to min max𝑖≠𝑗 𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 2 /𝑑(𝑖, 𝑗). To write this as an SDP we consider the decision
problem where we wish to check if there is an embedding with distortion at
most 𝑐 where 𝑐 ≥ 1 is a given number. We see that this is a vector program
below.

                                         2
                             𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗 2 ≥ 𝑑(𝑖, 𝑗)2           𝑖≠𝑗
                                         2
                             𝑣𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗        ≤ 𝑐 2 𝑑(𝑖, 𝑗)2    𝑖≠𝑗
                                                   𝑛
                                        𝑣𝑖   ∈ ℝ       𝑖 ∈ [𝑛]

    Expanding k𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗 k 22 = 𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑖 − 2𝑣 𝑖 · 𝑣 𝑗 + 𝑣 𝑗 · 𝑣 𝑗 , all constraints become
linear in the inner products. This is a vector program, hence an SDP. Note that
the ℓ 2 triangle inequality holds automatically for any vectors, so no additional
constraints are needed. To find the smallest 𝑐 one can do binary search.
    The following fundamental results bound the distortion of ℓ2 embeddings.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION296

Theorem 20.11 (Bourgain, 1985). Every 𝑛-point metric embeds into ℓ 2 with distortion
𝑂(log 𝑛). Moreover, there exist metrics that require distortion Ω(log 𝑛).

Lemma 20.7 (Johnson–Lindenstrauss, 1984). Every 𝑛-point subset of ℓ 2 (in any
dimension) can be embedded into ℝ 𝑑 , for 𝑑 = 𝑂(𝜀−2 log 𝑛), with distortion at most
1 + 𝜀.

20.6.2     Embedding into Negative-type Metric
Negative-type metrics are formally defined as follows.

Definition 20.12. A metric space (𝑉 , 𝑑) is of negative-type if for all finite set of points
𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ∈ 𝑉 and all real numbers 𝑐1 , 𝑐2 , . . . , 𝑐 𝑛 such that 𝑖 𝑐 𝑖 = 0 the following

                                    𝑛 Õ

   We will not discuss the motivation for the preceding abstract but there is a
nice geometric characterization via Schoenberg’s theorem. We state it for finite
metric spaces though it holds in the general case as well.

Theorem 20.13. A finite metric (𝑉 , 𝑑) is of negative-type iff there exists a mapping
                                              2
𝑓 : 𝑉 → ℝ 𝑑 such that 𝑑(𝑢, 𝑣) = 𝑓 (𝑢) − 𝑓 (𝑣) 2 for all 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑉.

   For the squared Euclidean distance 𝑑(𝑖, 𝑗) = k𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗 k 22 , the triangle inequality
does not hold automatically. For example, three collinear points at 0, 1, 2 give
k0 − 2k 2 = 4 > 1 + 1 = k0 − 1k 2 + k1 − 2k 2 .

Exercise 20.1. Prove that if (𝑉 , 𝑑) is induced by square distances of vectors in
Euclidean space then 𝑑 satisfies the abstract negative-type condition.

Lemma 20.8. Vectors 𝑣 1 , . . . , 𝑣 𝑛 satisfy the triangle inequality for squared Euclidean
distances, i.e. k𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑘 k 2 ≤ k𝑣 𝑖 − 𝑣 𝑗 k 2 + k𝑣 𝑗 − 𝑣 𝑘 k 2 for all 𝑖, 𝑗, 𝑘, if and only if every
three vectors form an acute or right triangle.

Proof. Exercise.                                                                                    
Lemma 20.9. Suppose (𝑉 , 𝑑) is an ℓ 1 metric. Then (𝑉 , 𝑑) is of negative type.

Proof. We sketch the proof. If (𝑉 , 𝑑1 ) and (𝑉 , 𝑑2 ) are of negative-type then their
non-negative sum is also of negative-type. This is easy to see by the abstract
characterization. We have seen that (𝑉 , 𝑑) is an ℓ 1 metric iff it is in the cut cone. It
is easy to verify that a cut metric is of negative-type by seeing it via the Euclidean
characterization. Putting together these two facts yields the desired lemma. 

    If we let 𝑑 be the set of all metrics we get a natural relaxation which requires

The dual of this LP relaxation, as we saw, the maximum concurrent flow LP.
We rounded the LP using the fact that any 𝑛-point metric embeds into ℓ1 with
𝑂(log 𝑛)-distortion. We also saw that if 𝑑 is an ℓ 1 metric then there is no loss in
the rounding. Note that an optimum integer solution to the sparsest cut problem
corresponds to a cut (𝑆, 𝑉 − 𝑆) which embeds into ℓ1 since all cut metrics are ℓ1
metrics. Thus, if we can force 𝑑 to be an ℓ1 metric, we will get an exact algorithm
for Non-Uniform Sparsest Cut but this is not feasible because the problem is
NP-Hard. However, we can try negative-type metrics since, as we saw, ℓ 1 is
a negative-type metric. As we saw in the preceding subsection, we can write

                                     p

is almost-tight.


20.7     Sum-of-Squares and Polynomial Optimization
SDP has a deep connection to polynomial optimization through the theory of
sum-of-squares (SOS) decompositions. We motivate it below.
   Examples of univariate polynomials

   • 𝑓 (𝑥) = 2𝑥 2 + 3𝑥 − 10

   • 𝑓 (𝑥) = 𝑥 3 + 10𝑥 2 + 𝑥 − 100

   • 𝑓 (𝑥) = 10𝑥 4 + 𝑥 3 − 53𝑥 2

    Examples of multivariate polynomials

   • 𝑓 (𝑥) = 𝑥 3 𝑦 2 + 𝑥 2 𝑦 1 − 3𝑥 2 𝑦 2 + 1 (degree is 5)

   • 𝑓 (𝑥) = 𝑥 4 𝑦 2 + 𝑥 2 𝑦 4 − 3𝑥 2 𝑦 2 + 1 (degree is 6)

    Consider the unconstrained optimization problem

                                  min 𝑝(𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ).
                                  𝑥∈ℝ 𝑛

Claim 20.7.1. If the degree of 𝑝 is odd, the minimum is −∞.

    Hence the interesting case is even-degree polynomials. Can we find the
minimum efficiently? Note that polynomials are neither convex nor concave
in general and can have many local minima. See figures. Finding a global
minimum is non-trivial, especially for multivariate polynomials. We consider
the decision version of the minimization problem phrased in a particularly nice
way.
Question 20.10. Given 𝑝(𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ) of even degree, is 𝑝(x) ≥ 0 for all x ∈ ℝ 𝑛 ?
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION300

   Suppose we can answer the above. Then finding the minimum of 𝑝 is finding

                       max 𝜆 such that 𝑝(𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ) − 𝜆 ≥ 0
   We can ask two questions about the preceding problem.
   • Computational. Can we solve it efficiently?
   • Structural/mathematical: Is there a nice and easy to understand reason
     that a polynomial is always non-negative?
Definition 20.15. A polynomial 𝑝(𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ) is a sum of squares (SOS) if there
                                                    Í𝑘
exist polynomials 𝑞 1 , . . . , 𝑞 𝑘 such that 𝑝(x) = 𝑖=1 𝑞 𝑖 (x)2 .
    Clearly every SOS polynomial is non-negative. For univariate polynomials
of any even degree, the converse also holds.
Theorem 20.16. A univariate polynomial 𝑝(𝑥) of even degree is non-negative for all
𝑥 ∈ ℝ if and only if it is SOS.
Proof sketch. Factor 𝑝 over ℂ. Since 𝑝 has real coefficients, complex roots appear
in conjugate pairs (𝑥 − 𝛼)(𝑥 − 𝛼) ¯ = (𝑥 − 𝑎)2 + 𝑏 2 , which is a sum of squares.
Non-negativity forces all real roots to have even multiplicity, so they contribute
squares as well. Hence 𝑝 is a product of non-negative quadratics and therefore a
sum of squares.                                                                 
   For multivariate polynomials the situation is more subtle. Hilbert proved in
1888 that non-negativity does not imply SOS in general.
Theorem 20.17 (Hilbert, 1888). Non-negative polynomials are SOS in exactly three
cases:
   1. univariate polynomials of any even degree (𝑛 = 1);
   2. quadratic polynomials in any number of variables (deg = 2);
   3. bivariate quartic polynomials (𝑛 = 2, deg = 4).
In all other cases there exist non-negative polynomials that are not SOS.
    The first explicit counterexample was given by Motzkin in 1967. Consider
the following polynomial that is named after him.

                        𝑀(𝑥, 𝑦) = 𝑥 4 𝑦 2 + 𝑥 2 𝑦 4 − 3𝑥 2 𝑦 2 + 1.

   Applying the AM-GM inequality to the three terms 𝑥 4 𝑦 2 , 𝑥 2 𝑦 4 , and 1 gives

                   𝑥4 𝑦2 + 𝑥2 𝑦4 + 1
                                     ≥ (𝑥 4 𝑦 2 · 𝑥 2 𝑦 4 · 1)1/3 = 𝑥 2 𝑦 2
                           3
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION301




Figure 20.3: Examples of even-degree univariate polynomials. A non-negative
univariate polynomial always admits an SOS decomposition.

and hence 𝑀(𝑥, 𝑦) ≥ 0 everywhere. However, one can show via SDP that 𝑀 is
not SOS. In fact, 𝑀 − 𝛾 is not SOS for any constant 𝛾.
   It turns out that checking whether a polynomial is non-negative is strongly
NP-Hard even for degree 4.
Theorem 20.18. Given a polynomial 𝑝 of degree 4, it is strongly NP-hard to decide if it
is positive definite, that is, if 𝑝(𝑥) > 0 for all 𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 .
    Despite the preceding negative result one can obtain interesting results by
considering SOS polynomials and using hierarchies of relaxations to approach
the global minimization problem.

20.7.1   Checking SOS via SDP
It turns out that one can check whether 𝑝 is an SOS polynomial via SDP. Since
the notation is a bit messy we will first illustrate this in the simpler setting of
univariate polynomials.
    Let 𝑝(𝑥) be a univariate polynomial of degree 2𝑑.
                       𝑝(𝑥) = 𝑝 0 + 𝑝 1 𝑥 + 𝑝 2 𝑥 2 + . . . + 𝑝 2𝑑 𝑥 2𝑑
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION302




Figure 20.4: Examples of multivariate even-degree polynomials. Unlike the
univariate case, non-negative multivariate polynomials need not be SOS.

vector of monomials in (𝑑 + 1) dimensions.
Theorem 20.19. 𝑝(𝑥) is SOS iff there exists a psd matrix 𝑄 ∈ 𝑀 𝑑+1 such that
𝑧(𝑥)𝑇 𝑄𝑧(𝑥) = 𝑝(𝑥).

which implies that 𝑝(𝑥) is an SOS polynomial. The converse direction is not too
hard either.
   We can see the above in action in the setting of a degree 4 polynomial.
𝑝(𝑥) = 𝑝 0 + 𝑝 1 𝑥 + 𝑝 2 𝑥 2 + 𝑝 3 𝑥 3 + 𝑝 4 𝑥 4 . We use monomial vector 𝑧 = [1, 𝑥, 𝑥 2 ]𝑇 .
We expand 𝑝(𝑥) = 𝑧 𝑇 𝑄 𝑧 for an unknown 3 × 3 symmetric matrix 𝑄:
        𝑧 𝑇 𝑄𝑧 = 𝑄 11 · 1 + 2𝑄 12 · 𝑥 + (𝑄 22 + 2𝑄 13 ) · 𝑥 2 + 2𝑄 23 · 𝑥 3 + 𝑄33 · 𝑥 4
    We then match each monomial coefficient with that of 𝑝:

           𝑄11 = 𝑝0 , 2𝑄 12 = 𝑝 1 , (𝑄 22 + 2𝑄 13 ) = 𝑝 2 , 2𝑄23 = 𝑝3 , 𝑄33 = 𝑝 4

   Checking whether 𝑝 is SOS reduces to an SDP feasibility problem: expand
𝑧(𝑥)𝑇 𝑄 𝑧(𝑥) and match each monomial coefficient with the corresponding
coefficient in 𝑝. Each such equality is a linear constraint on the entries of 𝑄. The
problem is:
                     Find 𝑄Õ  ∈ ℝ 𝑠×𝑠
                     s.t.        𝑄 𝛼𝛽 = 𝑝 𝛾 ∀ monomials 𝛾,
                              𝛼+𝛽=𝛾
                              𝑄  0.
If feasible, the Cholesky decomposition of 𝑄 yields the SOS representation.
Infeasibility certifies that 𝑝 is not SOS.
Example. For the Motzkin polynomial 𝑀(𝑥, 𝑦) of degree 6, the monomial
vector 𝑧 for degree ≤ 3 in two variables has 𝑠 = 53 = 10 entries, so 𝑄 would be
10 × 10. The linear system arising from matching coefficients, subject to 𝑄  0,
turns out to be infeasible. This confirms 𝑀 is not SOS.
Remark 20.2. Note that the running time to check whether 𝑝(𝑥) is an SOS
depends polynomially on 𝑛 𝑂(𝑑) . Since a degree 𝑑 polynomial in 𝑛 variables has
representation size at least 𝑛 (each variable needs to occur at least once), the
running time is polynomial for any fixed 𝑑, nevertheless the complexity grows
quite rapidly.

20.7.2    Hilbert’s 17th Problem
As we saw Hilbert showed that 𝑝(𝑥) can be non-negative without being an SOS
polynomial. He asked a related question.
Question 20.11. Hilbert’s 17th Problem (1900) If 𝑝(𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ) ≥ 0 for all 𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 ,
can 𝑝 be written as a sum of squares of rational functions?
                                           Õ  𝑞 𝑘 (𝑥)  2
                                  𝑝(𝑥) =
                                                𝑟 𝑘 (𝑥)
                                            𝑘

    His question was answered affirmatively by Artin.

Theorem 20.21 (Artin, 1927). Every nonnegative polynomial 𝑝 ∈ ℝ[𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ] is a
sum of squares of rational functions.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION305

    Artin’s proof was non-constructive. Finding an explicit representation and
doing so algorithmically required additional ideas and SDP plays a key role in
this.
    Recall the Motzkin polynomial.
                        𝑀(𝑥, 𝑦) = 𝑥 4 𝑦 2 + 𝑥 2 𝑦 4 − 3𝑥 2 𝑦 2 + 1
    We saw that 𝑀(𝑥, 𝑦) ≥ 0 but it is not SOS. However, one can show that
𝑀(𝑥, 𝑦) · (𝑥 2 + 𝑦 2 + 1) is SOS. This proves that 𝑀(𝑥, 𝑦) ≥ 0 since (𝑥 2 + 𝑦 2 + 1) is
SOS and gives an explicit ratio-of-SOS representation consistent with Artin’s
theorem.
    How did we know to try (𝑥 2 +𝑦 2 +1)? This is not obvious. Artin’s theorem says
that one can prove non-negativity of a polynomial by using rational functions
but does not explicitly bound the degree of the denominators nor does it provide
a systematic way to search for them. Subsequent work has developed various
theorems and methods to make this search constructive and algorithmic.

20.7.3   Polynomial Optimization with Constraints: The Lasserre Hi-
         erarchy
Consider the constrained polynomial optimization problem
           𝑝 ∗ = min 𝑝(𝑥),       𝐾 = {𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 : 𝑔𝑖 (𝑥) ≥ 0, 𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑚},
                 𝑥∈𝐾

where 𝑝 and 𝑔1 , . . . , 𝑔𝑚 are polynomials. The feasible set 𝐾 is a semialgebraic set.
Many important settings are semialgebraic: the box [−1, 1]𝑛 (via 𝑔𝑖 = 1 − 𝑥 2𝑖 ),
the unit ball (𝑔 = 1 − k𝑥 k 2 ), polytopes (linear 𝑔𝑖 ), and combinatorial problems
with binary variables (𝑥 2𝑖 = 1).
    Polynomial optimization is NP-hard in general: it subsumes integer program-
ming and quadratic programming over combinatorial constraints. The strategy
is not to solve exactly but to compute provable lower bounds via SOS-based
algebraic certificates.
    A value 𝜆 ≤ 𝑝 ∗ is a valid lower bound if and only if 𝑝(𝑥) − 𝜆 ≥ 0 on 𝐾.
Checking this condition is NP-hard, but we can certify it efficiently by finding an
SOS decomposition of the form
                                                𝑚
                                                Õ
                        𝑝(𝑥) − 𝜆 = 𝜎0 (𝑥) +           𝜎𝑖 (𝑥) 𝑔𝑖 (𝑥),
                                                𝑖=1

where each 𝜎𝑖 is SOS. This is valid because on 𝐾 each term 𝜎𝑖 𝑔𝑖 ≥ 0 (since
𝜎𝑖 ≥ 0 everywhere and 𝑔𝑖 ≥ 0 on 𝐾), so the right-hand side is non-negative on
𝐾, confirming 𝜆 ≤ 𝑝 ∗ . Maximizing 𝜆 subject to the existence of such a certificate
is an SDP.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION306

Level-𝒓 SDP (Lasserre Hierarchy). Fix a level 𝑟 ≥ 1 (the half-degree), large
enough that 2𝑟 ≥ deg 𝑝. Write 𝑑 𝑖 = ddeg 𝑔𝑖 /2e. Then we represent

         𝜎0 (𝑥) = 𝑧 𝑟 (𝑥)𝑇 𝑄 0 𝑧 𝑟 (𝑥),     𝜎𝑖 (𝑥) = 𝑧 𝑟−𝑑𝑖 (𝑥)𝑇 𝑄 𝑖 𝑧 𝑟−𝑑𝑖 (𝑥)   (𝑖 ≥ 1),

where each 𝑄 𝑖  0. This ensures that deg(𝜎𝑖 𝑔𝑖 ) ≤ 2𝑟 for every 𝑖. Thus 𝑄0 has
                                            𝑛+𝑟−𝑑 𝑖 
size 𝑛+𝑟   × 𝑛+𝑟
              𝑟 , while 𝑄 𝑖 has size                  × 𝑛+𝑟−𝑑  𝑖
                                                                   for 𝑖 ≥ 1. After expanding
                                                               
       𝑟                                      𝑟−𝑑 𝑖      𝑟−𝑑 𝑖
and matching monomial coefficients with 𝑝 − 𝜆, one obtains linear constraints
in 𝜆 and the entries of 𝑄 0 , 𝑄1 , . . . , 𝑄 𝑚 :

                  max 𝜆                                    Õ
                  s.t. coefficient equations: 𝑝 − 𝜆 = 𝜎0 +   𝜎𝑖 𝑔𝑖 ,
                                                                         𝑖
                          𝑄 0 , 𝑄1 , . . . , 𝑄 𝑚  0.

This is an SDP that can be solved in polynomial time (in the matrix size, which
is 𝑛 𝑂(𝑟) ).

Theorem 20.22 (Putinar, 1993). Suppose 𝐾 satisfies the Archimedean condition
(which holds whenever 𝐾 is compact and this is certified by the constraints). Then every
polynomial 𝑝 > 0 on 𝐾 admits an SOS-multiplier representation 𝑝 = 𝜎0 + 𝑖 𝜎𝑖 𝑔𝑖 with
                                                                            Í
𝜎𝑖 SOS.

Theorem 20.23 (Lasserre, 2001). The optimal values 𝜆𝑟 of the level-𝑟 SDPs form a
non-decreasing sequence converging to 𝑝 ∗ :

                                      𝜆1 ≤ 𝜆2 ≤ · · · ≤ 𝑝 ∗ .

Under the Archimedean condition, 𝜆𝑟 → 𝑝 ∗ as 𝑟 → ∞ (with finite convergence when
𝑝 > 0 on 𝐾).

    The Lasserre hierarchy (also known as the SOS hierarchy) is a systematic
sequence of increasingly tight SDP relaxations that converges to the true polyno-
mial optimum. The tradeoff is that the SDP size grows as 𝑛 𝑂(𝑟) , so higher levels
are more expensive but provide tighter bounds. In practice the hierarchy often
converges at level 𝑟 = 1 or 𝑟 = 2.

  𝑖𝑗∈𝐸 (1 − 𝑦 𝑖 𝑦 𝑗 )/2 over 𝑦 𝑖 = 1—can be cast as a level-1 Lasserre relaxation and
Í                              2

that this relaxation coincides with the Goemans–Williamson SDP.

3𝑥 2 𝑦 2 + 1, the polynomial 𝑀 · (𝑥 2 + 𝑦 2 + 1) is SOS by finding an explicit SOS
decomposition.

20.7.4   Dual Viewpoint: Moments and Pseudo-Distributions
It is useful to reinterpret the Lasserre hierarchy from the dual point of view.
Instead of thinking of the relaxation as searching for an SOS certificate proving
that 𝜆 ≤ 𝑝 ∗ , we can think of it as optimizing over a relaxed notion of probability
distributions on the feasible set 𝐾.
Exact distributional formulation. Suppose first that 𝜇 is an actual probability
distribution supported on 𝐾. Then

                                      𝔼𝜇 [𝑝(𝑥)] ≥ 𝑝 ∗
when 𝑝 ∗ is the minimum of 𝑝 over 𝐾, and in fact the minimum is attained by a
point mass distribution 𝜇 = 𝛿 𝑥 ∗ at an optimal point 𝑥 ∗ ∈ 𝐾. Thus we may write
the original problem as

                             𝑝∗ =        min           𝔼𝜇 [𝑝(𝑥)].
                                    𝜇 supported on 𝐾

This formulation is exact but infinite-dimensional: a general distribution has too
many degrees of freedom to optimize over directly.
Truncated moments. The key idea is to remember only the low-degree mo-
ments of the distribution. For each monomial 𝑥 𝛼 = 𝑥 1𝛼1 · · · 𝑥 𝑛𝛼 𝑛 of degree at most
2𝑟, define
                                  𝑦 𝛼 := 𝔼𝜇 [𝑥 𝛼 ].
These numbers are the moments of 𝜇. Since expectation is linear, the objective
depends linearly on them:
                                                Õ
                                 𝔼𝜇 [𝑝(𝑥)] =           𝑝 𝛼 𝑦𝛼 .
                                                 𝛼

Thus the moments play the role of SDP variables. When 𝜇 = 𝛿 𝑥 ∗ , we simply have
𝑦 𝛼 = (𝑥 ∗ )𝛼 .
Why psd matrices appear. If the moments come from a genuine distribution,
then for every polynomial 𝑞 of degree at most 𝑟,

                                      𝔼𝜇 [𝑞(𝑥)2 ] ≥ 0.
Write 𝑞(𝑥) = 𝑐 𝑇 𝑧 𝑟 (𝑥) in the monomial basis. Then

                   𝔼𝜇 [𝑞(𝑥)2 ] = 𝔼𝜇 [𝑧 𝑟 (𝑥)𝑇 𝑐𝑐 𝑇 𝑧 𝑟 (𝑥)] = 𝑐 𝑇 𝑀𝑟 (𝑦)𝑐,
where 𝑀𝑟 (𝑦) is the moment matrix indexed by monomials up to degree 𝑟, with
entries
                              𝑀𝑟 (𝑦)𝛼,𝛽 = 𝑦 𝛼+𝛽 .
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION308

Hence any true distribution must satisfy 𝑀𝑟 (𝑦)  0. Similarly, if 𝜇 is supported
on 𝐾 = {𝑥 : 𝑔𝑖 (𝑥) ≥ 0}, then for every 𝑞 of appropriate degree,
                                𝔼𝜇 [𝑔𝑖 (𝑥)𝑞(𝑥)2 ] ≥ 0,
which yields the psd localizing matrices associated with the constraints 𝑔𝑖 .
Pseudo-distributions. The level-𝑟 Lasserre relaxation keeps only moments up
to degree 2𝑟 and uses the numbers
                                  𝑦𝛼            (|𝛼| ≤ 2𝑟)
as the SDP variables. That is, for every monomial 𝑥 𝛼 up to degree 2𝑟 we introduce
one scalar variable 𝑦 𝛼 , intended to represent 𝔼[𝑥 𝛼 ]. The SDP objective is simply
                                                Õ
                                          min       𝑝 𝛼 𝑦𝛼 ,
                                                𝛼
and the constraints are:
   • 𝑦0 = 1,
   • the moment matrix 𝑀𝑟 (𝑦) is psd,
   • for each constraint polynomial 𝑔𝑖 , we form the matrix 𝑀𝑟−𝑑𝑖 (𝑔𝑖 𝑦) indexed
     by monomials of degree at most 𝑟 − 𝑑 𝑖 , where 𝑑 𝑖 = ddeg 𝑔𝑖 /2e, and require
     this matrix to be psd. Concretely, if 𝑞(𝑥) = 𝑐 𝑇 𝑧 𝑟−𝑑𝑖 (𝑥) then
                         𝑐 𝑇 𝑀𝑟−𝑑 (𝑔𝑖 𝑦)𝑐 = 𝔼˜ [𝑔𝑖 (𝑥)𝑞(𝑥)2 ] ≥ 0,
                                      𝑖

      so this matrix constraint is exactly the condition that the pseudo-expectation
      sees 𝑔𝑖 (𝑥) as nonnegative when multiplied by any square of appropriate
      degree.
Thus the SDP is really optimizing over a truncated moment vector 𝑦. However,
not every feasible truncated moment vector 𝑦 comes from an actual probability
distribution. For this reason it is better to think of 𝑦 as defining a degree-2𝑟
pseudo-expectation operator 𝔼˜ , or equivalently a pseudo-distribution, by setting
                                      𝔼˜ [𝑥 𝛼 ] := 𝑦 𝛼
and then extending linearly to all polynomials of degree at most 2𝑟. It behaves
like expectation on all such polynomials:
    • 𝔼˜ [1] = 1,
   • 𝔼˜ [𝑞(𝑥)2 ] ≥ 0 for deg 𝑞 ≤ 𝑟,
   • 𝔼˜ [𝑔𝑖 (𝑥)𝑞(𝑥)2 ] ≥ 0 whenever degrees fit.
But there may be no genuine probability measure behind it. Thus the hierarchy
optimizes over a larger space than the space of actual distributions, and is
therefore a relaxation.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION309

Example: Boolean variables. Many optimization problems in approximation
algorithms can be written as 0/1 integer programs

                         min{𝑐 𝑇 𝑥 : 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏, 𝑥 ∈ {0, 1} 𝑛 }.

This is a special case of polynomial optimization in which the constraint polyno-
mials are linear inequalities together with the Boolean identities 𝑥 2𝑖 − 𝑥 𝑖 = 0. Thus
the general Lasserre hierarchy specializes directly: one introduces moments
                                              "              #
                                                  Ö
                                   𝑦𝑆 := 𝔼˜             𝑥𝑖
                                                  𝑖∈𝑆

for |𝑆| ≤ 2𝑟, with the interpretation that they represent relaxed joint moments of
the Boolean variables. Because the identities 𝑥 2𝑖 = 𝑥 𝑖 allow every polynomial to be
multilinearized, the moment variables are naturally indexed by subsets 𝑆 ⊆ [𝑛]
rather than arbitrary multi-indices. The localizing matrices are also simpler than
in the general polynomial case, since the only inequality constraints are the linear
forms 𝑏ℓ − 𝑎ℓ 𝑥 ≥ 0. Thus the Lasserre hierarchy for 0/1 integer programs may
be viewed as the Boolean specialization of the general moment/SOS framework.
If one prefers variables in {−1, 1} instead, then the Boolean identities become
𝑥 2𝑖 = 1, and the same viewpoint applies after this change of coordinates.
Duality with SOS certificates. The primal SOS view and the dual moment
view are two sides of the same SDP. The primal asks for an SOS proof that
𝑝(𝑥) − 𝜆 ≥ 0 on 𝐾; the dual asks for a pseudo-distribution on 𝐾 minimizing the
relaxed expectation of 𝑝. Thus:
   • SOS certificates are efficiently checkable proofs of lower bounds;

   • pseudo-distributions are relaxed witnesses that mimic feasible random
     solutions.
To make this precise, let 𝑦 = {𝑦 𝛼 } |𝛼|≤2𝑟 be a truncated moment sequence. The
associated moment functional 𝐿 𝑦 is the linear map on polynomials of degree at
most 2𝑟 defined by
                                   𝐿 𝑦 (𝑥 𝛼 ) := 𝑦 𝛼
for each monomial 𝑥 𝛼 , and then extended linearly. Thus if
                                             Õ
                                  ℎ(𝑥) =           ℎ𝛼 𝑥 𝛼 ,
                                              𝛼

then                                         Õ
                                 𝐿 𝑦 (ℎ) =          ℎ 𝛼 𝑦𝛼 .
                                              𝛼
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION310

If 𝑦 comes from a genuine probability distribution 𝜇, then 𝐿 𝑦 (ℎ) = 𝔼𝜇 [ℎ(𝑥)]; in
the pseudo-distribution setting, this is exactly the pseudo-expectation operator
𝔼˜ [ℎ].
     The connection becomes very concrete once we pair an SOS certificate with

              𝜎0 (𝑥) = 𝑧 𝑟 (𝑥)𝑇 𝑄 0 𝑧 𝑟 (𝑥),          𝜎𝑖 (𝑥) = 𝑧 𝑟−𝑑𝑖 (𝑥)𝑇 𝑄 𝑖 𝑧 𝑟−𝑑𝑖 (𝑥)

This is exactly the weak duality relation between the primal SOS SDP and the

    One can read this identity in two complementary ways. From the primal
side, the matrices 𝑄 𝑖 encode a formal algebraic proof that 𝑝(𝑥) − 𝜆 is nonnegative
on 𝐾. From the dual side, the moments 𝑦 define a linear functional that tests
this proof against every low-degree square. If the pseudo-distribution were an
actual distribution supported on 𝐾, the inequality above would simply say that
every valid proof of a lower bound remains valid after taking expectation. The
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION311

moment relaxation keeps exactly the part of this reasoning that only depends
on low-degree moments.
    This also clarifies why the two SDPs have the shapes they do. In the primal,
coefficient matching is the natural way to enforce the polynomial identity
𝑝 − 𝜆 = 𝜎0 + 𝑖 𝜎𝑖 𝑔𝑖 . In the dual, each such coefficient equation gets a dual
                 Í
variable 𝑦 𝛼 , interpreted as the moment of the monomial 𝑥 𝛼 . The psd constraints
on the Gram matrices 𝑄 𝑖 become psd constraints on the moment and localizing
matrices. Thus “Gram matrix” and “moment matrix” are dual linear algebraic
avatars of the same low-degree polynomial space.
    What about strong duality? In general, weak duality always holds, but
strong duality need not hold automatically for an arbitrary SDP. Standard conic
duality tells us that there is no duality gap, and that the optimum is attained on
the opposite side, if a Slater-type condition holds: one of the two SDPs must
be strictly feasible. For the moment relaxation, this means there is a truncated
moment vector 𝑦 with

                    𝑀𝑟 (𝑦)  0,      𝑀𝑟−𝑑𝑖 (𝑔𝑖 𝑦)  0 for all 𝑖,

while for the SOS side it means there are positive definite Gram matrices
satisfying the coefficient equations.
    In polynomial optimization this strict feasibility is not automatic. If the
feasible set 𝐾 has nonempty interior, then one often gets it on the moment side
by taking a genuine probability distribution supported inside an open subset
of 𝐾 on which all 𝑔𝑖 are strictly positive; this makes every nonzero low-degree
square have strictly positive expectation, yielding positive definite moment and
localizing matrices. Under such a condition, the SOS SDP and the moment
SDP have the same optimum value. On the other hand, if 𝐾 lies in a lower
dimensional variety, or if one imposes equality constraints such as 𝑥 2𝑖 = 1, strict
feasibility may fail in the naive formulation, and then one should only assert
weak duality unless one has taken care of this degeneracy by an equivalent
reformulation.
    Thus the safe interpretation is:

   • weak duality always holds;

   • strong duality holds under a Slater condition, which in many natural
     compact semialgebraic settings is satisfied after a suitable formulation;

   • for degenerate algebraic feasible sets, one has to be more careful.

Conceptually, the primal is trying to prove that no feasible point can have value
below 𝜆, while the dual is trying to imitate a feasible point, or more generally a
feasible distribution, whose expected value is as small as possible. When strong
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION312

duality holds and the relaxation is tight, these two viewpoints meet at the true
optimum.
   This dual viewpoint is often very helpful in understanding both the power
and the limitations of the Lasserre hierarchy; see also [186].

20.7.5   Sherali–Adams on the Boolean Hypercube
It is instructive to compare the Lasserre hierarchy with the Sherali–Adams (SA)
hierarchy, which is an LP hierarchy based on a very similar philosophy. Classical
Sherali–Adams is most naturally viewed as a hierarchy for Boolean integer
programs with linear constraints, and we restrict ourselves to that setting here.
Thus we work with Boolean variables 𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ∈ {0, 1}.
     Suppose we have a 0/1 optimization problem

                 max 𝑐 𝑇 𝑥      s.t. 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏,              𝑥 𝑖 ∈ {0, 1} for all 𝑖.

If 𝜇 is an actual probability distribution on feasible Boolean solutions, then for
every pair of disjoint sets 𝑆, 𝑇 ⊆ [𝑛] the quantity

                                        Ö Ö                 
                                                            
                             𝑦𝑆,𝑇 := 𝔼𝜇 
                                            𝑥𝑖   (1 − 𝑥 𝑗 )
                                         𝑖∈𝑆 𝑗∈𝑇            
                                                            
is nonnegative. In fact, it is exactly the probability that all variables in 𝑆 are
equal to 1 and all variables in 𝑇 are equal to 0. These are the local probabilities
that SA keeps track of.
    The level-𝑟 Sherali–Adams relaxation introduces variables 𝑦𝑆,𝑇 for all disjoint
𝑆, 𝑇 with |𝑆| + |𝑇 | ≤ 𝑟. The objective is the relaxed expectation
                                                𝑛
                                                Õ
                                 𝔼˜ [𝑐 𝑇 𝑥] =         𝑐 𝑖 𝔼˜ [𝑥 𝑖 ].
                                                𝑖=1

Formally, the relaxation is the LP
         𝑛
         𝑖=1 𝑐 𝑖 𝑦 {𝑖},∅
         Í
 max
 s.t. 𝑦∅,∅ = 1,
      𝑦𝑆,𝑇 ≥ 0           for all disjoint 𝑆, 𝑇 with |𝑆| + |𝑇 | ≤ 𝑟,
      𝑦𝑆,𝑇 = 𝑦𝑆∪{𝑖},𝑇 + 𝑦𝑆,𝑇∪{𝑖}
        h for all disjoint       𝑆, 𝑇 with |𝑆| +i |𝑇 | ≤ 𝑟 − 1 and 𝑖 ∉ 𝑆 ∪ 𝑇,
                    ℓ
      𝔼˜ (𝑏ℓ − 𝑎 𝑥) 𝑖∈𝑆 𝑥 𝑖 𝑗∈𝑇 (1 − 𝑥 𝑗 ) ≥ 0
                         Î        Î

             for every row ℓ of 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏 and all disjoint 𝑆, 𝑇 with |𝑆| + |𝑇 | ≤ 𝑟 − 1.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION313

The splitting identities encode local consistency: once 𝑖 is not yet specified, the
event (𝑆, 𝑇) is the disjoint union of the two cases 𝑥 𝑖 = 1 and 𝑥 𝑖 = 0. The lifted
constraint inequalities come from the fact that if 𝑎ℓ 𝑥 ≤ 𝑏ℓ is valid for every
feasible Boolean assignment, then
                                        Ö         Ö
                           𝑏ℓ − 𝑎 ℓ 𝑥         𝑥𝑖         (1 − 𝑥 𝑗 ) ≥ 0
                                        𝑖∈𝑆        𝑗∈𝑇

for every disjoint 𝑆, 𝑇, and hence its expectation must also be nonnegative. Thus
SA optimizes over a collection of locally consistent marginals that look like
probabilities on all subsets of at most 𝑟 variables.
    This is closely related to the pseudo-distribution viewpoint for Lasserre.
Both hierarchies replace a true distribution by a truncated collection of low-
degree moments. The key difference is in what nonnegativity conditions they
enforce. Sherali–Adams only uses nonnegativity statements obtained by taking
an original constraint, rewriting it as a linear inequality of the form 𝑏ℓ − 𝑎ℓ 𝑥 ≥ 0,
and multiplying it by Boolean indicator monomials 𝑖∈𝑆 𝑥 𝑖 𝑗∈𝑇 (1− 𝑥 𝑗 ). Because
                                                      Î        Î
these test functions are so special, the resulting conditions are all linear. Lasserre,
on the other hand, tests nonnegativity against all low-degree squares 𝑞(𝑥)2 and,
more generally, 𝑔𝑖 (𝑥)𝑞(𝑥)2 , which yields the psd moment and localizing matrix
constraints.
    In this sense, Sherali–Adams is the LP analogue of the moment method: it
captures local consistency of small Boolean events. Lasserre is a strictly stronger
SDP strengthening that captures positivity of all low-degree polynomial test
functions. One can think of SA as asking that “all small local views look like
valid distributions,” while Lasserre asks for the stronger condition that “all
low-degree polynomials behave as if expectation were taken under a genuine
distribution.”

20.7.6   Boolean CSPs as a Common Framework
Boolean CSPs provide a particularly clean setting in which to see both Sherali–
Adams and Lasserre arise from the same basic idea. Suppose we have Boolean
variables 𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑛 ∈ {0, 1} and an objective of the form
                                        Õ
                                max           𝑤 𝐶 𝑃𝐶 (𝑥 𝑆𝐶 ),
                                        𝐶∈𝒞

where each constraint 𝐶 depends only on a small set 𝑆𝐶 of variables, has weight
𝑤 𝐶 ≥ 0, and 𝑃𝐶 : {0, 1} 𝑆𝐶 → {0, 1} indicates whether the local assignment satis-
fies the predicate of 𝐶. Equivalently, we seek a Boolean assignment maximizing
the total weight of satisfied constraints. In contrast to the general constrained
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION314

polynomial optimization setup discussed earlier, there are no additional feasibil-
ity constraints here beyond the requirement that the variables lie in {0, 1} 𝑛 ; the
predicates only define the objective being optimized.
    The common relaxation idea is to replace a genuine distribution on Boolean
assignments by a truncated collection of local statistics. In the Sherali–Adams
view, these statistics are local probabilities of partial assignments. For each small
set of variables one keeps track of quantities such as

                       P[𝑥 𝑖 = 1 for 𝑖 ∈ 𝑆, 𝑥 𝑗 = 0 for 𝑗 ∈ 𝑇],

and imposes linear constraints saying that these local probabilities are nonneg-
ative and marginalize consistently. The contribution of a constraint 𝐶 to the
objective is then just its expected satisfaction under the corresponding local
distribution. If every predicate has arity at most 𝑘, then one needs at least level
𝑟 ≥ 𝑘 in order for the level-𝑟 relaxation to represent the objective exactly, since
the value of 𝑃𝐶 depends on the joint distribution of all variables in 𝑆𝐶 . Higher
levels allow the relaxation to correlate several nearby constraints by tracking
larger local neighborhoods.
     The Lasserre viewpoint starts from the same Boolean CSP, but expresses
it as a polynomial optimization problem over the Boolean cube. In the 0/1
setting one enforces the constraints 𝑥 𝑖 ∈ {0, 1} via the polynomial identities
𝑥 2𝑖 = 𝑥 𝑖 , and each predicate 𝑃𝐶 can be represented by a multilinear polynomial
in the variables of 𝑆𝐶 . One then introduces moments or pseudo-expectations of
low-degree monomials and optimizes the relaxed objective
                                 Õ
                                      𝑤 𝐶 𝔼˜ [𝑃𝐶 (𝑥 𝑆𝐶 )]
                                𝐶∈𝒞

subject to the moment and localizing matrix constraints. These psd constraints
enforce not just consistency of local marginals but positivity of 𝔼˜ [𝑞(𝑥)2 ] and
𝔼˜ [𝑔(𝑥)𝑞(𝑥)2 ] for all low-degree test polynomials 𝑞. Here too the arity matters:
if each predicate has arity at most 𝑘, then one wants at least level 𝑟 ≥ 𝑘, since
otherwise the relaxation does not contain all degree-𝑘 moments needed to
evaluate the predicate polynomials exactly.
     Thus Boolean CSPs form a natural common framework for both hierarchies.
Sherali–Adams is the LP relaxation obtained by keeping only linear local-
probability consistency conditions, while Lasserre is the stronger SDP relaxation
obtained by imposing psd constraints on low-degree moments. Presenting
the two hierarchies in this common Boolean CSP setting helps isolate their
conceptual difference: SA reasons about small local events, whereas Lasserre
reasons about all low-degree polynomials.
CHAPTER 20. SEMIDEFINITE PROGRAMMING AND APPROXIMATION315

Bibliographic Notes
The Goemans–Williamson 0.878-approximation for Max-Cut appeared in [122];
it was the first use of SDP to achieve a polynomial-time approximation ratio
substantially better than 1/2 for a fundamental NP-hard problem. The matching
hardness result under the Unique Games Conjecture is due to Khot, Kindler,
Mossel, and O’Donnell [175].                     √
     The SDP-based graph coloring results—𝑂( 𝑛) coloring via neighborhood
2-coloring, 𝑂(Δ𝑎 log 𝑛) coloring via the vector chromatic number, and the
𝑂(𝑛 1/4 polylog) algorithm—are from Karger, Motwani, and Sudan [167]. The
vector chromatic number and the Łovász theta function, closely related SDPs
for graph problems, were introduced earlier by Grötschel, Lovász, and Schrĳver
[130].
     Bourgain’s 𝑂(log 𝑛) embedding theorem    p appeared in [44]; the Johnson–
Lindenstrauss lemma is from [163]. The 𝑂( log 𝑛)-approximation for sparsest
cut is due to Arora, Rao, and Vazirani [22]; see also the Fulkerson Prize citation.
     Hilbert’s 1888 theorem on non-negative polynomials appears in his paper
on ternary quartic forms; Artin’s positive solution to Hilbert’s 17th Problem
is from [23]. The Motzkin polynomial was published in [209]. The SOS–SDP
connection and the Lasserre SDP hierarchy were developed by Lasserre [184]
and, independently, by Parrilo [216]. See the survey of Laurent [186] for a com-
prehensive treatment of SOS hierarchies and their applications. For background
on the relation between Sherali–Adams and Lasserre for 0/1 problems, see
also Laurent [186]. In the CSP setting, Sherali–Adams is especially useful for
languages where local consistency captures the global optimum; see, for example,
Thapper–Živný [245] on valued CSPs of bounded width. For approximation of
hard Boolean max-CSPs, Sherali–Adams often serves more as a benchmark for
the limitations of LP relaxations: see Chan–Lee–Raghavendra–Steurer [58] and
the strong Sherali–Adams gap constructions of Georgiou–Magen–Tulsiani [119].
By contrast, the strongest positive approximation results for Boolean CSPs come
from the SDP side: the basic examples are the Goemans–Williamson SDP for
Max-Cut and Max-2-SAT [122], Raghavendra’s theorem gives the canonical SDP
viewpoint for all CSPs [222], and Barak–Raghavendra–Steurer [36] show how
higher rounds of the Lasserre hierarchy can be rounded effectively for 2-CSPs
and Unique Games.
Chapter 21

Hardness of Approximation

The preceding chapters have emphasized algorithmic techniques for proving
upper bounds on approximation ratios for several classes of NP-Hard optimizatio
problems. In this chapter we breifly look at the complementary question. How
do we prove lower bounds on the approximation ratio that can be achieved in
polynomial-time? Clearly, such lower bounds can only be shown under the
assumption that 𝑃 ≠ 𝑁𝑃. Proving hardness of approximation bounds is a big
topic and this study has led to many deep results with the PCP theorem being
the most notable one. We will not be able to go over these results in any level
of detail. The goal in this chapter is to give a flavor of hardness results by
considering three techniques.

  1. direct reductions that create an objective-value gap;

  2. amplification, where a small gap is used via reductions to obtain a larger
     gap; and

  3. the PCP theorem, which is the source of many hardness results.

    Throughout this chapter, an inapproximability result is a statement of the
following form. If a polynomial-time algorithm with a specified approximation
guarantee existed, then we could decide an NP-complete language in polynomial
time. Thus the approximation algorithm would imply 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃.
    We use the same convention as in the rest of the notes: for a maximization
problem, an 𝛼-approximation has value at least 𝛼 OPT with 0 < 𝛼 ≤ 1; for a
minimization problem, a 𝜌-approximation has value at most 𝜌 OPT with 𝜌 ≥ 1.




                                     316
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                         317

21.1    Direct Gap Reductions
The simplest hardness proofs for approximation are ordinary many-one reduc-
tions that convert a decision problem into an optimization problem where the
objective value exhibits a gap between the yes and no instances of the decision
problem. The reduction in Theorem 1.3 of Chapter 1 for 𝑘-Center is a simple and
nice example. The reduction starts from the decision version of Dominating Set:
given an undirected graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) and an integer 𝑘, is there a dominating
set in 𝐺 of size at most 𝑘? It creates a metric 𝑘-Center instance whose optimum
is 1 in the yes case and at least 2 in the no case. The reduction is extremely
simple: we convert (𝐺, 𝑘) into (𝐻, 𝑘) where 𝐻 is the metric closure of 𝐺 and 𝑘 is
the number of centers allowed. It is easy to see that if 𝐺 has a dominating set
of size ≤ 𝑘 then the optimum vlaue for the 𝑘-center instance on 𝐻 is at most
1. If there is no dominating set of size 𝑘 in 𝐺 then any 𝑘-center solution in 𝐻
must have value at least 2. A (2 − 𝜖)-approximation would distinguish these two
cases, which is impossible unless 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃 [153]. We saw that 𝑘-Center admits a
2-approximation and hence we obtain a tight approximability threshold for this
basic problem in an easy fashion. We also mentioned the 𝑘-Supplier problem in
the clustering chapter where one can show a tight approximation threshold of 3,
and the hardness is again via a simple direct reduction.
    The general pattern is worth spelling out. Let Π be a minimization problem.
Suppose that from an instance 𝐼 of an NP-hard decision problem 𝐿 (think of the
problem as a language recognition problem) we can construct, in polynomial
time, an instance 𝑓 (𝐼) of Π such that
             𝐼 ∈ 𝐿 ⇒ OPT( 𝑓 (𝐼)) ≤ 𝐴,      𝐼 ∉ 𝐿 ⇒ OPT( 𝑓 (𝐼)) ≥ 𝐵,
where 𝐵 > 𝐴. Then no polynomial-time approximation algorithm with ratio
strictly smaller than 𝐵/𝐴 can exist unless 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃. Indeed, such an algorithm
would output a solution of value less than 𝐵 in the yes case, while every feasible
solution has value at least 𝐵 in the no case.
    For maximization problems the inequalities are reversed. If a reduction
creates instances with
             𝐼 ∈ 𝐿 ⇒ OPT( 𝑓 (𝐼)) ≥ 𝐵,      𝐼 ∉ 𝐿 ⇒ OPT( 𝑓 (𝐼)) ≤ 𝐴,
where 𝐵 > 𝐴, then no algorithm can always output a solution of value more
than 𝐴/𝐵 times optimum, unless 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃.
   Two points are important. First, the proof only uses the algorithm as a
decision procedure; we compare the value of the algorithm’s output with a
threshold.
   Ideally we want the hardness to be robust in the sense that OPT( 𝑓 (𝐼)) to
grow with the |𝐼 |. So technically we want the ratio OPT( 𝑓 (𝐼)) in the no and
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                                     318

yes cases to be larger than some ratio 𝛼 > 1 (for minimization problems). For
example, in the 𝑘-Center problem we can easily scale the lengths of the edges
and the reduction is robust. However, in some other examples we cannot do
that. Recall the reduction from 2-Partition to Bin Packing. In the yes case we can
pack using 2 bins and in the no case we need at least 3 bins and this shows that
we cannot get better than a (3/2 − 𝜖)-approximation. However, the reduction is
not multiplicatively robust since we know the existence of an asymptotic PTAS.


21.2      Maximum Directed Edge-Disjoint Paths
We next give a second direct reduction, this time for a maximization problem.
In the Maximum Directed Edge-Disjoint Paths problem, the input is a directed
graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐴) and terminal pairs (𝑠 1 , 𝑡1 ), . . . , (𝑠 𝑘 , 𝑡 𝑘 ). The goal is to route as
many pairs as possible by directed paths, subject to the paths being mutually
edge-disjoint.

Theorem 21.1 (Fortune, Hopcroft, and Wyllie [114]). Given a directed graph 𝐺 and
two terminal pairs (𝑠 1 , 𝑡1 ) and (𝑠 2 , 𝑡2 ), it is NP-complete to decide whether there are
two mutually edge-disjoint directed paths 𝑃1 and 𝑃2 where 𝑃1 connects 𝑠 1 to 𝑡1 and 𝑃2
connects 𝑠 2 to 𝑡2 .

Theorem 21.2. Unless 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃, for every fixed 𝜖 > 0 there is no (1/2 + 𝜖)-
approximation for Maximum Directed Edge-Disjoint Paths.

Proof. Take an instance of the two-pair directed disjoint paths problem and view
it as an instance of the maximization problem with two demand pairs. We can
assume without loss of generality that each of the given two pairs can be routed
individually, otherwise the problem is easy. If the two-pair instance is a yes
instance, then the optimum value is 2. If it is a no instance, then the optimum
value is at 1.
    A polynomial-time algorithm that always returns more than (1/2 + 𝜖) times
optimum would, on yes instances, return a solution of value strictly larger than
1, and hence of value 2. On no instances no solution of value 2 exists. Thus the
approximation algorithm would decide the NP-complete two-pair problem. 

21.2.1    Gap Amplification for Directed EDP
The preceding direct reduction gives only a factor-2 gap. A simple and beautiful
amplification due to Guruswami, Khanna, Rajaraman,  √ Shepherd, and Yannakakis
[137] turns the same two-pair hardness into a near- 𝑛 inapproximability result
for Max-EDP, where 𝑛 is the number of vertices in the final directed graph. The
