each bent route 𝑃𝑖 from 𝑠 𝑖 to 𝑡 𝑖 , and a copy 𝐻 𝑖𝑗 at the intersection of routes 𝑃𝑖
and 𝑃 𝑗 . Private connector arcs, which concatenate the gadgets into the actual
𝑠 𝑖 -𝑡 𝑖 routes, are suppressed.


where two canonical demands can interact are their common crossing copies
𝐻 𝑖𝑗 . Figure 21.2 suppresses these private connectors, but each gray bent route
should be read as a directed corridor through the corresponding sequence of
boxes.
     Thus the construction has 𝑁 demand pairs and 𝑁2 copies of the two-pair
                                                          
gadget.
     The intuition is now simple. If 𝐻 is a yes instance, every gadget can locally
pass the two canonical paths through edge-disjointly, so all 𝑁 canonical paths
in Figure 21.2 survive. If 𝐻 is a no instance, then no two demands can both
be routed. The reason is not planarity by itself, but the forced ordering of the
corridors: any two routed demands must pass through a common crossing box,
and inside that box they would give a forbidden two-pair routing of a copy of 𝐻.
Lemma 21.1. If the original two-pair instance 𝐻 is a yes instance, then all 𝑁 demands
in 𝐺 𝑁 can be routed edge-disjointly. If 𝐻 is a no instance, then at most one demand in
𝐺 𝑁 can be routed.
Proof. Suppose first that 𝐻 has edge-disjoint paths 𝑄 1 from 𝑥1 to 𝑦1 and 𝑄 2 from
𝑥 2 to 𝑦2 . In every copy 𝐻 𝑖𝑗 , route demand 𝑖 through the copy of 𝑄1 and demand
𝑗 through the copy of 𝑄 2 . Concatenating these local routings with the private
connector arcs gives a routing of all 𝑁 demands. The only shared gadgets are
the copies 𝐻 𝑖𝑗 , and inside each such copy the two paths used are edge-disjoint.
     Conversely, suppose 𝐻 is a no instance and two demands are both routed
in 𝐺 𝑁 . Say they are demands 𝑖 and 𝑗, with 𝑖 < 𝑗. Let 𝑄 𝑖 and 𝑄 𝑗 be their paths.
Suppose we shrink each of the 𝐻-gadget graphs to a single node. Then we will
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                          321

obtain paths 𝑄 0𝑖 and 𝑄 0𝑗 in the skeleton graph that connect the pairs and are
edge-disjoint. These paths, by our earlier observation, have to cross at some
internal vertex 𝑣 of the skeleton. This implies that 𝑄 𝑖 and 𝑄 𝑗 have to traverse
the gadget graph 𝐻 at 𝑣 and this is only possible if 𝐻 is a yes instances — this is
an informal proof sketch and formalizing it requires a bit more work but does
not offer much more insight.                                                     
Theorem 21.3 (Guruswami et al. [137]). Let 𝑛 be the number of vertices. For
every fixed 𝜖 > 0, unless 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃, directed Max-EDP has no polynomial-time
𝑛 −1/2+𝜖 -approximation.
Proof. Let 𝑞 be the size of the two-pair instance 𝐻. The construction above uses
 𝑁
 2  copies of 𝐻 and 𝑂(𝑁 2 ) private connector arcs. Thus the number of vertices
in 𝐺 𝑁 is
                                   𝑛 = 𝑂(𝑁 2 𝑞).
The number of arcs is also 𝑂(𝑁 2 𝑞); this is the sparsity point in the Guruswami
et al. construction. We state the hardness in terms of 𝑛. In the yes case
OPT(𝐺 𝑁 ) = 𝑁, while in the no case OPT(𝐺 𝑁 ) ≤ 1.
    Choose 𝑁 = 𝑞 𝑐 for a constant 𝑐 to be fixed. Then 𝑛 = 𝑂(𝑞 2𝑐+1 ). For any fixed
𝜖 > 0, by choosing 𝑐 large enough we have

                               𝑞 𝑐 > 𝐶 𝑞 (2𝑐+1)(1/2−𝜖)

for the constant 𝐶 hidden in the 𝑂(·) notation and all sufficiently large 𝑞.
Equivalently, in the yes case 𝑁 > 𝑛 1/2−𝜖 .
    Therefore an 𝑛 −1/2+𝜖 -approximation algorithm would route more than one
demand in the yes case, since it would return a solution of value at least

                             OPT(𝐺 𝑁 ) · 𝑛 −1/2+𝜖 > 1.

In the no case it could route at most one demand. This would decide the
NP-complete two-pair directed EDP problem.                            
Undirected graphs. There is an important contrast between the exact and
approximate versions of the undirected problem. If the input has 𝑘 demand
pairs and the question is whether all 𝑘 pairs can be routed by edge-disjoint paths
in an undirected graph, then the problem is fixed parameter tractable in 𝑘; in
particular, it is polynomial-time solvable for each fixed 𝑘. This follows from
the Robertson–Seymour algorithm for the fixed-pair disjoint paths problem
[225]; the edge-disjoint version can be reduced to the vertex-disjoint version by
standard transformations.
    This does not imply good approximability when 𝑘 is part of the input. For
Max-EDP in undirected graphs, the strongest hardness results are much more
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                                         322

recent than the directed reduction above. Chuzhoy, Kim, and Nimavat [88]
proved
   √ that undirected Max-EDP is hard to approximate within a reciprocal factor
2Ω( log 𝑛) , unless all problems in NP have quasi-polynomial-time algorithms,
that is, unless
                              NP ⊆ DTIME(𝑛 𝑂(log 𝑛) ).
In the approximation-ratio √
                           convention used in these notes, this says that there
is no polynomial-time 2−Ω( log 𝑛) -approximation under the same assumption.
Their result holds even for subcubic planar graphs with all source vertices on
the boundary of one face.


21.3      Gap Amplification for Max-Clique
Graph products give a clean way to amplify a multiplicative gap for some
problems — this can be viewed as a self-reduction. We describe the idea for
Max-Clique. The same discussion also applies to Max-Independent-Set in
general graphs: an independent set in 𝐺 is exactly a clique in the complement
graph 𝐺, and hence
                              𝛼(𝐺) = 𝜔(𝐺).
Thus Max-Clique and Max-Independent-Set are equivalent for approximation
on unrestricted graphs.
    We first define the product for two graphs. Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) and 𝐻 = (𝑊 , 𝐹) be
undirected graphs. The strong product 𝐺  𝐻 has vertex set 𝑉 × 𝑊. Two distinct
vertices (𝑢, 𝑥) and (𝑣, 𝑦) are adjacent in 𝐺  𝐻 if

                    𝑢 = 𝑣 or 𝑢𝑣 ∈ 𝐸,           and       𝑥 = 𝑦 or 𝑥 𝑦 ∈ 𝐹.

Thus adjacency has to hold coordinate by coordinate, where equality is allowed
in a coordinate (at least one coordinate has to differ since they are distinct
vertices). Figure 21.3 shows the product 𝐾 2  𝑃3 .
    For one graph 𝐺, define the 𝑡-fold strong power by 𝐺1 = 𝐺 and

                                      𝐺(𝑡+1) = 𝐺 𝑡  𝐺.

Equivalently, the vertices of 𝐺 𝑡 are 𝑡-tuples from 𝑉 𝑡 , and two distinct tuples
𝑥 = (𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑡 ) and 𝑦 = (𝑦1 , . . . , 𝑦𝑡 ) are adjacent if, for every coordinate 𝑖, either
𝑥 𝑖 = 𝑦 𝑖 or 𝑥 𝑖 𝑦 𝑖 ∈ 𝐸.

Lemma 21.2. For every graph 𝐺 and integer 𝑡 ≥ 1,

                                       𝜔(𝐺 𝑡 ) = 𝜔(𝐺)𝑡 .
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                               323

                                                                   𝐺𝐻
                𝐺 = 𝐾2                   𝐻 = 𝑃3
                  𝑎                                          (𝑎, 𝑥) (𝑎, 𝑦) (𝑎, 𝑧)
                                     𝑥     𝑦      𝑧
                  𝑏                                          (𝑏, 𝑥) (𝑏, 𝑦) (𝑏, 𝑧)


Figure 21.3: The strong product 𝐾 2  𝑃3 . Horizontal edges come from 𝑃3 , vertical
edges come from 𝐾 2 , and diagonal edges come from moving in both coordinates
at once.


Proof. If 𝐶 is a clique in 𝐺, then 𝐶 𝑡 is a clique in 𝐺 𝑡 , so 𝜔(𝐺𝑡 ) ≥ 𝜔(𝐺)𝑡 .
    Conversely, let 𝑄 be a clique in 𝐺 𝑡 . For each coordinate 𝑖, let 𝑄 𝑖 be the set of
vertices of 𝐺 that appear as the 𝑖th coordinate of some tuple in 𝑄. The definition
of the strong product implies that each 𝑄 𝑖 is a clique in 𝐺: if two different
vertices 𝑢, 𝑣 appear in coordinate 𝑖 among tuples in 𝑄, the corresponding tuples
are adjacent, and hence 𝑢𝑣 ∈ 𝐸. Therefore |𝑄 𝑖 | ≤ 𝜔(𝐺) for every 𝑖. Since
𝑄 ⊆ 𝑄 1 × · · · × 𝑄 𝑡 , we have |𝑄| ≤ 𝜔(𝐺)𝑡 .                                         
    Suppose a reduction produces graphs for which it is NP-hard to distinguish

                           𝜔(𝐺) ≥ 𝑎         from      𝜔(𝐺) ≤ 𝑏,

where 𝑎 > 𝑏. Applying the strong power gives a polynomial-time reduction for
each fixed 𝑡 that distinguishes

                         𝜔(𝐺𝑡 ) ≥ 𝑎 𝑡      from      𝜔(𝐺 𝑡 ) ≤ 𝑏 𝑡 .

Thus a constant-factor gap can be raised to an arbitrarily large constant-factor
gap by choosing 𝑡 to be a sufficiently large constant. The price is that the number
of vertices grows from 𝑛 to 𝑛 𝑡 .
    One can push this deterministic powering further if one is willing to make a
stronger complexity assumption. If we choose 𝑡 = 𝑐 log 𝑛, then the output graph
has
                                  𝑛 𝑡 = 2𝑂((log 𝑛) )
                                                  2



vertices, so the reduction is quasi-polynomial rather than polynomial. The gap,
however, becomes
                                  (𝑎/𝑏)𝑡 = 𝑛 Ω(1)
for a suitable constant 𝑐. Thus graph powering alone gives polynomial-factor
hardness for Max-Clique under the assumption that NP is not contained in
quasi-polynomial time, for example

                                 NP * DTIME(𝑛 𝑂(log 𝑛) ).
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                              324

   This simple powering argument should be compared with the much stronger
known hardness for Max-Clique: for every fixed 𝜖 > 0, no polynomial-time
algorithm can achieve an 𝑛 −1+𝜖 -approximation for Max-Clique unless 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃
[144, 262]. Proving such a near-polynomial gap requires PCP machinery and
additional amplification ideas. The graph-powering argument above is the clean
algebraic core that turns a base gap into a larger one.

21.3.1   Randomized graph products
The deterministic strong product has one drawback: the graph size grows from
𝑛 to 𝑛 𝑡 . Randomized graph products reduce this blow-up by sampling from
the product graph. This idea was introduced in the early hardness work on
Max-Clique and later derandomized by Alon, Feige, Wigderson, and Zuckerman
[12].
    Here is the basic construction. Let 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) be a graph on 𝑛 vertices, and
choose parameters 𝑡 and 𝑀 with 𝑀 ≤ 𝑛 𝑡 . Form the full strong power 𝐺 𝑡 , whose
vertex set is 𝑉 𝑡 , but keep only a random sample. Let 𝑆 be a uniformly random
𝑀-element subset of 𝑉 𝑡 , and define

                                     𝑅 = 𝐺 𝑡 [𝑆],

the subgraph induced by 𝑆. We call 𝑅 the sampled product. If 𝑋 = (𝑥 1 , . . . , 𝑥 𝑡 )
and 𝑌 = (𝑦1 , . . . , 𝑦𝑡 ) are sampled tuples, then 𝑋 and 𝑌 are adjacent in 𝑅 iff, for
every coordinate 𝑖, either 𝑥 𝑖 = 𝑦 𝑖 or 𝑥 𝑖 𝑦 𝑖 ∈ 𝐸.
    The sampled product is useful when the original reduction gives a promise
gap in which the optimum clique size is a constant fraction of the number of
vertices:
                           𝜔(𝐺) ≥ 𝛼𝑛     versus      𝜔(𝐺) ≤ 𝛽𝑛,
where 0 < 𝛽 < 𝛼 ≤ 1 are constants. We use “with high probability” to mean
probability tending to one as 𝑛 → ∞. The two cases behave as follows.
Lemma 21.3 (Completeness of the sampled product). If 𝜔(𝐺) ≥ 𝛼𝑛, then with
high probability
                                   1
                             𝜔(𝑅) ≥ 𝛼 𝑡 𝑀.
                                   2
Proof idea. Let 𝐶 be a clique in 𝐺 of size at least 𝛼𝑛. Then 𝐶 𝑡 is a clique in 𝐺 𝑡 of
size at least 𝛼 𝑡 𝑛 𝑡 . The random set 𝑆 keeps about an 𝑀/𝑛 𝑡 fraction of the vertices
of 𝐶 𝑡 , so
                                                 |𝐶 𝑡 |
                              𝔼[|𝑆 ∩ 𝐶 𝑡 |] = 𝑀 · 𝑡 ≥ 𝛼 𝑡 𝑀.
                                                  𝑛
One can use concentration bounds for sampling without replacement to show
that the probability that we will get less than 1/2 the expected value is small. 
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                               325

Lemma 21.4 (Witnesses for product cliques). If 𝑄 is a clique in 𝐺 𝑡 , then there are
cliques 𝐶1 , . . . , 𝐶𝑡 in 𝐺 such that

                               𝑄 ⊆ 𝐶1 × 𝐶2 × · · · × 𝐶 𝑡 .

In particular, if 𝜔(𝐺) ≤ 𝛽𝑛, then every clique in 𝐺𝑡 is contained in a product set of
size at most (𝛽𝑛)𝑡 .
Proof. Let 𝐶 𝑖 be the set of vertices that appear in the 𝑖th coordinate of tuples in
𝑄. If 𝑢, 𝑣 ∈ 𝐶 𝑖 , then they are the 𝑖th coordinates of two tuples in 𝑄. Since those
tuples are adjacent in the strong product, either 𝑢 = 𝑣 or 𝑢𝑣 ∈ 𝐸(𝐺). Thus 𝐶 𝑖 is
a clique in 𝐺, and clearly 𝑄 ⊆ 𝐶1 × · · · × 𝐶𝑡 .                                   
Lemma 21.5 (Soundness of the sampled product). Fix constants 0 < 𝛽 < 𝜏 < 1.
There are constants 𝑐0 , 𝑐1 > 0, depending on 𝛽 and 𝜏, such that the following holds. Let
𝑡 = 𝑐 0 log 𝑛 and 𝑀 = 𝑛 𝑐1 . If 𝜔(𝐺) ≤ 𝛽𝑛, then with high probability

                                     𝜔(𝑅) ≤ 𝜏𝑡 𝑀.

Proof idea. By the witness lemma, any clique in 𝑅 is contained in 𝑆 ∩(𝐶1 ×· · ·× 𝐶𝑡 )
for some choice of cliques 𝐶1 , . . . , 𝐶𝑡 in 𝐺. In the no case, every such product
set has density at most 𝛽 𝑡 inside 𝑉 𝑡 . For any fixed product set, a concentration
bound can be used to show that the probability that the random set 𝑆 contains
more than 𝜏𝑡 𝑀 of its vertices, where 𝜏 > 𝛽, is exponentially small in 𝜏𝑡 𝑀. There
are at most 2𝑡𝑛 possible witnesses 𝐶1 , . . . , 𝐶𝑡 . Taking 𝑡 = 𝑐0 log 𝑛 and then
choosing 𝑐1 sufficiently large makes the union bound over these witnesses go to
zero while keeping 𝑀 = 𝑛 𝑐1 polynomial.                                           
    Choosing 𝜏 with 𝛽 < 𝜏 < 𝛼, the two lemmas give a formal gap amplification.
In the yes case,
                                        1
                                𝜔(𝑅) ≥ 𝛼 𝑡 𝑀,
                                        2
while in the no case,
                                 𝜔(𝑅) ≤ 𝜏𝑡 𝑀.
Thus the gap is at least
                              𝛼 𝑡 𝑀/2 1  𝛼  𝑡
                                         =       .
                                𝜏𝑡 𝑀       2 𝜏
Since 𝑡 = Θ(log 𝑛) and 𝑀 = 𝑛 𝑐1 , this is a polynomial gap in the size 𝑀 of the
sampled graph:
                                    𝛼 𝑡
                                          = 𝑀𝛾
                                     𝜏
for some constant 𝛾 > 0. The randomized product is therefore more powerful
than the full strong power for reductions: it keeps a polynomial-size random
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                          326

induced subgraph of the product while preserving many vertices from the
completeness clique and suppressing large soundness cliques with high proba-
bility. The derandomized graph product replaces the fully random subset 𝑆 by
a structured expander-walk sample space, removing the need for randomness
in the reduction.
    Graph products also play a role in hardness for graph coloring, but the
details are more delicate because the completeness case must preserve small
colorability; see the derandomized product ideas of Alon, Feige, Wigderson,
and Zuckerman [12] and later coloring hardness work for precise statements.


21.4    APX-Hardness of Max-3-SAT from PCP Theorem
We now turn to the source of constant-factor hardness for many optimization
problems. The class APX consists of optimization problems that admit a
polynomial-time constant-factor approximation. A problem is APX-hard if every
problem in APX has an approximation-preserving reduction to it. In particular,
if an APX-hard problem has a PTAS, then every problem in APX has a PTAS; for
the problems here this would imply 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃. The gap reductions below show
the concrete consequence we need: a PTAS for Max-3-SAT would imply 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃.
    The PCP theorem is best thought of as saying that witnesses for NP statements
can be rewritten in a special proof format. The verifier does not read the whole
proof. Instead it tosses a logarithmic number of random bits, looks at only
constantly many locations of this rewritten proof, and accepts or rejects according
to a constant-size local test.
    Let PCP(𝑂(log 𝑛), 𝑂(1)) denote the class of languages 𝐿 with the following
kind of verifier. There are constants 𝑐 and 𝑞 and a polynomial-time verifier 𝑉
such that, on input 𝑥, the verifier receives a proof string 𝑦, uses a random string
𝑟 of length 𝑐 log |𝑥|, queries at most 𝑞 bits of 𝑦, where 𝑞 = 𝑂(1), and then accepts
or rejects. The verifier has completeness 1 and soundness error < 1/2:

   1. if 𝑥 ∈ 𝐿, then there is a proof 𝑦 that makes 𝑉 accept with probability 1 over
      the choice of 𝑟;

   2. if 𝑥 ∉ 𝐿, then every proof 𝑦 makes 𝑉 accept with probability < 1/2 over
      the choice of 𝑟.

Theorem 21.4 (PCP theorem [20]). NP = PCP(𝑂(log 𝑛), 𝑂(1)).

    We will only use the theorem for 𝐿 = SAT. Thus there is a verifier for SAT
with constants 𝑐 and 𝑞 as above. This is much stronger than the elementary
verifier that chooses one random clause and checks the three variables in that
clause: that verifier has error roughly 1 − 1/𝑚 on an unsatisfiable formula with
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                                  327

𝑚 clauses. The PCP theorem gives a different proof format in which the error is
bounded away from 1, even though the verifier still reads only constantly many
proof bits.
     We first turn this verifier into a constraint satisfaction problem. For a
fixed constant 𝑞, an instance of Boolean Max-𝑞-CSP has Boolean variables and
constraints, where each constraint is an arbitrary Boolean predicate on at most 𝑞
variables. The objective is to maximize the number of satisfied constraints.
     Let Φ be a SAT formula of length 𝑛. The PCP theorem gives us a particular
polynomial-time verifier 𝑉 for SAT; it is part of the reduction, not an object
we search for. On input Φ, the verifier uses 𝑐 log 𝑛 random bits. Hence there
are only 𝑛 𝑐 possible random strings, and the reduction can enumerate all of
them in polynomial time. We may also assume, after increasing the constant
𝑐 if necessary, that the relevant proof has length at most 𝑛 𝑐 . Indeed, over all
random strings the verifier makes at most 𝑞𝑛 𝑐 queries, so only polynomially
many proof positions can ever be seen; all other positions are irrelevant and the
queried positions can be relabeled.
     Fix one random string 𝑟. Once Φ and 𝑟 are fixed, the verifier decides which
proof locations it will inspect, at most 𝑞 of them, and it accepts or rejects as a
Boolean function of the bits in those locations. Call this accepting predicate 𝑓𝑟 .
The CSP variables are the proof bits 𝑦1 , . . . , 𝑦 𝑁 , where 𝑁 ≤ 𝑛 𝑐 . For each random
string 𝑟, add the constraint 𝑓𝑟 on the proof bits queried by 𝑉(Φ, ·; 𝑟). Thus the
CSP is asking whether there is a proof string 𝑦 of length at most 𝑛 𝑐 such that
the verifier accepts Φ for the corresponding random strings. More precisely, the
value of the CSP assignment 𝑦 is exactly the number of random strings 𝑟 on
which 𝑉 accepts when it is given proof oracle 𝑦.
     This gives a Boolean Max-𝑞-CSP instance with 𝑛 𝑐 constraints. The key point
is that 𝑞 = 𝑂(1): the arity of every constraint is bounded by an absolute constant
independent of Φ.

Lemma 21.6. There is a constant 𝜂 > 0 and a polynomial-time reduction from SAT to
Boolean Max-𝑞-CSP, for some constant 𝑞, that maps a formula Φ to a CSP instance
𝐼Φ such that: if Φ is satisfiable, then all constraints of 𝐼Φ can be satisfied; if Φ is not
satisfiable, then every assignment satisfies at most a (1 − 𝜂) fraction of the constraints.

Proof. Use the construction above. If Φ is satisfiable, completeness of the PCP
verifier gives a proof string that makes 𝑉 accept for every random string 𝑟. The
corresponding assignment to the CSP variables satisfies every constraint 𝑓𝑟 .
    If Φ is not satisfiable, soundness says that every proof string makes 𝑉
accept with probability less than 1/2 over the random string 𝑟. Therefore every
assignment to the CSP variables satisfies fewer than half of the constraints. The
lemma follows with, for instance, 𝜂 = 1/2.                                      
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                               328

     We now convert this fixed-arity CSP gap to a Max-3-SAT gap. Consider one
CSP constraint 𝑓 on at most 𝑞 variables. Since 𝑞 is constant, we may write down
the whole truth table of 𝑓 . For every assignment on which 𝑓 evaluates to 0, add
one clause that forbids exactly that assignment. The conjunction of these clauses
is a CNF formula 𝜓 𝑓 equivalent to 𝑓 , and it has at most 2𝑞 clauses, each of width
at most 𝑞.
     If a CSP assignment satisfies 𝑓 , then it satisfies all clauses of 𝜓 𝑓 . If it does
not satisfy 𝑓 , then it violates at least one clause of 𝜓 𝑓 . Thus replacing every
CSP constraint by its CNF formula preserves perfect completeness, and in the
soundness case each violated CSP constraint creates at least one violated CNF
clause.
     The clauses in 𝜓 𝑓 may have width larger than 3, but their width is at most
the constant 𝑞. Replace each such clause by the standard chain of 3-clauses
using auxiliary variables. A satisfied long clause can be extended, by choosing
the auxiliary variables appropriately, to satisfy all clauses in the chain; an
unsatisfied long clause leaves at least one chain clause unsatisfied no matter how
the auxiliary variables are chosen. Clauses of width one or two can be padded
in the usual way, or simply allowed if one uses the convention that 3-CNF means
width at most three. Since 𝑞 is constant, each original CSP constraint produces
at most a constant number, say 𝐵, of 3-clauses.
     Combining the two steps, a satisfiable SAT instance produces a satisfiable
3-CNF formula. If the original SAT instance is unsatisfiable, then at least an
𝜂 fraction of the CSP constraints are violated by every assignment, and hence
at least an 𝜂/𝐵 fraction of the resulting 3-CNF clauses are violated by every
assignment.

Corollary 21.5. There is a constant 𝛿 > 0 such that it is NP-hard to distinguish
satisfiable 3-CNF formulas from 3-CNF formulas in which at most a (1 − 𝛿) fraction of
clauses can be satisfied. Consequently, unless 𝑃 = 𝑁𝑃, Max-3-SAT has no PTAS.

    Standard approximation-preserving reductions strengthen this statement
to the usual terminology: Max-3-SAT is APX-hard. This conclusion is much
weaker than the sharp inapproximability threshold known for Max-3-SAT,
but it captures the main conceptual point: the PCP theorem turns ordinary
NP-hardness into a constant multiplicative gap.

21.4.1   From Max-3-SAT to Max-Independent-Set
We next use the standard reduction from 3-CNF satisfiability to Independent-Set,
due to Karp [171], and observe that it preserves the number of clauses that can
be satisfied. Let Φ be a 3-CNF formula with 𝑚 clauses. For simplicity assume
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                           329

                       𝐶1 = (𝑥 ∨ 𝑦¯ ∨ 𝑧)             𝐶2 = ( 𝑥¯ ∨ 𝑦 ∨ 𝑤)


                      𝑥                𝑧             𝑥¯              𝑤

                              𝑦¯                             𝑦


                          dashed edges join complementary literals


Figure 21.4: The standard reduction from 3-CNF formulas to Independent-Set:
each clause becomes a triangle, and complementary literals in different clauses
are joined by consistency edges.


that each clause has exactly three literals; shorter clauses can be padded, or
handled by using a smaller clique.
    Build the graph 𝐻Φ as follows. For each literal occurrence in each clause,
create one vertex. For each clause, make its three vertices pairwise adjacent;
equivalently, create a triangle for each clause. These edges ensure that an
independent set chooses at most one literal from any clause. Finally, add
consistency edges between vertices from different clauses when they correspond
to complementary literals, say 𝑥 and 𝑥.
                                      ¯ Figure 21.4 shows the construction on a
two-clause example.

Lemma 21.7. There is a constant 𝛿 > 0 and a polynomial-time reduction from SAT
to Max-Independent-Set that maps a SAT instance 𝐼 to a graph 𝐻 and an integer
𝑀 such that, if 𝐼 is satisfiable, then 𝛼(𝐻) = 𝑀, while if 𝐼 is not satisfiable, then
𝛼(𝐻) ≤ (1 − 𝛿)𝑀. Moreover, |𝑉(𝐻)| = 3𝑀.

Proof. Apply the PCP-based reduction from SAT to Max-3-SAT from the preced-
ing subsection. It produces a 3-CNF formula Φ with 𝑀 clauses such that, in
the YES case, all 𝑀 clauses of Φ can be satisfied, while in the NO case, at most
(1 − 𝛿)𝑀 clauses can be satisfied.
    Now build the graph 𝐻Φ described above and illustrated in Figure 21.4.
An independent set in 𝐻Φ chooses at most one literal from each clause, and
the consistency edges ensure that it never chooses both 𝑥 and 𝑥.     ¯ Hence an
independent set of size 𝑠 can be extended to a truth assignment satisfying at least
the 𝑠 clauses from which those literals were chosen: set the chosen literals to
true and assign all remaining variables arbitrarily. Conversely, any assignment
satisfying 𝑠 clauses gives an independent set of size 𝑠, by choosing one true
literal from each satisfied clause. Thus

                                    𝛼(𝐻Φ ) = OPT(Φ),
CHAPTER 21. HARDNESS OF APPROXIMATION                                       330

where OPT(Φ) is the maximum number of satisfiable clauses. The claimed gap
follows, and 𝐻Φ has exactly three vertices for each clause of Φ.         
    Equivalently, by taking complements, the same reduction gives a constant
multiplicative gap for Max-Clique: the clique number is 𝑀 in the YES case and
at most (1 − 𝛿)𝑀 in the NO case. The randomized graph products from the
previous section can then amplify this constant gap while keeping the graph size
polynomial, yielding hardness for some polynomially small approximation ratio
for Max-Clique, and hence also for Max-Independent-Set on general graphs.
Equivalently, in the reciprocal terminology often used for clique hardness, this
rules out approximation within a polynomial factor.

Appendix A

Basic Feasible Solutions to LPs
and the Rank Lemma

We discuss the rank lemma about vertex solutions for linear programs. Recall
that a polyhedron in ℝ 𝑛 is defined as the intersection of finite collection of half
spaces. Without loss of generality we can assume that it is defined by a system
of inequalities of the form 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏 where 𝐴 is a 𝑚 × 𝑛 matrix and 𝑏 is a 𝑚 × 1
vector. A polyhedron 𝑃 is bounded if 𝑃 is contained in finite radius ball around
the origin. A polytope in ℝ 𝑛 is defined as the convex hull of a finite collection
of points. A fundamental theorem about linear programming states that any
bounded polyhedron is a polytope. If the polyhedron is not bounded then it can
be expressed as the Minkowski sum of a polytope and a cone.
    A bounded polyhedron 𝑃 in ℝ 𝑛 defined by a system 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏 must necessarily
have 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛. A point 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 is a basic feasible solution or a vertex solution of the
system if it is the unique solution to a system 𝐴0 𝑦 = 𝑏 0 where 𝐴0 is a sub-matrix
of 𝐴 with 𝑛 inequalities and the rank of 𝐴0 is equal to 𝑛. The inequalities in
𝐴0 are said to be tight for 𝑦. Note that there may be many other inequalities in
𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏 that are tight at 𝑦 and in general there may be many different rank 𝑛
sub-matrices that give rise to the same basic feasible solution 𝑦.

Lemma A.1. Suppose 𝑦 is a basic feasible solution of a system 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏, ℓ ≤ 𝑥 ≤ 𝑢
where 𝐴 is a 𝑚 × 𝑛 matrix and ℓ and 𝑢 are vectors defining lower and upper bounds
on the variables 𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 . Let 𝑆 = {𝑖 : ℓ 𝑖 < 𝑦 𝑖 < 𝑢𝑖 } be the set of indices of “fractional”
variables in 𝑦. Then |𝑆| ≤ 𝑟 𝑎𝑛 𝑘(𝐴) ≤ 𝑚. In particular the number of fractional
variables in 𝑦 is at most the number of “non-trivial” constraints (those that are defined
by 𝐴).

    An extension of the previous lemma is often useful when the system defining
the polyhedron has equality constraints.


                                            354
APPENDIX A. BASIC FEASIBLE SOLUTIONS TO LPS AND THE RANK LEMMA355

Corollary A.1. Suppose 𝑦 is a basic feasible solution of a system 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏, 𝐶𝑥 = 𝑑, ℓ ≤
𝑥 ≤ 𝑢 where 𝐴 is a 𝑚 × 𝑛 matrix, 𝐶 is a 𝑚 0 × 𝑛 matrix, and ℓ and 𝑢 are vectors defining
lower and upper bounds on the variables 𝑥 ∈ ℝ 𝑛 . Let 𝑆 = {𝑖 : ℓ 𝑖 < 𝑦 𝑖 < 𝑢𝑖 } be the set
of indices of “fractional” variables in 𝑦. Then |𝑆| ≤ 𝑟 𝑎𝑛 𝑘(𝐴, 𝐶) ≤ 𝑚 + 𝑚 0.
     A special case of the preceding corollary is called the rank lemma in [185].
     The lemmas are a simple consequence of the definition of basic feasible
solution. We will focus on the proof of Lemma A.1. It is interesting only when
𝑟𝑎𝑛 𝑘(𝐴) or 𝑚 is smaller than 𝑛, otherwise the claim is trivial. Before we prove
it formally we observe some simple corollaries. Suppose we have a system
𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏, 𝑥 ≥ 0 where 𝑚 < 𝑛. Then the number of non-zero variables in a basic
feasible solution is at most 𝑚. Similarly if the system is 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏, 𝑥 ∈ [0, 1]𝑛
then the number of non-integer variables in 𝑦 is at most 𝑚. For example in the
knapsack LP we have 𝑚 = 1 and hence in any basic feasible solution there can
only be one fractional variable.
     Now for the proof. We consider the system 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏, −𝑥 ≤ −ℓ , 𝑥 ≤ 𝑢 as a
single system 𝐶𝑥 ≤ 𝑑 which has 𝑚 + 2𝑛 inequalities. Since 𝑦 is a basic feasible
solution to this system, from the definition, it is the unique solution of sub-system
𝐶 0 𝑥 = 𝑑0 where 𝐶 0 is a 𝑛 × 𝑛 full-rank matrix. How many rows of 𝐶 0 can come
from 𝐴? At most 𝑟 𝑎𝑛 𝑘(𝐴) ≤ 𝑚 rows. It means that the rest of the rows of 𝐶 0 are
from the other set of inequalities −𝑥 ≤ ℓ or 𝑥 ≤ 𝑢. There are at least 𝑛 − 𝑟 𝑎𝑛 𝑘(𝐴)
such rows which are tight at 𝑦. Thus 𝑛 − 𝑟 𝑎𝑛 𝑘(𝐴) variables in 𝑦 are tight at lower
or upper bounds and hence there can only be 𝑟 𝑎𝑛 𝑘(𝐴) fractional variables in 𝑦.
     See [185] for iterated rounding based methodology for exact and approxima-
tion algorithms. The whole methodology relies on properties of basic feasible
solutions to LP relaxations of combinatorial optimization problems.

A.0.1    Some Examples
We give some examples to illustrate the utility of the rank lemma in the context
of LP relaxations that arise in approximation algorithms.
Knapsack: The natural LP relaxation for this of the form max 𝑛𝑖=1 𝑤 𝑖 𝑥 𝑖 subject
                                                                      Í
to 𝑥 ∈ [0, 1]𝑛 , 𝑛𝑖=1 𝑠 𝑖 𝑥 𝑖 ≤ 1 consisting of a single non-trivial constraint. A basic
                Í
feasible solution has at most 1 fractional variable. See Chapter 3.
Packing Integer Programs (PIPs): The LP relaxation is of the form max 𝑤𝑥
subject to 𝐴𝑥 ≤ 𝑏, 𝑥 ∈ [0, 1]𝑛 where 𝐴 is a 𝑚 × 𝑛 non-negative matrix. See
Chapter 4. When 𝑚 = 1 we have the Knapsack problem and hence the general
problem is some-times referred to as the 𝑚-dimensional Knapsack problem,
especially when 𝑚 is a fixed constant. A basic feasible solution for the LP has
at most 𝑚 fractional variables. When 𝑚 is a fixed constant one can exploit this
after guessing the big items to obtain a PTAS.
APPENDIX A. BASIC FEASIBLE SOLUTIONS TO LPS AND THE RANK LEMMA356

Generalized Assignment: See Chapter 6.

A.0.2    Connection to Caratheodary’s Theorem
Suppose we have 𝑛 points 𝑃 = {𝑝 1 , 𝑝2 , . . . , 𝑝 𝑛 } in 𝑑-dimensional Euclidean space
ℝ 𝑑 . A point 𝑝 ∈ ℝ 𝑑 is in the convex hull of 𝑃 iff 𝑝 is a convex combination of
points in 𝑃. Formally, this means that there exist scalars 𝜆1 , . . . , 𝜆𝑛 ≥ 0 such
that 𝑖 𝜆 𝑖 = 1 and 𝑝 = 𝑖 𝜆 𝑖 𝑝 𝑖 (note that this is a vector sum). Caratheordary’s
      Í                      Í
theorem states that if 𝑝 is in the convex hull of 𝑃 then there is subset 𝑃 0 ⊆ 𝑃 such
that 𝑝 is in the convex hull of 𝑃 0 and |𝑃 0 | ≤ 𝑑 + 1. One can prove Caratheodary’s
theorem directly but it is helpful to see it also as a consequence of the rank lemma.
Consider the system of inequalities 𝜆 𝑖 ≥ 0, 1 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑛, 𝑖 𝜆 𝑖 = 1, 𝑖 𝜆 𝑖 𝑝 𝑖 = 𝑝
                                                                 Í           Í
where the system 𝑖 𝜆 𝑖 𝑝 𝑖 = 𝑝 consists of 𝑑 equalities. This system of inequalities
                    Í
in the variables 𝜆1 , . . . , 𝜆𝑛 is feasible by assumption (since 𝑝 is in the convex hull
of 𝑃). If we take any basic feasible solution of this system of inequalities we see
that at most 𝑑 + 1 of them are non-zero by the rank lemma.
     One implication of Caratheordary’s theorem in the context of combinatorial
optimization is the following. Suppose we have a polytope 𝑃 which is an LP
relaxation of some combinatorial problem and let 𝑥 ∈ 𝑃 be any feasible point.
Then 𝑥 can be written as a convex combination of at most 𝑛 +1 vertices of 𝑃 where
𝑛 is number of variables. Moreover, via the Ellipsoid method, one can find such
a convex combination efficiently as long as one can optimize over 𝑃 efficiently.
As an example suppose 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) is a graph and 𝑃 is the spanning tree polytope
of 𝐺 (the vertices are the characteristic vectors of spanning trees) which is ℝ 𝑚
(𝑚 = |𝐸|). Then any fractional spanning tree 𝑥 ∈ 𝑃 can be decomposed into at
most 𝑚 + 1 spanning trees.
     In the context of approximation 𝑃 is typically a relaxation of some hard
combinatorial optimization problem. In such a case the vertices of 𝑃 do not
correspond to structures we are interested in. For example we can consider
the minimum Steiner tree problem in a graph 𝐺 = (𝑉 , 𝐸) with terminal set
𝑆 ⊆ 𝑉. There are several LP relaxations but perhaps the simplest one is the cut
relaxation which has an integrality gap of 2. In such a case a feasible point 𝑥 ∈ 𝑃
cannot be decomposed into convex combination of Steiner trees. However it can
be shown that 2𝑥 dominates a convex combination of Steiner trees and such a
convex combination can be found efficiently. It requires more technical work to
precisely formalize this and we refer the reader to the work of Carr and Vempala
[54] — you can also find a few applications of such decompositions in the same
paper and it is a simple yet powerful tool to keep in mind.
Appendix B

Probabilistic Inequalities

The course will rely heavily on probabilistic methods. We will mostly rely
on discrete probability spaces. We will keep the discussion high-level where
possible and use certain results in a black-box fashion.
    Let Ω be a finite set. A probability measure 𝑝 assigns a non-negative number
𝑝(𝜔) for each 𝜔 ∈ Ω such that 𝜔∈Ω 𝑝(𝜔) = 1. The tuple (Ω, 𝑝) defines a discrete
                                 Í
probability space; an event in this space is any subset 𝐴 ⊆ Ω and the probability
of an event is simply 𝑝(𝐴) = 𝜔∈𝐴 𝑝(𝜔). When Ω is a continuous space such as
                               Í
the interval [0, 1] things get trickier and we need to talk about measure spaces
𝜎-algebras over Ω; we can only assign probability to certain subsets of Ω. We
will not go into details since we will not need any formal machinery for what we
do in this course.
    An important definition is that of a random variable. We will focus only
on real-valued random variables in this course. A random variable 𝑋 in a
probability space is a function 𝑋 : Ω → ℝ. In the discrete setting the expectation
of 𝑋, denoted   by E[𝑋], is defined as 𝜔∈Ω 𝑝(𝜔)𝑋(𝜔). For continuous spaces
                                            Í
        ∫
E[𝑋] = 𝑋(𝜔)𝑑𝑝(𝜔) with appropriate definition of the integral. The variance
of 𝑋, denoted by Var[𝑋] or as 𝜎𝑋    2
                                                                  
                                      , is defined as E (𝑋 − E[𝑋])2 . The standard
deviation is 𝜎𝑋 , the square root of the variance.

Theorem B.1 (Markov’s Inequality). Let 𝑋 be a non-negative random variable such
that E𝑋 is finite. Then for any 𝑡 > 0, P[𝑋 ≥ 𝑡] ≤ E[𝑋]/𝑡.

Proof. The proof is in some sense obvious, especially in the discrete case. Here
is a sketch. Define a new random variable 𝑌 where 𝑌(𝜔) = 𝑋(𝜔) if 𝑋(𝜔) < 𝑡
and 𝑌(𝜔) = 𝑡 if 𝑋(𝜔) ≥ 𝑡. 𝑌 is non-negative and 𝑌 ≤ 𝑋 point-wise and hence

APPENDIX B. PROBABILISTIC INEQUALITIES                                                            358

E[𝑌] ≤ E[𝑋]. We also see that:

          E[𝑋] ≥ E[𝑌] =                     𝑋(𝜔)𝑝(𝜔) +                 𝑡𝑝(𝜔)
                                 𝜔:𝑋(𝜔)<𝑡                 𝜔:𝑋(𝜔)≥𝑡
                                     Õ
                            ≥ 𝑡               𝑝(𝜔)      (since 𝑋 is non-negative)
                                   𝜔:𝑋(𝜔)≥𝑡
                            ≥ 𝑡 P[𝑋 ≥ 𝑡].
The continuous case follows by replacing sums by integrals.                                        
   Markov’s inequality is tight under the assumption. It is useful to construct
an example. The more information we have about a random variable the better
we can bound its deviation from the expectation.
Theorem B.2 (Chebyshev’s Inequality).       𝑋 be a random variable with E[𝑋] and
                                        Let
Var[𝑋] are finite. Then P[|𝑋 | ≥ 𝑡] ≤ E 𝑋 2 /𝑡 2 and P[|𝑋 − E[𝑋]| ≥ 𝑡𝜎𝑋 ] ≤ 1/𝑡 2 .
Proof. Consider the non-negative random variable 𝑌 = 𝑋 2 . P[|𝑋 | ≥ 𝑡] = P[𝑌 ≥
𝑡 2 ] and we apply Markov’s inequality to the latter. The second inequality is
similar by considering 𝑌 = (𝑋 − E[𝑋])2 .                                     
Chernoff-Hoeffding Bounds: We will use several times various forms of the
Chernoff-Hoeffding bounds that apply to a random variable that is a finite sum
of bounded and independent random variables. There are several versions of
these bounds. First we state a general bound that is applicable to non-negative
random variables and is dimension-free in that it depends only the expectation
rather than the number of variables.
Theorem B.3 (Chernoff-Hoeffding). Let 𝑋1 , 𝑋2 , . . . , 𝑋𝑛 be independent binary
random variables and let 𝑎 1 , 𝑎2 , . . . , 𝑎 𝑛 be coefficients in [0, 1]. Let 𝑋 = 𝑖 𝑎 𝑖 𝑋𝑖 . Then
                                                                                  Í

   If we are interested only in the upper tail we also have the following bounds
which show the dependence of 𝜇 on 𝑛 to obtain an inverse polynomial probability.

Remark B.1. If the 𝑋𝑖 are in the range [0, 𝑏] for some 𝑏 not equal to 1 one can
scale them appropriately and then use the standard bounds.
   Some times we need to deal with random variables that are in the range
[−1, 1]. Consider the setting where 𝑋 = 𝑖 𝑋𝑖 where for each 𝑖, 𝑋𝑖 ∈ [−1, 1]

and −1 with probability 1/2. Then 𝑋 = 𝑖 𝑋𝑖 corresponds to a 1-dimensional

random walk and even though the expected value is 0 the standard deviation of

Remark B.2. Compare the Chebyshev bound to the Chernoff-Hoeffding bounds
for the same variance.
    Sariel Har-Peled maintains a cheat sheet of Chernoff bounds and also has an
interesting derivation. See his notes [143].
APPENDIX B. PROBABILISTIC INEQUALITIES                                          360

Statistical Estimators, Reducing Variance and Boosting: Randomized algo-
rithms compute a function 𝑓 of the input. In many cases they produce an
unbiased estimator, via a random variable 𝑋, for the function value. That is, the
algorithm will have the property that the E[𝑋] is the desired value. Note that
the randomness is internal to the algorithm and not part of the input (we can
also consider randomness in the input). Having an estimator is not often useful.
We will also typically try to evaluate Var[𝑋] and then we can use Chebyshev’s
inequality. One way to reduce the variance of the estimate is to run the algorithm
in parallel (with separate random bits) and get estimators 𝑋1 , 𝑋2 , . . . , 𝑋 ℎ and
use 𝑋 = 1ℎ 𝑖 𝑋𝑖 as the final estimator. Note that Var[𝑋] = 1ℎ 𝑖 Var[𝑋𝑖 ] since
            Í                                                     Í
the 𝑋𝑖 are independent. Thus the variance has been reduced by a factor of ℎ.
A different approach is to use the median value of 𝑋1 , 𝑋2 , . . . , 𝑋 ℎ as the final
estimator. We can then use Chernoff-Hoeffding bounds to get a much better
dependence on ℎ. In fact both approaches can be combined.
